# CodeHarbor bug-hunt round — 29 July 2026

Full record of a systematic review of the whole repository. Every problem found is
listed with a unique label, what was wrong, why, what was changed, and why it matters.
Labels are unique to this round only.

## 1. How the review was run

The repository holds two codebases that talk to each other:

- a **desktop client** written in C++ with the Qt 6 framework, under `src/`; and
- a **remote service** written in TypeScript for Node.js, under `remote/`, which runs on
  the SSH server and owns all the real state (files, terminal processes, tmux sessions,
  saved workspace definitions).

Two smaller TypeScript bundles under `src/web/` are web pages (an xterm.js terminal and a
Monaco code editor) that the desktop client loads inside an embedded browser and drives
over a message channel.

The review was done in two passes.

**Pass one** split the tree into 14 non-overlapping areas and reviewed each one in depth,
with a strict rule: only edit files inside your own area. Anything noticed outside the
area was written down and handed back rather than fixed on the spot, because 14 reviewers
editing one working copy at the same time would otherwise overwrite each other.

**Pass two** took those 44 handed-back items, matched each one to whoever owns the file it
actually lives in, and fixed them there. Pass two items are labelled `X1`…`X36`.

Nothing was reviewed by guessing: every area was read end to end, and every claim about
the specification was checked against `docs/SPEC.md`.

## 2. Totals

- **268 items** recorded in total: 232 in pass one, 36 in pass two.
- By kind:

  - Latent bug: 63
  - Footgun: 41
  - Active bug: 39
  - Documentation error: 33
  - Missing test: 29
  - Nit: 25
  - Inconsistency: 21
  - Simplification: 11
  - Lint: 6

- By outcome:

  - Fixed: 217
  - Not fixed (explained): 48
  - Uncertain: 3

The "not fixed (explained)" items are deliberate: each one is either a design trade-off
that was re-examined and kept (with the reasoning now written into the code), a claim that
turned out on inspection not to be a defect, or a change too large and too risky to make
blind. Every one of them says which, and why.

## 3. Verification

Before the review the tree was measured so any later failure could be attributed:

| Check | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| C++ build (`cmake --build --preset dev`) | clean | clean |
| C++ test suite, portable path (`ctest --preset dev`, no SSH server) | 33/33 pass, 1 skipped | 33/33 pass, 1 skipped |
| C++ test suite with the live gates armed (real `sshd`, real `tmux`, real Node.js) | 13/13 live gates pass | 13/13 live gates pass |
| Remote-service test suite (`cd remote && npm test`) | 106/106 pass | 136/136 pass |
| TypeScript type check (`npm run typecheck`, all three workspaces) | clean | clean |
| Release configuration (`cmake --preset release`, tests off) | configures | configures |

The single skipped C++ test is `tst_notifierlive`, which needs a live desktop notification
service; it reports Skipped rather than Passed by design.

The live gates are the important ones for this round: they stand up a throwaway SSH server
on localhost, start the real remote service over a real SSH channel, drive real tmux, load
the real editor and terminal web pages, and kill and restore the connection. They cover the
SSH channel-leak fix (SH1), the use-after-free fix (SH2), the crash-recovery fix (ED1) and
the reconnect fixes. They are skipped unless an environment variable arms them, so they do
not run in a normal build; they were armed and run for this review.

30 new remote-service tests and a number of new C++ tests were added; the remote-service
count going from 106 to 136 is those new tests.

### Two problems the verification itself caught

These were introduced by the review and fixed before finishing, and are recorded for honesty.

**V1 — a new test would not compile.** The new cross-language check added by `X8` used a C++
*raw string literal* (a form that lets you write backslashes without escaping them) as the
argument of a macro. Qt's meta-object compiler, a code generator that runs over test files
before the real compiler, has its own simplified parser that does not understand raw strings
in that position, and failed with `missing ')' in macro usage`. Rewritten using ordinary
escaped string literals, with a comment recording why.

**V2 — a pre-existing fragile assertion started failing.** The live test
`tst_liveeditorreconnect` proved that a reconnect really creates a new SSH channel by
comparing the memory address of the old channel object against the new one. The old object
is destroyed and the new one allocated immediately after, so the memory allocator is free to
hand the same address back — which it began doing once the SSH fixes changed the allocation
pattern. The test now uses a Qt *guarded pointer*, which becomes empty when the object it
watches is destroyed: the test asserts the old channel was genuinely destroyed and a new
non-null one exists. That is what the test always meant to check.

### One consistency gap closed during verification

**V3 — the documented minimum SSH library version was not enforced anywhere.** Pass two
(`X5`) established, symbol by symbol, that the real minimum libssh version is 0.11.2, and
the documentation was corrected. But the build accepted any version silently. A hard
requirement would refuse to configure on distributions still shipping 0.10.x, where the
client does build and work, so the build now prints an explicit warning naming the version,
the floor, and the two reasons for it. Verified by running the check against 0.11.3 (silent),
an unknown version (silent), 0.11.2 (silent), 0.10.6 (warns) and 0.9.6 (warns).

---

## 4. Pass one — findings by area

### 4.1 Remote service — workspace store and SQL schema (`RW`)

*Scope:* `remote/src/workspace.ts`, `remote/sql/schema.sql` and their tests. This is the SQLite-backed store on the SSH server that owns every group, Dev Session, viewer pane, terminal pane, pane layout, and the server's own identity.

*15 findings:* 5 latent bug, 4 footgun, 1 inconsistency, 1 active bug, 1 simplification, 1 missing test, 1 documentation error, 1 nit.

#### RW1 — setLayout stored the client-supplied serverId instead of the parent session's, breaking the "a row shares its ancestors' server" rule

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`, `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The server-side workspace store keeps a `server_id` column on every row so that multi-server support can be added later. When a pane is created (`createViewerPane`, `createTerminalPane`) the store deliberately IGNORES the `serverId` the client sent and reads the authoritative value from the parent Dev Session row instead, and there is an existing test asserting exactly that. `setLayout`, which stores a region's split-pane layout tree for a Dev Session, did not do this: it wrote `params.serverId` verbatim. A client that sent a wrong or stale serverId therefore created a layout row claiming to belong to a different server than the Dev Session it points at. It also accepted an unknown `devSessionId` up to the point where the database's foreign key rejected it, producing a raw "FOREIGN KEY constraint failed" message instead of the store's clear "dev_sessions not found: <id>".

**Why it was wrong.** setLayout was written before the "derive server_id from the parent row" rule was introduced for panes and sessions, and was never brought in line.

**What was done.** setLayout now calls the existing private helper `parentServerId("dev_sessions", params.devSessionId)` and stores that value, exactly as the two pane-creating methods do. The helper also throws a clear error for an unknown Dev Session before anything is written. The `serverId` parameter is still accepted on the wire (the Qt client sends it and a C++ test asserts the request carries exactly four parameters), it is just no longer trusted. A new test asserts the stored value comes from the parent even when the caller sends "srv-EVIL", and that an unknown Dev Session is rejected with the clear message.

**Why it matters.** Today only one server is configured, so no user-visible breakage; it was a latent hole in the invariant the rest of the store enforces, and the error message for a bad Dev Session id was needlessly cryptic.


#### RW2 — setLayout's read-then-write upsert could crash when two connections saved a layout at the same time

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`, `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** `setLayout` used to run a SELECT to find an existing layout row for the (Dev Session, region) pair, then branch into either an UPDATE or an INSERT. Two connections to the same database file (the project does start a second server process in one live test) can both run the SELECT, both see no row, and both INSERT; the database has a uniqueness constraint on (dev_session_id, region), so the second INSERT fails and the caller's layout save is lost with a constraint error.

**Why it was wrong.** A check-then-act sequence across two separate statements, with no transaction and no use of the uniqueness constraint that already exists in the schema.

**What was done.** Replaced the SELECT + branch with one atomic statement: `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (dev_session_id, region) DO UPDATE SET server_id = excluded.server_id, tree = excluded.tree, updated_at = excluded.updated_at`. This is a single statement, so it cannot interleave, and it is also simpler (one prepared statement instead of three). The update branch keeps the row's original primary key and `created_at`, so a layout row's identity survives every rewrite; a new test asserts the id and creation timestamp are stable across a second save and that only one row exists.

**Why it matters.** Removes a crash/lost-write window when two processes share the workspace database, and removes ten lines of branching.


#### RW3 — Reordering groups or sessions with an incomplete list left two rows sharing one position, making their displayed order random

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`, `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** `reorderGroups(serverId, orderedIds)` and `reorderSessions(groupId, orderedIds)` renumbered ONLY the rows named in `orderedIds`, assigning them 0, 1, 2, ... Any row in the same scope that the caller left out kept its old number. So if a group holds sessions A(0), B(1), C(2) and the client sends just [C], C becomes 0 and now ties with A at 0. Every read query sorts by `position, id`, so a tie is settled by comparing random UUIDs — the two rows can appear in either order, and the order can differ between reads on different machines. The same code path also silently accepted ids from a different group or a different server (the guard in the UPDATE's WHERE clause simply matched nothing, leaving a gap in the numbering) and accepted a repeated id (consuming a position number and leaving a gap).

**Why it was wrong.** The reorder methods treated the caller's list as the complete truth and only wrote the rows it mentioned, while the rest of the store depends on positions being a contiguous 0..n-1 sequence with no duplicates. The `moveSessionToGroup` method already had a long comment explaining exactly this hazard and re-packed properly; the two reorder methods never got the same treatment.

**What was done.** Both reorder methods now go through a new shared private helper `packOrder(table, scopeColumn, scopeValue, orderedIds, ts)`. It reads the scope's current order, keeps only the requested ids that really are in that scope (dropping foreign and unknown ids, collapsing repeats to their first occurrence), appends every row the caller omitted in its current relative order, and then renumbers the whole scope 0..n-1. `moveSessionToGroup` was switched to the same helper (it already passed a complete list, so its behaviour is unchanged), and the two now-redundant helpers `packSessions` and `orderedSessionIds` were replaced by `packOrder` and a generalised `orderedIds`. A new test drives a partial list, a repeated id, and an id from a sibling group, and asserts the scope is still packed 0..n-1 with the requested row first and the sibling group untouched.

**Why it matters.** A client that sent a filtered, paged, or stale ordering could permanently corrupt the sidebar's ordering into something that shuffles on every read. Now such a request is reconciled into a valid, fully packed order.


#### RW4 — Moving a Dev Session into a group belonging to another server left the session and its panes tagged with the old server

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`, `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** `moveSessionToGroup` updated the session's `group_id` but never its `server_id`, and never the `server_id` of its viewer panes, terminal panes, or layout rows. Moving a session into a group owned by a different server therefore produced rows that were listed under the target server's group while still reporting the old server on the row itself — the exact contradiction that the create paths go out of their way to prevent. Separately, an unknown target group id was only caught later by the database's foreign key, giving a raw "FOREIGN KEY constraint failed" error.

**Why it was wrong.** The move operation was written for the single-server case and only considered the position/ordering side of the problem, not the server ownership side.

**What was done.** `moveSessionToGroup` now resolves the target group's `server_id` up front with `parentServerId("groups", params.groupId)` — which also rejects an unknown target group with a clear message before any row is written — writes it onto the session together with the new group, and, only when the server actually changes, updates `server_id` on that session's viewer panes, terminal panes, and layout rows. All of it happens inside the existing single transaction, so a failure leaves nothing half-moved. A new test moves a session with a pane of each kind and a layout across two servers and asserts every child row followed, that each server's listing is coherent, and that a move to an unknown group throws without changing anything.

**Why it matters.** Not reachable today (version 1 assumes one configured server), but it was a data-integrity bug waiting for the multi-server feature the column exists for. Also improves the error message for a bad target group id.


#### RW5 — Partial updates from two connections could silently erase each other's changes

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** `updateGroup`, `updateSession`, `updateViewerPane`, and `updateTerminalPane` all work by reading the current row, filling in whichever fields the caller omitted, and then writing ALL columns back. None of them was wrapped in a transaction. With two connections open on the same database file, one call renaming a group and another collapsing it can each read the row before either writes; both then write every column from their own stale snapshot, and whichever writes second silently discards the other's change.

**Why it was wrong.** Read-modify-write over multiple statements with no transaction around it. The store's delete/reorder/duplicate operations were already transactional; the four update operations were not.

**What was done.** Each of the four methods now runs its read-and-write inside the store's `transaction()` helper, with a comment on `updateGroup` explaining the hazard and the other three pointing at it. See RW6 for the accompanying changes to `transaction()` that make this actually serialise writers and make nesting safe.

**Why it matters.** Concurrent edits from two server processes (or a future concurrent request path) can no longer silently drop a field change; the loser now fails loudly instead.


#### RW6 — The transaction helper used a deferred BEGIN, could not be nested, and its rollback could hide the real error

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** The private `transaction()` helper ran a plain `BEGIN`. In SQLite a plain BEGIN is *deferred*: it takes no write lock until the first write statement, so two connections can both begin, both read, and only then contend — which is precisely the read-modify-write window described in RW5. The helper was also not re-entrant: because SQLite rejects a nested BEGIN, calling one transactional method from inside another would throw, and the outer `catch` would then issue a ROLLBACK that aborted the *outer* transaction. Finally, if the failure had already aborted the transaction, the unguarded `ROLLBACK` in the catch block would itself throw and replace the original, informative error with "cannot rollback - no transaction is active".

**Why it was wrong.** The helper was written for the simple single-writer case and never revisited when more operations became transactional.

**What was done.** `transaction()` now (a) issues `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, which takes the write lock up front so two writers serialise and the loser fails loudly with a busy error instead of quietly overwriting; (b) tracks nesting with a private `txDepth` counter on the connection, so a nested call simply joins the outer transaction instead of issuing a second BEGIN — a nested failure still aborts the whole outermost transaction, which is what every caller wants; and (c) wraps the ROLLBACK in its own try/catch so the original error is always the one that propagates. Every caller is a writer, so `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` is correct for all of them. I re-ran the existing rollback scenario (corrupting a stored layout tree so that duplicating a session fails part way) against the rewritten helper and confirmed nothing partial survives.

**Why it matters.** Makes RW5's fix effective across processes, makes it safe for any future method to compose transactional helpers, and stops rollback failures from masking the real cause of an error.


#### RW7 — A schema migration that failed part way left the database in a half-upgraded state

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** The `migrate()` function applied each pending migration step and then recorded the new schema version, all outside any transaction. If a step failed half way (out of disk space, a partially applied ALTER in a future step), the earlier statements stayed committed while the recorded version stayed at the old value, so the next start-up would re-run steps against a partially changed database.

**Why it was wrong.** No transaction around the migration sequence.

**What was done.** The whole loop plus the version write now run inside one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` ... `COMMIT`, with a guarded ROLLBACK on failure (same reasoning as RW6). The comment notes that the leading `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON` inside schema.sql is a no-op inside a transaction and that `openWorkspace` already sets that pragma on the connection before migrating, so nothing is lost — I verified at runtime that foreign keys are still enforced after opening a fresh database (a raw group delete with a child session is still rejected).

**Why it matters.** An interrupted upgrade now leaves the database exactly as it was, instead of at a version no migration was written for. Today's only migration is a set of idempotent CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS statements, so this is protection for future structural steps rather than a fix for a live failure.


#### RW8 — A database written by a newer build was accepted silently instead of being refused

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`, `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** `migrate()` returned early whenever the stored schema version was greater than OR equal to the version this build targets. Equality is the normal case, but the "greater than" case means the file was written by a newer release whose migrations this build does not have — possibly with columns renamed or dropped. The old code used such a database as if it were compatible, so the mismatch surfaced later as scattered, confusing SQL errors during normal use.

**Why it was wrong.** A single `>=` comparison conflated "already up to date" with "from the future".

**What was done.** The early-return branch now throws `workspace database schema version <N> is newer than this build supports (<M>)` when the stored version is strictly greater, and still returns silently on equality. A new test rewinds nothing but bumps the stored version by one and asserts that reopening throws.

**Why it matters.** A downgraded or mixed-version deployment now fails immediately with an explanatory message instead of corrupting or mis-reading a newer workspace.


#### RW9 — Table and column names were interpolated into SQL through plain `string` parameters

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** The private helper `nextPosition(table: string, scopeColumn: string, scopeValue: string)` builds its query by string interpolation: `SELECT COALESCE(MAX(position), -1) AS m FROM ${table} WHERE ${scopeColumn} = ?`. SQL cannot bind a table or column name as a parameter, so interpolation is unavoidable here — but typing the parameters as bare `string` means nothing stops a future caller from passing a value that came from a request. Every current caller passes a hard-coded literal, so there is no injection today. The sibling helper `parentServerId` already had the right shape: its table parameter is a union of the two allowed literals.

**Why it was wrong.** The helper predates the literal-union convention that `parentServerId` established.

**What was done.** `nextPosition`'s parameters are now the literal unions actually used (`"groups" | "dev_sessions" | "viewer_panes" | "terminal_panes"` and `"server_id" | "group_id" | "dev_session_id"`), and the two new helpers added for RW3 (`orderedIds`, `packOrder`) use the same pattern, with a comment stating why: an identifier cannot be a bound parameter, so the type is the thing that keeps a caller-supplied name out of the query text. The only string still interpolated anywhere in this file is one of these literals.

**Why it matters.** Removes the only way a table/column name could ever reach the SQL text from outside; behaviour is unchanged.


#### RW10 — Duplicating a Dev Session re-compiled the same INSERT for every copied row, and copied each child's own server tag instead of the parent's

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** `duplicateSession` copies a Dev Session's viewer panes, terminal panes, and layout rows. Inside each of the three loops it called `db.prepare(...)` again for every row, re-compiling identical SQL and leaving one statement handle per row for the garbage collector to finalise. Each copied child row also took its `server_id` from the source CHILD row rather than from the new session, so any pre-existing mismatch (see RW1/RW4) would be faithfully duplicated instead of normalised.

**Why it was wrong.** The prepare call was written inline at the point of use inside the loop; the server tag was copied field-by-field along with everything else.

**What was done.** Each loop now prepares its INSERT once before the loop and reuses it, and all three copy `source.server_id` (the new session's own server) instead of each child row's. A comment records both reasons. Existing tests already cover the copied field values, fresh ids, fresh tmux targets, and layout remapping; I re-ran an equivalent duplicate-and-verify scenario by hand after the change.

**Why it matters.** Fewer redundant SQL compilations and no per-row statement handles when duplicating a session with many panes, and a duplicate always satisfies the server-ownership invariant even if the source data predates that rule.


#### RW11 — No tests covered partial reorder lists, layout-row ownership, cross-server moves, the newer-database guard, or the layout table's own constraints

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/workspace.test.ts`, `remote/test/schema.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The existing suite is thorough about the happy paths and about the undefined-versus-null rules, but nothing exercised: a reorder request whose id list is partial, repeated, or contains a foreign id (RW3); where a layout row's server tag comes from, or that a layout save reuses the same row (RW1, RW2); moving a Dev Session between two servers (RW4); a database claiming a newer schema version (RW8); or the two database-level constraints on the layout table that the store now leans on directly — the CHECK that limits `region` to 'viewer'/'terminal' (which is what makes it safe to key a two-slot object straight off the stored string) and the UNIQUE (dev_session_id, region) pair (which is the conflict target of the new upsert).

**Why it was wrong.** Each of these is either newly fixed behaviour or an invariant that was previously only implied.

**What was done.** Added four tests at the end of remote/test/workspace.test.ts (partial/stale reorder lists for both groups and sessions; layout ownership plus stable row identity plus unknown-session rejection; cross-server move of a session with panes and a layout plus unknown-target rejection; refusal of a newer-versioned database) and one test at the end of remote/test/schema.test.ts (the layout table admits one row per region and rejects an unknown region). All follow the neighbouring conventions: `node:test` + `node:assert/strict`, a temp-file database per test, `cleanup()` at the end, and the existing `mkSession`/`ordered` helpers. I executed every new test body verbatim outside the project test runner (I did not run any project build or test command) and all of them pass.

**Why it matters.** Each new test fails if the corresponding fix is reverted, so the behaviours cannot silently regress.


#### RW12 — schema.sql's `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON` line invited the reader to believe the file turns foreign keys on for everyone

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/sql/schema.sql`

**What was wrong.** The DDL file starts with `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;` with no comment. In SQLite that setting is per connection and is not stored in the database file, so this line only affects connections that execute this script — and, after the RW7 change, it is additionally a no-op because the migration runner now applies the DDL inside a transaction (SQLite ignores this pragma while a transaction is open). What actually protects the running service is the identical pragma that `openWorkspace()` issues on every connection it opens. A reader could easily conclude the file guarantees enforcement and remove the code that really does.

**Why it was wrong.** An undocumented pragma whose true scope is not what its placement suggests.

**What was done.** Added a comment above the pragma spelling out that the setting is per connection, not stored in the file; that it is a no-op inside the transaction the migration runner uses; and that `openWorkspace()` in remote/src/workspace.ts sets the same pragma before migrating, which is what every runtime connection relies on. Verified at runtime that a connection from `openWorkspace` still reports foreign_keys = 1 and still rejects deleting a group that has sessions.

**Why it matters.** Comment only; no behaviour change. Prevents someone from deleting the line that actually enforces foreign keys.


#### RW13 — Deleting a viewer or terminal pane leaves the stored split layout pointing at the deleted pane

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** `deleteViewerPane` and `deleteTerminalPane` delete only the pane row. The region's split layout is stored as a JSON tree in a separate column, so its leaf entries can still name the deleted pane's id; the database cannot enforce anything about ids inside a JSON blob.

**Why it was wrong.** Pane rows and the layout tree that references them are stored separately, and only the client rebuilds the tree.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED, deliberately. The desktop client owns the split tree: closing a pane rewrites the tree and saves it with the layout-save call, and the client-side code and its tests treat the stored tree as something only the client authors (the server "stores and echoes the tree verbatim"). Having the server also rewrite the tree on a pane delete would mean two writers racing to author the same structure, and the server has no way to know which sibling should absorb the freed space. Recording it here so the coupling is visible: if a future change lets a pane be deleted without a following layout save, the layout must be repaired at the same time.

**Why it matters.** No user-visible effect on the current client, which always saves a rewritten tree when it closes a pane. A future or third-party client that deletes a pane without saving a new layout would leave a leaf pointing at nothing.


#### RW14 — One corrupt stored layout tree makes the entire workspace listing fail

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** `getLayouts` and `getLayout` call `JSON.parse` on the stored tree with no error handling. The nested listing call walks every group and every Dev Session and parses every layout, so a single unparseable tree makes the whole sidebar listing throw — the client gets an error instead of a workspace, and cannot even reach the session to repair it.

**Why it was wrong.** A stored JSON blob is parsed without considering that it might not parse. The existing suite proves this state is reachable in principle: one test deliberately writes an invalid tree to check that duplicating a session rolls back.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED, and I am flagging it as a judgement call rather than a certainty. The alternative — catching the parse error and reporting the region as having no layout — silently discards a user's pane arrangement and hides real corruption, which for authoritative state is arguably worse than failing loudly. Corruption here can only come from outside this store (the store always writes the output of JSON.stringify). If the project decides availability wins, the change is small and local: wrap the two parse sites and report null for the affected region.

**Why it matters.** Only reachable if something outside this store writes a malformed tree into the database.


#### RW15 — Only one of the nineteen remote-procedure handlers validates its parameters

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/workspace.ts`

**What was wrong.** In the handler table at the bottom of the file, the listing handler checks that it received an object with a string `serverId` and throws a clear message otherwise. The other eighteen handlers cast the incoming parameters to their expected shape with no check at all, so a malformed request produces whatever error the store happens to hit (typically a TypeError from reading a property of undefined, or a database constraint failure).

**Why it was wrong.** The parameter shapes are declared as TypeScript interfaces, which are erased at runtime; only the listing handler was given a runtime guard.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. Any thrown error already becomes a well-formed error response to the caller, so nothing crashes or corrupts. Adding eighteen hand-written validators would duplicate the shared method contract in a second place and is a design decision about how much the service should distrust its own client, not a bug I should settle unilaterally inside a bug-hunting pass. Recording it so the asymmetry is a deliberate, visible choice.

**Why it matters.** A malformed request gets a confusing error message instead of a precise one. No data-integrity consequence.


**Follow-up.** **Partly addressed later in this round by X30**, which documented the validation the dispatcher does enforce.

*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope: remote/src/workspace.ts, remote/sql/schema.sql, remote/test/workspace.test.ts, remote/test/schema.test.ts only. I read all four end to end plus sections 3.1-3.5 and 4.2 of docs/SPEC.md, and I read (never wrote) src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.{h,cpp}, src/app/SessionLayouts.*, and the C++ tests to confirm no client change is implied by my fixes — notably that the Qt client still sends a serverId with every layout save (a C++ test asserts the request has exactly four parameters), so RW1 overrides that value without changing the wire shape. Twelve findings fixed, three explicitly left alone with reasons. No project build or test command was run, per instructions. Verification instead: I exercised every changed code path in throwaway scripts under /tmp (since deleted) that import the module directly — the reconciled reorder for both groups and sessions, the layout upsert's row identity and derived server tag, the cross-server move cascade, both rejection paths, the rollback behaviour of the rewritten transaction helper, the fresh-database and version-1-upgrade migration paths, the newer-database refusal, and that foreign keys are still enforced after the now-transactional migration. I also ran all four new workspace test bodies and the new schema test body verbatim; all pass. Two things I want to flag as honest uncertainty rather than claimed fact: (1) I could not run the project's own test runner, so while I traced every existing assertion that touches my changed methods and believe all are unaffected (they all use a single server id and complete ordering lists), that is reasoning, not a green suite; (2) the switch to BEGIN IMMEDIATE means a second process writing concurrently now fails fast with a busy error where it previously failed later at the first write — better behaviour in my judgement, but it is a behaviour change under contention, not a pure no-op.

### 4.2 Remote service — file service and tmux driver (`RF`)

*Scope:* `remote/src/files.ts` (remote file stat/read/write, path resolution, file-change watching) and `remote/src/tmux.ts` (creating, listing and attaching tmux sessions), plus their tests.

*16 findings:* 5 latent bug, 3 footgun, 2 active bug, 2 simplification, 1 documentation error, 1 inconsistency, 1 nit, 1 missing test.

#### RF1 — Save-conflict errors named the current revision with a key the client never reads

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`, `remote/test/files.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** When a save is refused because the file on the server changed, the server sends back an error whose extra data was supposed to include the file's current revision token so the desktop client can immediately offer "reload / overwrite". The server put that token under the field names `actual` (mismatch case) and `revision` (create-only case). The client (src/editor/EditorController.cpp, EditorController::save) reads the field `currentRevision`, and so does the browser-side editor page (src/web/editor/src/index.ts) and the C++ RPC test (src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp). Because the names did not match, the field the client looked for was always missing.

**Why it was wrong.** Two sides of the same payload were named independently and nobody compared them: files.ts invented `expected`/`actual`/`revision`, while the C++ and web client had settled on `currentRevision`. Nothing in the repository read `actual` or the create-only `revision`, so no test noticed.

**What was done.** In assertRevisionMatches() in remote/src/files.ts, the on-disk token is now always reported as `currentRevision` (with `expected` kept for the client's benefit and `currentRevision: null` for the "file was deleted" case, which genuinely has no current token). A comment records why that exact name is load-bearing. A new test in remote/test/files.test.ts pins the whole error payload for both the stale-revision and the already-exists cases with deepEqual, so a future rename fails the suite.

**Why it matters.** Every conflicting save had to make a second round trip to the server (an extra stat call) before the conflict dialog could be shown, and that fallback read the revision from the stat method, which was itself wrong for symlinks (see RF2). Now the conflict dialog opens straight from the error.


#### RF2 — stat() minted a symlink's own revision while read and write follow the link, making every symlinked file look permanently conflicted

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`, `remote/test/files.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The remote service hands the client an opaque "revision" token per file, used to detect that a file changed. `stat` built that token from lstat, i.e. from the symbolic link itself (the link's own timestamps, inode and length). `readFile` and `writeFile` both open/stat *through* the link, so they built the token from the file the link points at. For any file opened through a symbolic link the two tokens could never be equal, so: (a) the client's reconnect probe, which compares the token from `stat` against the token it got from `readFile`, always concluded the file had been changed by someone else; and (b) the conflict fallback path fed the link's token back as the expected revision of a save, which the write guard then always rejected.

**Why it was wrong.** `stat` used lstat for a good reason (so a symbolic link is reported with kind "symlink" instead of masquerading as its target), but the revision token was minted from the same lstat result as a side effect, without noticing that the other two methods resolve the link.

**What was done.** remote/src/files.ts, stat(): kind, size, mtime and mode still come from lstat (unchanged reporting), but when the entry is a symbolic link the revision is minted from a stat that follows the link. A dangling or looping link has no readable target, so that stat's failure falls back to the link's own token instead of turning stat into an error. A new test asserts that stat, readFile and writeFile all agree on the token through a symlink, that the save replaces the linked-to file, and that the link itself is not severed.

**Why it matters.** Editing a file reached through a symbolic link (a very common layout: a config or source file symlinked into a repository) reported bogus "changed on the server" warnings on every reconnect and could leave the user unable to save at all.


#### RF3 — Temp-file cleanup on a failed save could replace the real error with a meaningless one

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** Saves are atomic: the new content goes into a hidden temporary file next to the target, then that file is renamed over the target. If anything in that sequence fails, the error handler deleted the temporary file and re-threw. The delete itself was awaited without a guard, so if the delete failed (for example a read-only or full directory, or a permission change) its error propagated instead of the original one.

**Why it was wrong.** `await fsp.rm(tmp, { force: true })` in the catch block. `force: true` only swallows "file does not exist"; every other failure still throws, and a throw inside a catch block discards the error being handled.

**What was done.** The cleanup is now `await fsp.rm(tmp, { force: true }).catch(() => {})`, with a comment explaining that cleanup must never mask the real failure.

**Why it matters.** In the failure-on-failure case the client would have received a generic internal error instead of the tagged revision-mismatch error, so the "file changed externally" dialog would never have appeared and the user could have been told the save failed for an unrelated reason.


#### RF4 — Malformed base64 upload was silently written as short, corrupt bytes and reported as a successful save

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`, `remote/test/files.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** A write request may declare its content is base64-encoded (used for binary files). The service decoded it with Node's Buffer.from(content, "base64"), which is deliberately forgiving: it silently discards every character outside the base64 alphabet and throws away a trailing partial group. A payload damaged in transit therefore produced fewer bytes than intended, the write succeeded, and the client was told the save was fine.

**Why it was wrong.** No validation of the encoded payload before decoding; the decoder's leniency was mistaken for tolerance rather than data loss.

**What was done.** remote/src/files.ts, writeFileLocked(): when the declared encoding is base64, the payload is validated before anything touches the filesystem. Whitespace and trailing padding are stripped, then the remainder must consist only of standard (+ /) or URL-safe (- _) base64 characters, and its length may not be 1 modulo 4 (a length that encodes no byte string at all). A violation throws an error with the message "Invalid base64 content for <path>". A new test proves both a junk payload and a payload cut mid-group are rejected and that no file is created.

**Why it matters.** Latent rather than active: the current desktop client always writes UTF-8 text and never sets the base64 encoding, so nothing sends base64 writes today. If anything ever does (binary editing, an image write), a corrupted transfer would have silently destroyed file contents while reporting success.


#### RF5 — Double decode of every file read, plus a byte-order-mark trap for the next person

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`, `remote/test/files.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** To decide whether a file is text or binary, the read path decoded the whole buffer once with a strict UTF-8 decoder (throwing the result away) and then, if it was text, decoded the same bytes a second time to produce the string it returned. Separately, the strict decoder is a trap: JavaScript's TextDecoder removes a leading byte-order mark by default, so the obvious cleanup (reuse the decoded string) would have silently dropped three bytes from the client's buffer, and the next save would have deleted the byte-order mark from the file.

**Why it was wrong.** The binary check was written as a standalone predicate returning a boolean, discarding the decoded text it had already produced. The BOM-stripping default of TextDecoder was not documented anywhere.

**What was done.** The helper `isBinary` is replaced by `decodeUtf8`, which returns the decoded string or undefined when the bytes are not text (any NUL byte, or invalid UTF-8). It passes `ignoreBOM: true` so the mark is preserved, with a comment stating that this is a correctness requirement, not an optimisation. The read path uses the returned string directly, so large files are walked once instead of twice. A new test writes a file with a UTF-8 byte-order mark, checks it comes back as text with the mark intact, saves the same content back, and asserts the file is byte-for-byte unchanged.

**Why it matters.** Halves the decoding work on every text read, and the new test now fails if anyone reintroduces BOM stripping.


#### RF6 — The requested read length was clamped in two places that had to stay identical

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** A ranged read (offset plus length) clamped the caller's length to a non-negative integer with the same expression written twice: once to size the buffer that is read, and once to compute the `truncated` flag that tells the caller bytes were left unread.

**Why it was wrong.** The truncated flag was added later and repeated the clamp instead of reusing it.

**What was done.** remote/src/files.ts, readFile(): the clamped length is computed once into `wanted` and used by both the read size and the truncated flag, with a comment saying why it must only exist once.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change today; removes the possibility of the two clamps drifting apart and reporting a wrong truncated flag, which would make the client treat a partial read as a whole file.


#### RF7 — The temporary save file was created with the target's permissions, including file-type bits

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** When overwriting an existing file the service created its temporary file with the mode taken straight from the existing file's stat result. That value contains not only the permission bits but also the file-type bits the operating system reports, which have no meaning as a creation mode. It also means the temporary file was group- or world-readable from the moment it was created, i.e. while it was still half written.

**Why it was wrong.** `existing.mode` was passed through unmasked to both open() and chmod(). Linux happens to ignore the surplus bits, which is why nothing broke.

**What was done.** remote/src/files.ts, writeFileLocked(): the preserved mode is masked with 0o7777 (keeping permissions plus the setuid/setgid/sticky bits and dropping the file-type bits) into `finalMode`, which is what chmod pins after the content is flushed. The temporary file itself is now created 0o600 in the overwrite case, since its final mode is set by that chmod anyway; the create-a-new-file case keeps its previous 0o644. Comments explain both choices.

**Why it matters.** Same resulting file permissions as before (the existing mode-preservation test still holds), but partially written content is no longer briefly readable by other users on the server, and the code no longer relies on the kernel ignoring nonsense mode bits.


#### RF8 — A tmux invocation that never returns hung the RPC method, and the client's session list, forever

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/tmux.ts`

**What was wrong.** Every tmux query (list sessions, does session exist, kill session) is run as a child process. No time limit was set. The caller awaits the child, and the JSON-RPC dispatcher awaits the caller, so a tmux that never answers -- an unresponsive tmux server socket, or a socket directory on a stalled network mount -- would leave the request outstanding indefinitely with no error and no result.

**Why it was wrong.** The child-process options set an output buffer limit but no timeout.

**What was done.** remote/src/tmux.ts: a named constant TMUX_TIMEOUT_MS (10 seconds) is passed as the child's timeout. On expiry the child is killed and the failure surfaces as a spawn failure, which the module's existing rule ("absence is not failure") already turns into an empty list / false / successful no-op rather than an error. A comment explains the choice.

**Why it matters.** A wedged tmux can no longer strand a request. Ten seconds is far beyond any healthy tmux listing, so healthy hosts are unaffected. Not directly unit-testable without spawning a real hanging process, so it is covered by reasoning rather than a new test.


#### RF9 — A long security comment sat above the wrong function

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/tmux.ts`

**What was wrong.** A fourteen-line comment explaining why every tmux listing record is prefixed with an unguessable per-call marker (so a hostile session name cannot forge an extra record) was positioned directly above execFileRunner, the function that merely spawns the child process. That function has nothing to do with markers or parsing; the marker is minted in listSessions and enforced in parseSessions, both further down the file. The comment also ended with a sentence about base64url that read as if it described the code immediately below it.

**Why it was wrong.** The comment was presumably written before the functions were reordered, and the reference to it ("the per-call record anchor described above" in parseSessions' documentation) kept it looking plausible.

**What was done.** The block is moved to immediately above parseSessions, so the code it describes is the code beneath it and the existing "described above" reference still resolves. The final sentence is reworded to say explicitly that listSessions is what mints the marker with base64url. No code changed.

**Why it matters.** A reader of the spawn helper is no longer sent looking for marker logic that is not there, and the parser's security invariant is documented where the parser is.


#### RF10 — Inconsistency: existence checks avoid tmux's target syntax on purpose, but killing a session still uses it

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/tmux.ts`

**What was wrong.** The existence check deliberately does not ask tmux "does session X exist"; it lists all sessions and compares names, and its documentation says this is because tmux's target syntax treats a colon as a session/window separator. Killing a session, three lines below, does use that same target syntax. Nothing said why the reasoning did not apply there, which invites a future reader either to "fix" the kill path or to copy the target syntax back into the existence check.

**Why it was wrong.** Two functions written against the same hazard with different answers, and only one of them explained.

**What was done.** Documented rather than changed. remote/src/tmux.ts, killSession(): the doc comment now states that there is no listing-only way to destroy a session, that the `=` exact-match prefix is what stops an option-shaped or wildcard name from reaching a different session, and that the one construct `=` does not neutralise is a colon inside the name -- which tmux itself rewrites away when a session is created, so such a name should not exist on the host. I did not change the code: switching kill to a listing pre-check would add a second tmux round trip and rewrite the argv contract that several existing security tests pin, and I could not verify tmux's target parsing for that case without a live server.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change. The remaining residual risk is now written down instead of being a silent asymmetry. Marked as documented-only rather than fixed code.


#### RF11 — The "inside the repository" flag is a lexical hint, not a sandbox, and nothing said so

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** resolvePath turns a possibly relative path into an absolute one and returns a flag saying whether it lands inside the project root. The flag is computed purely from the text of the path: it never consults the filesystem, so a symbolic link inside the project that points somewhere else still reports "inside". The product specification (section 9) explicitly allows opening files outside the project root and only asks the interface to indicate it, so this is correct behaviour -- but a future reader could easily mistake the flag for an access-control boundary and build a permission check on it.

**Why it was wrong.** The existing comment explained the ".." edge cases but never stated what the flag is and is not for.

**What was done.** Comment only. remote/src/files.ts, above resolvePath: states that the check is intentionally lexical (it costs nothing and works for paths that do not exist yet), that a symlink out of the tree still reports inside, and that this service confines nothing because the specification requires outside paths to remain openable. I confirmed there is no confinement claim anywhere in the module that this contradicts.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change. Prevents the flag being misread as a security boundary. To be explicit about the audit result: I found no path-traversal or sandbox-escape bug, because this service is not specified to sandbox anything -- ".." segments, absolute paths and symlinks are all deliberately permitted.


#### RF12 — Undocumented environment variable overriding the tmux binary

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/tmux.ts`

**What was wrong.** The tmux executable is taken from the environment variable CODEHARBOR_TMUX when set, falling back to plain `tmux` found on the PATH. Nothing said the override existed or what it is for, even though the module's header comment describes the module's other two rules in detail.

**Why it was wrong.** Added without a comment.

**What was done.** A two-line comment above the constant in remote/src/tmux.ts stating the override exists so a test suite can point at a wrapper or a tmux that is not on the PATH.

**Why it matters.** Discoverability only.


#### RF13 — Missing tests for symlinks, byte-order marks, malformed uploads, the conflict payload, watch-before-create, and carriage returns

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/files.test.ts`, `remote/test/tmux.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** Six behaviours had no coverage, four of which are exactly where the bugs above lived. (1) No test read or wrote a file through a symbolic link, which is why RF2 survived. (2) No test covered a UTF-8 byte-order mark, so a decoder change could silently start deleting it. (3) No test covered a malformed base64 upload. (4) No test looked inside a revision-mismatch error's data, which is why RF1 survived. (5) No test watched a path that does not exist yet -- the branch where the operating system's file-watcher cannot attach and the polling fallback is the only signal, and the branch that produces a "created" rather than "modified" event. (6) In the tmux parser, the code that strips a trailing carriage return (output can cross an SSH channel that translates line endings) was never exercised, and neither was a well-marked but too-short record.

**Why it was wrong.** Coverage followed the happy paths and the well-known race conditions; symbolic links, encoding markers, malformed input and the "not there yet" watch case were never enumerated.

**What was done.** Six tests added, following the existing conventions (node:test, node:assert/strict, real temporary directories, event-driven waits with a wall-clock guard rather than sleeps). In remote/test/files.test.ts: byte-order-mark round trip is byte-exact; stat, read and write agree on the revision through a symlink and the link survives the save (also asserts a directory listing classifies the link as a symlink); malformed and mid-group-truncated base64 are rejected and create no file; the revision-mismatch error payload is pinned with deepEqual for both the stale and already-exists cases; watching a not-yet-existing path yields a "created" event when it appears. In remote/test/tmux.test.ts: a carriage-return-terminated listing parses identically to a newline one (a stray carriage return would otherwise end up inside the session name and break every exact-name comparison), and a properly marked but short record is dropped rather than half-parsed into a nameless session that a later kill could act on.

**Why it matters.** Each new test fails on a plausible regression of the corresponding fix. Note: as instructed, I did not run the test suite; the tests are written against the code as it now stands.


#### RF14 — A rapidly changing watched file queues one filesystem check per change signal

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** Each watch subscription serialises its filesystem checks by chaining every new check onto the previous one's promise. That is what stops the operating-system signal and the polling timer from both reporting the same change. But there is no ceiling on the chain: if change signals arrive faster than the checks complete (a log file being appended to in a tight loop), each signal appends another link, so memory use tracks the signal rate rather than a constant.

**Why it was wrong.** The design deliberately serialises rather than coalesces, and coalescing was never needed for the rates it was written for.

**What was done.** Not fixed, deliberately. The safe fix is to allow at most one check to wait behind the in-flight one: a waiting check has not read the filesystem yet, so it will still observe any change whose signal arrived before it started, meaning no event can be lost. I judged the trade bad on balance: a check takes microseconds and the queue drains, so the growth is transient at realistic rates, whereas the coalescing flag adds a new invariant (it must be cleared exactly when the waiting check begins, not when it is scheduled) that a future edit can quietly break -- turning a theoretical memory blip into real lost change notifications. Recorded here so the reasoning is not lost.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change. Reachable only under pathological change rates on a watched file; no duplicate or missing events result, only transient memory.


#### RF15 — A whole-file read has no size ceiling

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** A read with no byte range loads the entire file into memory, then turns it into a string inside a single line of JSON-RPC output. There is no upper bound. A multi-gigabyte file fails outright (it exceeds the maximum buffer size), and anything between a few hundred megabytes and that limit will stall the single-threaded remote service and balloon its memory while the line is built.

**Why it was wrong.** The ranged-read path exists and is bounded, but the whole-file path was never given a ceiling, and the one client that always requests whole files never sends a range.

**What was done.** Not fixed on the server side, on purpose. The result already carries a `truncated` flag, and the image/PDF viewer path in the C++ client sets an 8 MiB range and refuses a truncated result rather than rendering partial content. The editor, however, requests whole files and never inspects `truncated`, so if I capped reads here the editor would silently receive a partial buffer and the user could save the truncation over the real file -- destroying data, far worse than the current stall. Making the whole-file path fail loudly past a ceiling is a cross-side policy change: the editor must either pass a range or treat a capped read as "open read-only / streaming" (specification 8.8 already calls for a streaming read-only viewer for very large text). Routed as out-of-scope for the editor owner.

**Why it matters.** Opening a very large file in the editor today stalls the remote service and may fail with an out-of-memory-class error. No data loss.


**Follow-up.** **Resolved later in this round by X11 and X14**: the editor now always sends a byte ceiling, and the `truncated` flag is now correct on every return path.

#### RF16 — The revision returned by a successful save is read back from disk after the rename

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** After renaming the temporary file over the target, the service stats the target again to mint the revision token it returns. If another process modifies the file in the instant between the rename and that stat, the token describes the other process's version, and the client would then believe its own content is stored under that token -- so its next save would pass the guard and overwrite the other process's content.

**Why it was wrong.** There is no way to obtain the post-rename token atomically: reading the token before the rename would capture a stale change-time (the rename itself updates it), which would make the client's next legitimate save fail instead.

**What was done.** Not fixed. Both alternatives are worse than the current behaviour: minting the token before the rename produces a token that never matches the file, and locking out external processes is not possible. The per-path write lock already prevents this between two of the service's own writers; only a third-party process can hit it. Reported for completeness rather than changed. I am confident about the mechanism but consider the practical exposure very small.

**Why it matters.** An external editor writing the same file in a sub-millisecond window around a save could later be overwritten without a conflict prompt.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope was remote/src/files.ts, remote/src/tmux.ts and their two test files; I wrote to those four files only. As instructed I ran no build, type-check or test command -- every change was made by reading the surrounding code carefully, and the two behavioural claims that cross module boundaries (the currentRevision key in RF1 and the symlink revision in RF2) were verified by reading the consumers: src/editor/EditorController.cpp, src/web/editor/src/index.ts, src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp and src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp. No existing test asserts the payload key names or symlink stat behaviour, so neither fix should disturb the suite; the new deepEqual test now pins the payload. On the specific hunts requested: there is no path-traversal or sandbox-escape bug because this service is specified not to confine paths (specification section 9 requires files outside the project root to stay openable) -- I documented that instead of "fixing" it (RF11). tmux command construction is genuinely safe: every invocation goes through an argument array with no shell, and hostile names are already covered by existing tests; the only real gap there was the missing timeout (RF8). The stat/read race was already closed by reading bytes and metadata from one open descriptor, and the write path already re-checks the revision immediately before the rename; I found no new race there beyond the inherent post-rename one (RF16). Two findings are documentation-only by choice (RF10, RF11) and three are explained rather than changed (RF14, RF15, RF16); everything else is fixed in code with a test where a test can express it.

### 4.3 Remote service — JSON-RPC dispatcher, agent-status bridge and adapters (`RR`)

*Scope:* `remote/src/codeharbord.ts` (the service entry point and its JSON-RPC 2.0 dispatcher), `remote/src/rpc-types.ts`, `remote/src/events.ts`, `remote/src/bridge.ts`, `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts`, `remote/src/adapters/*`, plus their tests.

*25 findings:* 8 latent bug, 7 missing test, 4 footgun, 2 nit, 2 documentation error, 1 active bug, 1 inconsistency.

#### RR1 — Command-line entry point silently does nothing when the install path needs URL escaping

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`, `remote/src/bridge.ts`, `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts`

**What was wrong.** All three runnable scripts decided "am I being run directly, or merely imported?" by comparing `import.meta.url` (a URL) against the hand-built string `` `file://${process.argv[1]}` `` (a raw filesystem path). Node reports `import.meta.url` percent-encoded, so for a script installed under a directory containing a space (or any other character a URL must escape) the two strings never match. The service then starts, runs no code at all, and exits with status 0. For `codeharbord` that means the client opens an SSH channel, gets an immediately-closed stream and no error text; for the hook it means agent status silently stops updating.

**Why it was wrong.** String concatenation was used where URL construction is required. I confirmed the behaviour concretely: for `/tmp/argv test/probe.mjs`, `process.argv[1]` is `/tmp/argv test/probe.mjs` while `import.meta.url` is `file:///tmp/argv%20test/probe.mjs`.

**What was done.** Added `import { pathToFileURL } from "node:url"` at the top of each of the three files and changed each guard to `if (import.meta.url === pathToFileURL(process.argv[1] ?? "").href)`. This also fixes invocation through a relative path and is documented with a comment explaining why concatenation is wrong. The `?? ""` keeps the expression safe when there is no script argument at all (Node then resolves the empty path against the working directory, which can never equal a module URL).

**Why it matters.** A CodeHarbor install under a path such as `/home/me/My Projects/` previously produced a remote service that appeared to launch and did nothing. Now the entry point is path-independent.


#### RR2 — A response that cannot be serialized killed the whole remote service

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** In `runStdio`, each request line was handled with `void handleLine(line).then(response => { if (response) process.stdout.write(JSON.stringify(response) + "\n"); })` with no rejection handler. `JSON.stringify` throws on a value containing a reference cycle or a `BigInt`. Because the throw happened inside a `.then` callback of a promise nobody catches, it became an unhandled promise rejection, which by default terminates a modern Node process. One badly shaped result from any single request handler would therefore take down the service and every live file-watch subscription with it, and the client would see the whole channel die instead of one failed call.

**Why it was wrong.** Missing error handling around serialization plus a floating promise (`void ...` with no `.catch`).

**What was done.** Serialization now happens inside a `try`/`catch` within the `.then`; on failure the server answers that request with a JSON-RPC internal error (code -32603, message "Response could not be serialized") so the client's pending call completes instead of waiting forever. A trailing `.catch` was also added as a last line of defence, with a comment explaining that an unhandled rejection is fatal in Node.

**Why it matters.** A single malformed payload can no longer kill the service, and the requesting client always gets an answer.


#### RR3 — Writing to a closed client pipe crashed the service with EPIPE

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** When the client disappears (SSH channel closed, client killed), the next write to standard output raises an `error` event on the stream. Nothing listened for it, and an unhandled stream `error` event terminates the process with a stack trace on standard error. Any in-flight response or file-watch notification arriving after the client vanished produced that crash.

**Why it was wrong.** No `error` listener on `process.stdout`.

**What was done.** Added `process.stdout.on("error", () => {})` at the start of `runStdio`, with a comment stating that the orderly shutdown is driven by the standard-input close handler instead.

**Why it matters.** Client disconnection is now an ordinary shutdown rather than a crash with noise in the server log.


#### RR4 — The service never exited after the client disconnected if any file watch was active

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** `runStdio` had no handler for the end of standard input. The file-watch service (in `remote/src/files.ts`) holds live `fs.watch` handles per subscription; those handles keep Node's event loop alive. So after the client went away, a `codeharbord` process that had ever served a `file.watch` request stayed resident forever, still watching directories. Every reconnect started another one.

**Why it was wrong.** No shutdown path on end-of-input; only the polling timers in the watch service were `unref`'d, not the `fs.watch` handles.

**What was done.** Added `rl.on("close", () => { fileWatchService.closeAll(); })`. `closeAll()` is the existing public API of the watch service (no edit to `files.ts`), and releasing the watchers lets the process exit by itself once in-flight work settles. The comment explains the leak being prevented.

**Why it matters.** Removes an accumulating stray-process and file-descriptor leak on the development server.


#### RR5 — Request parameters were never type-checked, so bad input surfaced as a generic internal error

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** JSON-RPC 2.0 requires that `params`, when present, be a structured value: an object or an array. The dispatcher accepted anything, including numbers, strings, booleans and `null`, and passed it straight to a handler. Handlers immediately treat the value as a record (e.g. `p.path`), so a primitive produced an internal `TypeError` reported to the client as error code -32603 ("internal error") instead of -32602 ("invalid params"). The standard error code -32602 was not even defined in the file.

**Why it was wrong.** Envelope validation in `isRpcRequest` covered `jsonrpc`, `method` and `id` but not `params`.

**What was done.** Added `export const RPC_INVALID_PARAMS = -32602;` alongside the other reserved codes, and a `paramsOk` check in `dispatch`. A request whose `params` is neither absent nor a non-null object/array is answered with -32602 and a message naming the method. Ordering was chosen deliberately: an unknown method still wins over bad parameters, so a client is told the method is gone rather than being sent to fix arguments for a method that never existed. A notification (no `id`) with bad parameters is dropped silently and its handler is not called, because JSON-RPC forbids replying to notifications at all.

**Why it matters.** Malformed calls now get the correct, actionable protocol error, and handlers are no longer entered with a value of the wrong shape. The C++ client only ever sends an object or omits `params`, so no existing caller changes behaviour.


#### RR6 — A handler returning nothing would put a response on the wire with neither result nor error

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** On success the dispatcher built `{ jsonrpc, id, result }` directly from the handler's return value. If a handler returned `undefined`, `JSON.stringify` drops the `result` key entirely, emitting `{"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":7}` — a response containing neither a `result` nor an `error` member, which JSON-RPC 2.0 forbids. The C++ client treats such a frame as a protocol violation. No handler returns `undefined` today (they all return an object), so this was reachable only via a future handler with no payload.

**Why it was wrong.** No normalization of an absent return value.

**What was done.** The success branch is now `result: result === undefined ? null : result`, with a comment stating the exclusivity requirement.

**Why it matters.** A handler with no payload can no longer produce an invalid frame.


#### RR7 — Batch requests (a JSON array of calls) are not supported, and nothing said so

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** JSON-RPC 2.0 defines batching: a client may send an array of request objects and expect an array of responses. This server rejects an array with a single "Invalid Request" error (identifier `null`), because an array fails the request-shape check. That is a deliberate and reasonable limitation — the only client, the C++ `CodeharbordClient`, writes exactly one request per line and matches replies by identifier — but nothing in the code recorded the decision, so the next reader cannot tell a choice from an oversight.

**Why it was wrong.** Undocumented intentional scope limit.

**What was done.** Not changed behaviourally: implementing batching would add an unused code path in the most critical function in the service. Instead the documentation comment on `dispatch` now states explicitly that batches are deliberately unsupported, why (one request per line, replies correlated by identifier), and what a client gets if it tries. A test (`handleLine answers a batch line with a single Invalid Request`) pins that behaviour so it cannot change silently.

**Why it matters.** The limitation is now explicit and locked down by a test rather than being accidental.


#### RR8 — A JSON array passed the "is this an object?" checks for event metadata and native events

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/events.ts`, `remote/src/bridge.ts`

**What was wrong.** Both the event validator (`validateEvent` in events.ts) and the bridge message decoder (`processBridgeLine` in bridge.ts) tested object-ness with `typeof x === "object" && x !== null`. In JavaScript that is also true for arrays. Consequences: (1) an event whose `metadata` is an array validated as a well-formed agent event, and the C++ side reads it with a to-object conversion that silently yields an empty object; (2) a bridge message whose `native` field is an array passed validation and was then read as a harness-native event with no event name, i.e. a silent no-op instead of a rejected line.

**Why it was wrong.** The classic JSON validation footgun: `typeof [] === "object"`.

**What was done.** Added one exported type guard `isPlainObject(value)` in events.ts (`typeof === "object" && !== null && !Array.isArray(...)`), matching the existing `isAgentState`/`isHarness` house style, and used it in `validateEvent` for `metadata` and in `processBridgeLine` for both `native` and `metadata`. Comments explain the trap. Test added: `validateEvent({...good, metadata: []})` is now false while a real object is still accepted.

**Why it matters.** Structurally wrong events are rejected at the boundary instead of being partially accepted and silently emptied later.


#### RR9 — The agent-status hook could block the user's agent run forever

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts`

**What was wrong.** `emitHookEvent` opened a connection to the bridge's Unix socket and waited for either a `connect` or an `error` event, with no time limit. If the socket file exists and a bridge process is present but wedged (accepted the connection and stopped reading, or is stuck before accepting), neither event ever arrives and the promise never settles. Because the coding agent runs this hook synchronously as part of its own lifecycle, the agent itself hangs. The project specification (section 6.4) states that a harness adapter must never block or break the coding agent.

**Why it was wrong.** No timeout on the socket used by a blocking, agent-critical code path.

**What was done.** Added `export const HOOK_TIMEOUT_MS = 2000` and an optional third parameter `timeoutMs` to `emitHookEvent`. The socket now sets an inactivity timeout that destroys the socket and rejects with a message naming the socket path and the elapsed limit; the timeout is cleared once the line has been written. `main` already swallows rejections and reports them on standard error, so a wedged bridge now costs the agent at most two seconds.

**Why it matters.** An unavailable or stuck CodeHarbor bridge can no longer stall a coding-agent run indefinitely.


#### RR10 — The hook could exit non-zero, which a harness may read as a failed hook

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts`

**What was wrong.** The command-line entry point was `void main();` with no rejection handler. `main` catches failures from the socket write, but its own reporting write to standard error can itself fail (for example EPIPE if the harness closed the hook's error stream). That rejection would be unhandled, and Node exits non-zero on an unhandled rejection. A harness that inspects hook exit status would then report a hook failure to the user — precisely the disruption the design forbids.

**Why it was wrong.** Floating promise at the entry point of a script whose contract is "never disturb the agent".

**What was done.** The entry point is now `void main().catch(() => {});` with a comment explaining that the outcome is pinned to success on purpose.

**Why it matters.** The hook is now guaranteed to exit successfully regardless of the state of CodeHarbor or of its own error stream.


#### RR11 — The bridge left its socket file behind when stopped by a signal, and had no shutdown path

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/bridge.ts`

**What was wrong.** The bridge's command-line entry point started the server and returned. With no handler for interrupt or terminate signals, the default action killed the process immediately, leaving the Unix socket file on disk. The next run then had to treat a leftover socket as stale (see RR12), and any tooling that checks for the socket's presence to decide whether the bridge is running was misled.

**Why it was wrong.** No signal handling or cleanup around a process that owns a filesystem resource.

**What was done.** The entry point now keeps the server object, installs one shutdown function on both interrupt and terminate signals that closes the listener, removes the socket file if it is still a socket, and exits with status 0. It deliberately does not wait for open connections to drain, because a single stuck producer would otherwise block shutdown forever; the comment says so.

**Why it matters.** Stopping the bridge now leaves a clean filesystem state and a predictable exit code.


#### RR12 — Starting a second bridge silently orphans the first instead of failing

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/bridge.ts`

**What was wrong.** Before listening, `startBridge` removes an existing socket file (guarded so that a regular file is never clobbered). It cannot tell a leftover socket from one a live bridge is still listening on, because that requires an asynchronous connection probe. So starting a second bridge on the same path succeeds: the second one takes over the filesystem name and the first keeps listening on a socket no producer can reach any more. Its relayed events stop arriving and nothing reports an error.

**Why it was wrong.** Liveness of a Unix socket cannot be determined synchronously, and `startBridge` is a synchronous function returning a server object.

**What was done.** Not changed. A correct probe would make `startBridge` asynchronous and change its signature and every caller and test; "one bridge per host per user" is the intended deployment. Instead the existing comment above the unlink now states the limitation explicitly: a live socket is indistinguishable from a stale one here, so a second bridge orphans the first rather than failing with an address-in-use error.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change; the trap is now written down where the code makes it, so the next reader does not have to rediscover it.


#### RR13 — The server puts no upper bound on the length of one input line, while the client does

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** The service reads requests with Node's line reader on standard input. A peer that streams megabytes without ever sending a newline makes that reader buffer without limit, so memory grows until the process dies. The C++ side of the same wire protocol explicitly guards against this: `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` defines a 16 MiB cap (`kMaxLineBytes`) for exactly this reason. The two ends of one protocol therefore disagree about whether unbounded frames are tolerated.

**Why it was wrong.** Node's line reader owns the buffer and offers no maximum-line option; enforcing a cap means reading raw chunks and doing the newline framing by hand.

**What was done.** Not changed, deliberately.
