Checking the length after the line is delivered does not bound the memory (it is already allocated), so a real fix means replacing the line reader with hand-written framing in the single most critical function of the service — and I am not permitted to run the build or test suite to validate it. Mitigating facts: the only writer is the authenticated CodeHarbor client at the far end of one SSH channel, and that client caps its own frames at 16 MiB. Recommended follow-up: replace the line reader with a chunk reader that tracks a pending-buffer length, drops the connection past the cap, and mirror the 16 MiB constant so both ends agree.

**Why it matters.** Unchanged today. Documented so the asymmetry with the client is not mistaken for a deliberate choice.


#### RR14 — Blank-line check copied every incoming line, including multi-megabyte write requests

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`

**What was wrong.** Each input line was tested with `line.trim().length === 0` to skip separator lines. `trim()` allocates a full copy of the string. A `file.writeFile` request carrying base64 content can be many megabytes, so every such request paid a full extra copy of its own body purely to answer the question "is this line blank?". The C++ reader avoids exactly this copy on purpose and documents it.

**Why it was wrong.** Convenient method chosen without considering that the payload can be large.

**What was done.** Replaced with `if (!/\S/.test(line)) return;`, which scans for the first non-whitespace character without allocating, and a comment pointing at the C++ reader's matching decision.

**Why it matters.** Removes one full copy of every request body on the hot path; behaviour is identical (a line is blank exactly when it contains no non-whitespace character).


#### RR15 — Comment claimed the file-method handlers did not exist yet

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/rpc-types.ts`

**What was wrong.** The comment above the frozen file-method name table said "Handlers are added in the R-server workstream", written before those handlers existed. They have existed for some time, in the `fileMethods` table in `remote/src/files.ts`, which the dispatcher spreads into its method map. A reader looking for the implementation was pointed at a plan document instead of a file.

**Why it was wrong.** Comment not updated when the handlers landed.

**What was done.** Rewritten to say the handlers live in `files.ts` (the `fileMethods` table the dispatcher spreads into its method map) and that the string values are the frozen contract.

**Why it matters.** The contract file now points at the real implementation.


#### RR16 — Hook documentation and usage text omitted the environment-variable form of the event name

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts`

**What was wrong.** `readHookInput` accepts the harness-native event name either as the first positional argument or, failing that, from the `OMP_HOOK_EVENT` environment variable. Both the file header comment ("the native event name as the sole positional argument") and the usage string printed on misuse denied that second form existed, so a user integrating a harness that cannot pass arguments to hooks would not know the supported alternative.

**Why it was wrong.** The environment fallback was added without updating the two places that describe the interface.

**What was done.** Header comment now reads "as the positional argument — or, for a harness that cannot pass arguments to a hook, in $OMP_HOOK_EVENT". The usage string lists `[OMP_HOOK_EVENT]` alongside the other optional variables. A test now pins the fallback (`readHookInput falls back to OMP_HOOK_EVENT ...`).

**Why it matters.** The documented interface matches the implemented one, and the fallback is protected by a test.


#### RR17 — The Claude Code adapter had no tests at all

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/adapters.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** Of the three harness adapters, only the Oh My Pi one was tested. The Claude Code adapter — which is the only adapter that reads its event name from a `hook` field rather than a `type` field — had zero coverage, so a wrong or dropped mapping (for example the "Notification" hook, which is what signals that the agent is waiting for user input) would ship unnoticed.

**Why it was wrong.** Test file grew around one adapter only.

**What was done.** Added `claude-code adapter maps its lifecycle hook names`, asserting all eight hook names, the error override, an unrecognized hook mapping to nothing, and — importantly — that a `type`-keyed event is NOT mapped by this adapter, which pins the field-name split between harnesses.

**Why it matters.** The Claude Code state mapping is now a checked contract.


#### RR18 — The claim that the Pi adapter is identical to the Oh My Pi adapter was unverified

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/adapters.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** `remote/src/adapters/pi.ts` is a byte-for-byte copy of `oh-my-pi.ts` apart from its harness name, and its comment says the mapping "is currently identical" and is kept separate so the two can diverge deliberately. Nothing enforced that: editing one file alone — the likeliest way to break it — passed the suite while the two silently diverged.

**Why it was wrong.** Duplicated logic with no equivalence test.

**What was done.** Added `pi adapter mirrors the oh-my-pi mapping`, which runs thirteen native events (including the no-op, the error override, and the empty event) through both adapters and asserts equal results, plus that the Pi adapter reports the harness name "pi".

**Why it matters.** Accidental one-sided divergence now fails the suite; an intentional divergence requires editing this test, which is the point.


#### RR19 — No tests covered JSON-RPC envelope conformance

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/adapters.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** Existing tests covered a good request, an unknown method and a parse error. Nothing covered: wrong protocol version, missing method, a method that is not a string, an identifier of an illegal type, a batch array, non-object input, an identifier that is literally `null` (a legal identifier that must be echoed and must not be mistaken for the absent identifier marking a notification), or parameters of the wrong type.

**Why it was wrong.** Coverage focused on the happy path and one error path.

**What was done.** Added two tests plus one batch test. `dispatch rejects anything that is not a JSON-RPC 2.0 request object` loops over eight malformed inputs and asserts error code -32600 with identifier `null` in every case. `dispatch echoes a null id and rejects non-structured params` asserts the `null` identifier round-trip, the new -32602 rejection with the identifier preserved, that an unknown method outranks bad parameters, that an array is an acceptable structured parameter value, and that a notification with bad parameters yields no response at all. `handleLine answers a batch line with a single Invalid Request` pins the documented no-batch policy.

**Why it matters.** The protocol envelope, including the parts easiest to break by accident, is now covered.


#### RR20 — The agent-state token list existed twice in two languages with no drift gate

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/events.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The set of agent-status state tokens ("starting", "running", "waiting_input", "idle_unseen", "idle", "error", "stopped", "unknown") is written twice: as `AGENT_STATES` in `remote/src/events.ts` and inside `agentStateFromWireStrict()` in the C++ header `src/agent/AgentEvent.h`, whose comment claims it mirrors the TypeScript list. Neither language's test suite could see the other copy, so a token added or renamed on one side alone passed both suites and would only appear at run time as live events the desktop client silently drops. The repository already solves the same problem for method names in `remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts`, so the pattern existed and was simply not applied here.

**Why it was wrong.** Cross-language duplicated contract with no parity test.

**What was done.** Added `agent wire-state tokens match src/agent/AgentEvent.h`: it reads the C++ header, extracts the body of `agentStateFromWireStrict`, pulls the quoted tokens out of it, asserts the extraction was non-empty (so a regex that stopped matching cannot make the test vacuously pass), and compares the sorted token list against `AGENT_STATES`. I verified the extraction against the real header outside the test runner: it yields exactly the eight expected tokens. I also updated the comment at the top of `events.ts` to name this test and the C++ function.

**Why it matters.** Adding a state on one side alone now fails the remote test suite instead of degrading agent status at run time.


#### RR21 — The revision-mismatch error code existed twice with no parity check

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The application-level error code used to reject a write whose file changed underneath the editor is defined as -32001 both in `remote/src/rpc-types.ts` (`RPC_REVISION_MISMATCH`) and in `src/remote/RpcTypes.h` (`kRevisionMismatch`). The existing cross-language test compared only method-name strings, so a change to the number on one side would turn a precise "the file changed under you" conflict into an unrecognized generic failure in the editor.

**Why it was wrong.** The drift gate covered names but not the numeric code that travels the same wire.

**What was done.** Added `the revision-mismatch error code matches between TypeScript and C++`, which parses `inline constexpr int kRevisionMismatch = <n>;` out of the C++ header and compares it with the TypeScript constant, and additionally asserts the code lies inside the -32000..-32099 band JSON-RPC 2.0 reserves for implementation-defined server errors. I verified the parse against the real header (-32001).

**Why it matters.** Closes the numeric half of the same drift hole the method-name tests already close.


#### RR22 — Nothing checked that the server's schema version satisfies the client's minimum

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The desktop client refuses to adopt a remote service whose reported `schemaVersion` is below `AppController::kMinimumServerSchemaVersion` (currently 4, in `src/app/AppController.h`); the service reports `RPC_SCHEMA_VERSION` (currently 4, in `remote/src/codeharbord.ts`). Raising the client's floor without shipping a matching server bump makes every connection fail the compatibility gate — the client shows an error and an empty sidebar over a perfectly healthy SSH session, which is the exact failure that gate exists to explain. No test related the two numbers.

**Why it was wrong.** A compatibility floor in one language and the value it gates in another, with no shared check.

**What was done.** Added `the server's schemaVersion satisfies the client's minimum`, which parses `kMinimumServerSchemaVersion` out of `src/app/AppController.h` and asserts the server's `RPC_SCHEMA_VERSION` is greater than or equal to it, with a failure message quoting both numbers. Verified the parse yields 4 against the real header.

**Why it matters.** A version-skew mistake between the two halves of the product now fails the test suite instead of producing an unusable client.


#### RR23 — The hook's failure and misuse paths were untested

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/test/agent-hook.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** Existing hook tests all ran against a working bridge socket. The behaviour that actually matters for the specification's "never break the agent" rule — what happens when no bridge is listening, and what happens when the hook is invoked with no event name — had no coverage. Neither did the `OMP_HOOK_EVENT` fallback nor the requirement that only "1"/"true" count as an error flag.

**Why it was wrong.** Coverage of the happy path only.

**What was done.** Added three things. A small `captureStderr` helper (the hook reports failures there; capturing keeps the test output clean and lets the test assert on the report). `a missing bridge socket fails fast and main swallows it (SPEC 6.4)` points the hook at a socket path in a fresh empty temporary directory, asserts `emitHookEvent` rejects rather than hangs, then asserts the command-line entry point turns that into exactly one "oh-my-pi-hook: ..." line on standard error and returns normally. `main prints usage and touches no socket when the event name is missing` asserts the usage path. `readHookInput falls back to OMP_HOOK_EVENT and ignores a false OMP_ERROR` covers the environment fallback, the error-flag spellings, and that missing session coordinates degrade to empty strings instead of throwing.

**Why it matters.** The "never disturb the agent" contract is now defended by tests rather than by comments.


#### RR24 — The bridge ignores back-pressure when relaying events to its output stream

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/bridge.ts`

**What was wrong.** The bridge's default sink writes each mapped event with a plain write to standard output and ignores the return value, which is how a Node stream signals "my buffer is full, stop writing". It also never pauses the incoming socket. If the consumer at the other end of the SSH channel stalls while producers keep emitting, events queue in memory unboundedly.

**Why it was wrong.** Fire-and-forget writes with no flow control.

**What was done.** Not changed. Events are small single-line JSON messages emitted at human-interaction rates (a handful per agent turn), so the practical exposure is small, and a correct fix means threading pause/resume from the sink back into every accepted socket — a structural change to the sink signature that I cannot validate without running the suite. Recorded here so the limitation is a known one.

**Why it matters.** Unchanged today; noted for whoever raises the event rate (for example a future high-frequency progress event).


#### RR25 — The hook's metadata field can never be populated from a real hook invocation

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts`

**What was wrong.** `HookInput` declares an optional `metadata` object and `toBridgeMessage` faithfully copies it onto the outgoing message, but `readHookInput` — the only way the command-line hook builds its input — reads no environment variable for it. So in production the field is always absent; it is reachable only by calling the functions directly, as tests do.

**Why it was wrong.** The wire format supports metadata; the command-line front end never grew a way to supply it.

**What was done.** Not changed. The two candidate resolutions are opposite feature decisions (add an `OMP_METADATA` variable parsed as JSON, or delete the field from the hook's input type), and both are product choices rather than bug fixes; inventing either would exceed this review's remit. The field is harmless and correctly propagated where it is set.

**Why it matters.** None today. Flagged so it is not mistaken for a working feature.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Slice covered end to end: remote/src/codeharbord.ts, rpc-types.ts, events.ts, bridge.ts, hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts, all six files under remote/src/adapters/, and the four test files I own. I also read specification sections 6.1-6.6 and 10.1-10.3, and read (never edited) remote/src/files.ts, remote/src/workspace.ts, src/models/SessionState.h, src/agent/AgentEvent.h, src/remote/RpcTypes.h, src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp and src/app/AppController.h to check the cross-language contracts.

No build or test command was run, per instructions. The only command I executed was a read-only `node -e` snippet that (a) confirmed the percent-encoding mismatch behind finding RR1 on a path containing a space, and (b) confirmed that the three new regular expressions I added to tests actually match the real C++ headers and extract the expected values (eight agent-state tokens, -32001, and 4). No source file was compiled or executed.

I found no mismatch between the TypeScript contracts and their C++ mirror: the method-name sets, the agent-state tokens, the revision-mismatch code and the schema-version floor all currently agree. Three of my new tests exist precisely to keep that true.

Things I checked and found already correct, so no finding was raised: identifier handling for string, numeric and absent identifiers; the notification path swallowing handler errors; the reserved error-code range; the socket path resolution order in `resolveSocketPath` matching the specification exactly; the socket directory and socket permission tightening in `startBridge` (0700 directory, 0600 socket) and its refusal to unlink a non-socket; the Oh My Pi and Pi state mappings against the specification's table; and the fallback activity detector's clock-free, non-monotonic-safe design.

Uncertainty I want to be explicit about: RR6 (a handler returning nothing) and RR2 (a non-serializable payload) are latent, not reachable with today's handlers — I fixed them because both produce a protocol violation or a dead service rather than a contained error. RR13 (unbounded input line length) and RR12 (a live bridge socket being unlinked) I deliberately did not fix; both need structural changes I could not validate without running the suite, and both are now documented in code.

### 4.4 Desktop client — application layer (`AP`)

*Scope:* `src/app/`: the top-level controller, the SSH connect state machine (host-key prompt, credential prompt, retry chain), Dev Session bring-up, stored server profiles, pane-layout persistence, desktop notifications, and the object graph in `main.cpp`.

*10 findings:* 6 latent bug, 1 simplification, 1 nit, 1 missing test, 1 lint.

#### AP1 — The SSH connect pre-flight waits out its whole timeout budget (and blames the wrong thing) when the probe socket fails immediately

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Before doing the real (blocking) SSH handshake, `SessionBootstrap::probeEndpoint()` opens a throwaway TCP connection and waits for the remote server to say something, using a nested Qt event loop bounded by a deadline timer (5 seconds by default). The waiting is done by `QEventLoop::exec()`. Four callbacks can end the wait early, and each of them calls `QEventLoop::quit()`. The problem: `QEventLoop::quit()` called BEFORE `exec()` is entered is simply discarded by Qt — it does not make the subsequent `exec()` return immediately. `QTcpSocket::connectToHost()` can report failure synchronously (before it returns) for some inputs, e.g. an empty or already-cached-unresolvable host name, or an address family with no route. In that case the error callback fires, calls `quit()` into a loop that has not started, and the code then enters `exec()` anyway and sits there for the full connect budget. It finally exits via the deadline timer, so the failure is reported as "<host>:<port> did not answer within 5000 ms" (endpoint is alive but mute) instead of the accurate "cannot reach <host>:<port> — <socket error>".

**Why it was wrong.** `QEventLoop::quit()` is only honoured while the loop is running; the function unconditionally entered the loop without checking whether a verdict had already been reached.

**What was done.** Added a local `bool settled` flag that every one of the four callbacks (readyRead, errorOccurred, disconnected, deadline timeout) now sets alongside `loop.quit()`, and changed the loop entry to `if (!settled) loop.exec(...)`. With that, a verdict reached before the loop starts skips the loop entirely and falls straight into the existing error-classification block, which then produces the correct socket-error message.

**Why it matters.** Removes a possible multi-second freeze of the connect path plus a misleading diagnostic. I want to be explicit about reachability: with the current callers the host string is always non-empty (`ServerProfiles` refuses to store a blank host, and the environment-variable path checks it), so I could not construct a case that triggers this today — hence 'latent'. The fix is a pure guard and changes nothing on the normal asynchronous path.


#### AP2 — Desktop notifications: a late reply can make the next repeat rewrite the WRONG bubble

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/Notifier.cpp`, `src/app/Notifier.h`

**What was wrong.** `Notifier` shows OS desktop notifications and de-duplicates them: an identical (title, body) arriving within 5 seconds is 'coalesced', and if the notification service previously handed back an id for the currently tracked bubble, the repeat is re-sent with that id so the desktop refreshes the existing bubble in place instead of stacking a duplicate. The id comes back asynchronously: `deliver()` starts a non-blocking D-Bus call and a completion handler stores the returned id into the member `m_lastId`. Nothing tied that stored id to the notification it belonged to. So if two DIFFERENT notifications are raised in quick succession, there are two outstanding replies, both flagged as 'new bubble'. When the FIRST reply lands after the second notification has already become the tracked one, `m_lastId` ends up pointing at bubble #1 while the tracked (title, body) is #2. A subsequent repeat of notification #2 then re-issues the D-Bus call with bubble #1's id as `replaces_id`, so the desktop overwrites the first bubble with the second notification's text — one notification is silently destroyed and the other appears in the wrong place.

**Why it was wrong.** `m_lastId` was written by whichever asynchronous reply happened to arrive, with no check that the raise it belongs to is still the one being tracked.

**What was done.** Added a `quint64 m_raiseSerial` member, incremented once per raised bubble in `notify()`. `deliver()` snapshots the serial at call time into the completion handler, which now only adopts the returned id when `serial == m_raiseSerial` is still true (in addition to the pre-existing 'no error' and 'this was a new bubble, not a replacement' conditions). A reply belonging to a superseded raise is discarded, which degrades to the already-supported 'no id yet, just drop the repeat' behaviour.

**Why it matters.** Prevents cross-talk between two notifications raised close together. Only reachable with a real D-Bus notification daemon present, which is why it is latent and why I did not add a test: without a backend `m_lastId` is always 0 and the whole replacement path is inert, so a unit test in `tst_notifier.cpp` (which deliberately forces a bus-less environment) could not distinguish fixed from broken.


#### AP3 — An SSH password/passphrase the server never asked for stayed resident in the connection pool for the rest of the process

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp`, `src/app/AppController.h`

**What was wrong.** When the SSH login needs a password or a private-key passphrase, `AppController` refuses that attempt, asks the user, and then starts one fresh attempt with the typed secret captured by value inside a callback function it installs on the SSH connection pool. The pool keeps whatever callback is installed until it is replaced. The class header states the secret is 'used for exactly one attempt', and the callback does erase its copy the moment the SSH library consumes it. But if the library NEVER consumes it — for example the user types a private-key passphrase and the server only offers password authentication, so the passphrase is deliberately not offered — nothing erases it, and the plaintext secret sits inside the pool's stored callback until the user starts another connect (possibly never). Exactly the same applies to the host-key fingerprint the user approved: if the attempt failed before the key check, that approval stayed armed inside the callback, so a later attempt to a DIFFERENT host presenting a key with that identical fingerprint would have been auto-trusted.

**Why it was wrong.** The per-attempt callbacks were installed once at the start of an attempt and left in place afterwards, so any per-attempt payload they still held outlived the attempt.

**What was done.** Extracted the two callback installations from `startConnect()` into a new private helper `AppController::installPoolCallbacks(acceptedFingerprint, secret, secretKind)` (declared in the header, defined in the .cpp; the callback bodies and their explanatory comments are unchanged). `startConnect()` now calls it once with the attempt's payload before connecting, and again with both arguments EMPTY as soon as the connect call returns. Both callbacks therefore remain installed — which is required, because the automatic reconnect ladder re-does the SSH handshake through them and an unknown host key met with nobody waiting must still be refused — but they hold nothing. There is no behaviour change on the success path, because a consumed secret was already erased by the callback itself.

**Why it matters.** A secret now genuinely cannot outlive the single attempt it was typed for, matching what the code's own documentation promises and what the project's security section requires (no credentials retained). Also eliminates the stale host-key approval. Verified against the existing tests by reading them: `submittedSecretIsSpentOnceAndNeverPersistedOrLogged` re-invokes the installed credential callback after a failed attempt and asserts it yields no secret and arms no prompt — the freshly installed empty callback satisfies both, since by then the attempt is over.


#### AP4 — The 'no transport yet' guard in the sidebar reload did not actually protect against a null RPC client

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `AppController::refresh()` reloads the sidebar from the server. It began with `if (m_client && !m_client->transport()) return;` — i.e. 'if I have a client and it has no transport attached, do nothing'. When `m_client` is null the condition is false, so execution continues to `m_db->list(...)`, and `WorkspaceDb` holds and dereferences that same null client pointer. So the one case the `m_client &&` was presumably written to tolerate is precisely the case it fails to handle. Elsewhere in the same class (`adoptServerIdentity()`) a null client is handled with a plain `if (!m_client) return;`, so the class does contemplate a null client.

**Why it was wrong.** An inverted null check: the null case fell THROUGH the guard instead of being caught by it.

**What was done.** Changed the condition to `if (!m_client || !m_client->transport()) return;`. The surrounding comment (which explains that this is a capability check, not a filter on error text) still applies verbatim and was left alone.

**Why it matters.** Makes the guard consistent with the rest of the class and removes a null-pointer dereference. Latent, not active: every construction site in the application and in the tests passes a real client, so the null path is not reached today.


#### AP5 — Injecting the connection objects twice stacked duplicate signal handlers, doubling every connect error and every server identity lookup

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `AppController::setConnection(pool, bootstrap, profiles, layouts)` wires up handlers for the session bootstrapper's state/wired/error signals and the SSH pool's diagnostic signal. Before wiring, it removed old handlers only when the incoming object DIFFERED from the stored one (`if (m_bootstrap && m_bootstrap != bootstrap) disconnect(...)`). Calling it twice with the SAME objects therefore skipped the cleanup and added a second complete set of connections. Consequences: every successful wire would run the server-identity lookup twice (two `server.info` remote calls, two sidebar reloads), every connect failure would emit the user-facing `error` signal twice (two error toasts for one failure), and every state change would be mirrored twice. The existing comment claimed the code guarded against duplicates, which was true only for the different-object case.

**Why it was wrong.** The `!=` test made cleanup conditional on the object changing, but duplicate connections are created by re-wiring the SAME object.

**What was done.** Made the disconnect unconditional for a non-null stored pointer: `if (m_bootstrap) disconnect(m_bootstrap, nullptr, this, nullptr);` and likewise for the pool. Severing and immediately re-establishing the same connections is harmless, and `AppController` is the only thing that connects these signals to itself, so nothing else is collaterally severed. Replaced the now-wrong comment with one that states why the cleanup must be unconditional.

**Why it matters.** Removes duplicated error reporting and duplicated remote calls on re-injection. Latent: `main.cpp` and every test call `setConnection()` exactly once, so no duplicate exists today — this is the trap set for the next person who re-injects (e.g. to swap a layouts object).


#### AP6 — Changing the server left the previous server's split layout loaded and editable, so the next pane edit could be written into the wrong server's row

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp`, `src/app/SessionLayouts.h`

**What was wrong.** `SessionLayouts` caches the two pane-layout trees (viewer region, terminal region) for the currently selected workspace session, and persists edits with a remote call that takes BOTH the server id and the session id. `setServerId()` only replaced the server id; it left the currently selected session id and its loaded trees untouched. Since a workspace session belongs to exactly one server, that combination is invalid: the next structural edit (split a pane, close a pane, drag a splitter) would pass its permission check and then persist the OLD session's tree under the NEW server's key. `AppController` happens to prevent this from its own side (its `setServerId` also drops the active session), so the guarantee rested entirely on one caller doing two things in the right order — and `SessionLayouts` is also handed directly to the user interface layer, which can call `load()` on it itself.

**Why it was wrong.** The invariant 'a loaded session belongs to the configured server' was enforced by a caller instead of by the object that owns both fields.

**What was done.** `setServerId()` now deselects first when a session is loaded: `if (!m_devSessionId.isEmpty()) load(QString());`. That existing primitive is exactly right — it bumps the internal generation counter (so any in-flight layout reply is dropped), clears the session id, emits the id-changed notification, clears both region trees to their 'nothing loaded' state, and issues no network request. Documented the new behaviour on the declaration in the header.

**Why it matters.** Cross-server layout corruption is now impossible regardless of caller ordering. No behaviour change through the existing application path (the controller already cleared the session, so the follow-up clear is a no-op) and none in the existing tests, all of which set the server id before loading anything.


#### AP7 — Server switch duplicated the active-session teardown logic instead of calling the function that exists for it

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `AppController::setServerId()` open-coded three statements (clear the active session id, tell the layouts object to unload both region trees, emit the changed notification) that are byte-for-byte what the existing private helper `clearActiveSession(/*forget=*/false)` plus one emit already does. Two copies of the same teardown is how the two drift apart: anything added to `clearActiveSession()` later would silently not happen on a server switch.

**Why it was wrong.** The helper was introduced later than this call site and this call site was not migrated onto it.

**What was done.** Replaced the inline block with `clearActiveSession(/*forget=*/false); emit activeSessionChanged();` and a short comment saying why `forget` is false here (the session is not gone, it just is not this server's, so the previous server must keep remembering it).

**Why it matters.** One place to change instead of two; no behaviour change. I checked the argument carefully: `forget=false` is required, because `clearActiveSession(true)` would write the 'no active session' marker under the NEW server id (the new id is assigned earlier in the function), which would wrongly erase the incoming server's remembered session.


#### AP8 — The credential kind was decoded before it was validated

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `AppController::submitCredential(secret, kind)` takes the credential type as a string, either "password" or "keyPassphrase". It first mapped the string to the internal enumeration with `kind == "password" ? Password : KeyPassphrase` — which turns ANY unrecognised string into 'private-key passphrase' — and only afterwards rejected unrecognised strings. The end behaviour was correct because the rejection happened before the decoded value was used, but the reading order invites someone to move or reuse the decode and silently offer the typed secret to the wrong authentication method.

**Why it was wrong.** Validation placed after derivation.

**What was done.** Swapped the two blocks so the unknown-string rejection comes first, and the enumeration is only derived from a string already known to be one of the two accepted values. Added a comment stating why the order matters.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change; removes a footgun in security-relevant code.


#### AP9 — No in-process test covered the unknown-host-key prompt, its accept/reject answers, or the approval surviving a follow-up credential prompt

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/tests/tst_appcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The first-use host-key trust flow is the security-critical part of connecting: an unknown host key must be REFUSED (the SSH library is mid-handshake, so a dialog there would re-enter the user interface), shown to the user as a fingerprint, and the connection retried only after they approve — pinned to exactly the key they were shown. The companion credential flow had four dedicated unit tests, but the host-key flow had none. Its only coverage was the end-to-end acceptance gate, which requires a real SSH server (environment variable `CH_LIVE_SSH`) and is skipped in every normal test run. So on an ordinary run, nothing checked that an unknown key is refused, that accepting retries exactly once with the right key pinned, that rejecting ends the attempt without leaving an approval armed, or that an approval given before an authentication prompt survives the retry that follows it.

**Why it was wrong.** The flow was only exercisable through a live server until the test file grew a fake bootstrapper seam (`FakeBootstrap::duringConnect`) that lets a test stand exactly where the SSH library consults the installed policy; that seam was used for credentials but never for host keys.

**What was done.** Added a small helper pair in the test file's private namespace — a fixed fake key blob, the base64 SHA-256 fingerprint computed from it the same way the controller does, and `offerUnknownHostKey(pool)` which invokes the installed policy for an unknown host — plus three tests. (1) `hostKeyPromptParksTheAttemptAndAcceptRetriesWithItPinned`: the key is refused, the prompt carries the right host, key type and fingerprint, the state becomes "hostkey", NO error is reported (an expected refusal is not a fault), a second Connect while the prompt is parked is swallowed, and accepting produces exactly one retry in which the same key is accepted and no second prompt appears. (2) `rejectedHostKeyEndsTheAttemptAndLeavesNothingPinned`: rejecting retries nothing and lands in "disconnected", a stale sheet answering a second time cannot redial, and the next real connect meets the same key as unknown again — proving the rejected fingerprint was not left armed. (3) `hostKeyApprovalSurvivesTheCredentialRetryInTheSameChain`: key approved, then authentication asks for a passphrase, then the passphrase retry meets the same key as unknown a third time and must trust it silently — because an attempt that stopped at authentication never got as far as writing the newly trusted key to the known-hosts file. Each test fails on a plausible regression of the code it covers.

**Why it matters.** The unknown-host-key contract is now defended on every ordinary test run instead of only when a live SSH fixture is configured.


#### AP10 — Include hygiene: an unused include and a type used without its own include

- **Kind:** Lint
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/Notifier.cpp`, `src/app/AppController.h`

**What was wrong.** Two small header problems. (1) `Notifier.cpp` included `<QVariantList>`, which nothing in the file uses; the file does use `QStringList` and `QVariantMap`, and `QStringList` was only reaching it indirectly through that unrelated include. (2) `AppController.h` declares a `QPair<QString, QString>` member and the .cpp calls `qMakePair`, but neither file included `<QPair>` — it compiled only because some other header happened to drag it in, which is exactly the kind of dependency that breaks when an unrelated include is tidied.

**Why it was wrong.** Includes drifted out of step with what the files actually use.

**What was done.** Replaced `#include <QVariantList>` with `#include <QStringList>` in `Notifier.cpp`, and added `#include <QPair>` to the existing alphabetically ordered include block in `AppController.h`.

**Why it matters.** Both files now include what they use; no behaviour change.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Went through all nine target source files plus the two CMake files and the test files, and cross-checked against docs/SPEC.md sections 4 (application shell/sidebar), 10.3 (RPC) and 12 (SSH trust and credentials). Ten findings, all fixed.

As instructed I ran NO build or test command. Every change was checked by reading: each edited declaration against its header (`installPoolCallbacks` added to AppController.h and defined with the matching file-local `CredentialKind` alias exactly as the neighbouring `startConnect` does; `setServerId` documentation updated in SessionLayouts.h alongside the .cpp change; the new `m_raiseSerial` member added to Notifier.h before use in Notifier.cpp), and each existing test that touches changed behaviour was re-read to confirm it still holds. In particular the credential-lifetime change (AP3) was validated against `submittedSecretIsSpentOnceAndNeverPersistedOrLogged`, which re-invokes the installed callback after a failed attempt — the freshly installed empty callback yields no secret and arms no prompt, which is what that test asserts — and against the live cold-start gate, whose host-key assertions depend on the callback merely remaining installed (it does) and on the key having been written to the known-hosts file after a successful connect (unchanged).

Things I examined and found genuinely sound, so no finding was invented for them: the destruction order in main.cpp (the QML engine is declared last so it is destroyed first, and the reverse-declaration unwinding really does put the pane factories before the SSH pool, the session bootstrapper before the pool whose channels it opened, and the RPC client and agent monitor last — the long comment there is accurate); every asynchronous callback in AppController and SessionLayouts already uses a weak pointer or a context object so it cannot fire into a destroyed object; the two monotonic generation counters (sidebar reload, layout load) correctly drop superseded out-of-order replies while still reporting errors; the connect state machine's ordering (host key first, then credentials, with the approval carried across the credential retry) is correct and its long explanatory comments are true; ServerProfiles' validation, insertion-ordering, active-selection and permission-narrowing logic all match their documentation; UiStateStore's per-server scoping and its deliberate 'no disk flush on width changes' choice are correct and tested; the split-tree ratio invariant that closePane relies on is genuinely enforced by the tree parser, so the unchecked index removal there is safe.

One judgement call worth stating: I considered converting `SessionBootstrap::m_pool` from a raw pointer to a weak pointer for symmetry with its two sibling members, and decided against it. The pool is a constructor-required collaborator, every use site already null-checks it, the destruction order that keeps it valid is enforced and explicitly documented in main.cpp, and the change would be churn with no behavioural benefit. I would rather report that than make a cosmetic edit to a file another agent may also be reading.

### 4.5 Desktop client — SSH transport (`SH`)

*Scope:* `src/ssh/`: the libssh connection pool (host-key verification and authentication), the byte-stream device wrapping one SSH channel, and known-hosts parsing.

*16 findings:* 5 latent bug, 4 active bug, 3 footgun, 1 inconsistency, 1 documentation error, 1 nit, 1 missing test.

#### SH1 — Every finished SSH channel stayed allocated, so a session ran out of channel slots and wedged

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h`, `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h`, `src/ssh/tests/tst_livessh.cpp`

**What was wrong.** An SSH connection multiplexes independent 'channels' (one per remote command, per terminal, per background helper). Servers cap how many a single connection may have open at once — OpenSSH's MaxSessions is 10 by default. In this code the connection pool (SshConnectionPool) allocated a channel with ssh_channel_new()/ssh_channel_open_session() and remembered it in a list; the QIODevice wrapper (SshChannelDevice) that used it only ever called ssh_channel_close() when done, and nothing ever freed the channel or removed it from the pool's list before the entire SSH connection was torn down. Worse, once the remote command had exited, ssh_channel_is_open() already returned false, so closeChannel() and the destructor did not even send the close — the channel just sat there. Because the desktop client opens a fresh channel per remote command (each terminal attach, each 'tmux kill-session', each JSON-RPC start attempt), the connection permanently accumulated dead channels and the eleventh remote command silently failed to open a channel, leaving the whole session half-working. A comment in an unrelated live test (src/app/tests/tst_liveeditor.cpp around line 595) already described this exact symptom: 'a finished exec channel does NOT give its slot back ... A channel per command therefore wedges the whole session part-way through the run.'

**Why it was wrong.** The pool had no API to hand a channel back. Its documented ownership rule was 'the pool frees every channel it handed out when the session goes down', which is only correct for channels that live as long as the session; there was no path for a short-lived channel to be reclaimed earlier. Three code paths leaked: SshChannelDevice::closeChannel(), SshChannelDevice::~SshChannelDevice(), and SshChannelDevice::abortStart() (the failure path when the exec/PTY request on a freshly opened channel fails — which a reconnect loop retries repeatedly).

**What was done.** Added SshConnectionPool::releaseChannel(ssh_channel): it removes the handle from the pool's list (using QList::removeOne, whose false return also makes a double release a harmless no-op), closes the channel if still open, and frees it. SshChannelDevice now calls it from closeChannel(), from its destructor, and from abortStart() instead of merely closing. Header documentation on both classes was rewritten to describe the hand-back rule. Added a live regression test, TstLiveSsh::manyShortLivedChannelsReuseTheirSlots(), which runs 25 short-lived exec channels one after another on ONE session and asserts each opens and delivers its output; against the old code this failed at roughly the eleventh channel.

**Why it matters.** Before: after about ten remote commands on one connection, opening any further channel failed, so terminals would not attach and remote commands stopped running until the user reconnected. After: each channel's slot is returned as soon as the device using it is closed or destroyed, so a long-lived session survives unlimited short-lived commands.


#### SH2 — A byte-stream device that outlived its SSH session kept reading from freed libssh memory (use-after-free)

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h`, `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h`

**What was wrong.** SshChannelDevice reads from its channel on a repeating 10 ms timer (it polls, rather than watching a socket, because all channels share one socket). When the connection pool closed the session — either on disconnect or at the start of every reconnect attempt — it freed every channel and then the session itself, while any still-living SshChannelDevice went on holding the now-dangling ssh_channel pointer and went on calling ssh_channel_read_nonblocking() on it from the timer. That is a read of freed memory: undefined behaviour, in practice a crash or silent corruption. Safety depended entirely on every owner destroying its devices before the session went down. The JSON-RPC/agent devices did that, but terminal panes deliberately survive an SSH drop and reattach afterwards (specification section 5.6), and nothing tore their PTY devices down when the session closed. Two existing live tests contain comments showing the authors knew the hazard and worked around it by hand ('the retry calls connectToHost(), which frees every channel on the old session — including this killer's — so the device must be gone by then').

**Why it was wrong.** Channel lifetime was managed only from the pool side, with no notification to the holders of the handles, and SshChannelDevice held a raw, non-tracking pointer to the pool, so it could not even tell whether the pool still existed.

**What was done.** SshConnectionPool now emits a new signal, sessionClosing(), from closeSession() immediately before any channel is freed. SshChannelDevice connects to it in its constructor and answers by running its own closeChannel(), which stops the poll timer, hands the channel back and clears its handle. The pool's member holding the session is also detached into a local before the signal is emitted, so a handler that asks for another disconnect cannot start a second teardown of the same session, and the channel sweep afterwards works on a snapshot taken once the handlers have settled. Finally, SshChannelDevice's pool pointer became a QPointer, which self-clears if the pool is destroyed first, so the device never dereferences a dead pool.

**Why it matters.** An SSH drop with a live terminal pane no longer risks reading freed libssh memory; the device closes cleanly and reports end-of-stream so the owner can show the disconnect and reattach. The correctness of the client no longer depends on every caller getting teardown order right.


#### SH3 — Wildcard known-hosts entries were treated as literal text, so a changed key at a wildcard-covered host got the friendly 'new host' prompt instead of a hard refusal

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/KnownHosts.cpp`, `src/ssh/KnownHosts.h`, `src/ssh/tests/tst_knownhosts.cpp`

**What was wrong.** OpenSSH's known_hosts file allows the host column to be a pattern rather than a literal name: '*' matches any run of characters and '?' exactly one, so '*.example.com ssh-ed25519 AAAA...' trusts one key for every host in that domain. This code compared the stored host column to the looked-up host with plain string equality, so such an entry matched nothing at all. The verdict for a host covered only by a pattern was therefore 'Unknown' (never seen before) rather than 'the host is trusted'. 'Unknown' is the one verdict that reaches the user-approval callback and the one after which the presented key is written into the trust store. The result: for a user whose known_hosts uses wildcards, an attacker impersonating build.example.com would be greeted with the reassuring 'new host — do you trust this fingerprint?' dialog instead of the outright refusal the security requirements (specification section 12.1) demand, and an approval would persist the attacker's key. This is exactly the downgrade the file already defends against for key types (see the existing test trustedHostRefusesUnknownKeyType), just reached through the host column instead.

**Why it was wrong.** KnownHosts implemented only literal host matching; the header and comments described the file format as 'host, key-type, base64 key' with no notion of the pattern syntax OpenSSH actually permits there.

**What was done.** Added OpenSSH-compatible matching in KnownHosts.cpp: hasHostPatternSyntax() detects a host field using '*', '?' or '!'; globMatches() implements OpenSSH's match_pattern() ('*' any run, '?' exactly one, case-insensitive) iteratively with a single backtrack point so no recursion or catastrophic blow-up is possible; patternListMatches() applies it across a comma-separated list. entryHostMatches() routes pattern fields there. Pattern entries are stored verbatim as one entry and marked unsupported for replacement, exactly as hashed entries already were, so they round-trip through serialize() untouched and add() never rewrites them. New test wildcardHostIsATrustedHost() covers match, changed key, changed key type, non-covered hosts, single-character '?', and a wildcard @revoked entry.

**Why it matters.** A user whose known_hosts uses wildcard entries now gets the hard refusal for a key that does not match, instead of a first-use prompt that could be used to install an attacker's key. Hosts genuinely outside the pattern are still treated as first use, so nothing legitimate becomes unreachable.


#### SH4 — Splitting a comma-separated host list would have destroyed OpenSSH's '!' exclusions, trusting the exact hosts the file carves out

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/KnownHosts.cpp`, `src/ssh/tests/tst_knownhosts.cpp`

**What was wrong.** A known_hosts line may list several hosts separated by commas, and OpenSSH lets one of those tokens start with '!' to EXCLUDE the hosts it names from the rest of the line — '*.example.com,!secret.example.com' means 'this key is valid for the whole domain except secret.example.com'. The parser expanded every comma-separated list into one independent entry per token. On its own that was harmless (no token was ever pattern-matched, see SH3), but the moment pattern matching exists, splitting is actively wrong: the '!secret.example.com' token becomes a nonsense host name of its own that matches nothing, while the surviving '*.example.com' entry happily trusts secret.example.com — that is, a key accepted for precisely the host the administrator excluded.

**Why it was wrong.** The parser flattened the host column into independent per-name entries, a representation that cannot express a negation because negation is a property of the whole list, not of one token.

**What was done.** parse() now keeps a host field containing '*', '?' or '!' verbatim in a single entry (the same treatment hashed '|1|' fields already got) and patternListMatches() evaluates the whole list: any match on a negated token rejects the entry outright, regardless of token order, while one positive match accepts it. Plain comma lists with no pattern syntax still expand per name, so existing behaviour and tests are unchanged. New test negatedWildcardHostIsExcluded() asserts the covered host matches, the excluded host stays first-use, the line stays a single entry, and the field round-trips through serialization.

**Why it matters.** Prevents the wildcard support added in SH3 from silently trusting hosts the known_hosts file deliberately excludes.


#### SH5 — Host names were compared case-sensitively, unlike OpenSSH, so a changed key at a differently-cased name read as first use

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/KnownHosts.cpp`, `src/ssh/tests/tst_knownhosts.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The store compared the stored host name to the looked-up host with an exact, case-sensitive string comparison, and an existing test locked that in with the justification 'OpenSSH matches hostnames case-sensitively'. That justification is factually wrong: OpenSSH's match_pattern() compares host names with tolower() on both sides. The practical consequence of being stricter here is not extra safety but the opposite: if the file holds 'Host.Example' and the connection is made as 'host.example', the store answered 'Unknown' (never seen), so a key that does NOT match the trusted one was offered to the user as a friendly first-use prompt rather than refused, and accepting it appended a second, contradictory line for the same machine.

**Why it was wrong.** Comparison used QString equality; the accompanying test comment asserted an incorrect fact about OpenSSH's behaviour, so the divergence looked intentional.

**What was done.** Plain host names are now compared with QString::compare(..., Qt::CaseInsensitive), and the pattern matcher folds case too. KnownHosts::add() uses the same case-insensitive rule when deciding whether it is replacing an existing entry, so re-trusting a host under a different spelling overwrites the old key instead of appending a contradictory line. The stored spelling itself is preserved on round-trip. The test was renamed hostnamesMatchCaseInsensitively(), its incorrect claim about OpenSSH replaced with the real rule and the security reason for it, and it now also asserts the add()-replacement behaviour.

**Why it matters.** A changed key can no longer slip through as first use because of a capitalisation difference between the stored line and the configured host name, and the trust file cannot accumulate two conflicting entries for the same machine.


#### SH6 — An unreadable server host key was treated as a brand-new host instead of being refused

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** During host-key verification the pool asks libssh for the server's key type name and for the key itself as base64 text, then decodes the base64 to raw bytes and compares those bytes against the trust store. Two failure modes were unhandled. First, the base64 was decoded with the lenient QByteArray::fromBase64() overload, which silently skips characters it does not understand instead of failing — so a corrupt export could produce bytes that are not the key the server actually presented, and those bytes would be compared and possibly stored. Second, if libssh could not name the key type (ssh_key_type_to_char() returning null) or the decode produced nothing, verification carried on with an empty key type and/or an empty key. An empty key type does not match any stored entry, so the store answered 'Unknown' — the verdict that prompts the user to trust the host and then writes the key to disk. The written line would have two spaces where the key type belongs, which collapses into too few fields when re-read, so the entry is dropped on the next launch and the user is asked to trust the host all over again.

**Why it was wrong.** Missing validation between 'libssh handed us something' and 'this is a key we can compare', combined with a base64 decoder that never reports failure.

**What was done.** verifyHostKey() now decodes with QByteArray::fromBase64Encoding() using AbortOnBase64DecodingErrors, and refuses the connection outright — emitting a clear error, before any credential is sent — if the key type name is empty or the key bytes could not be decoded. The comment explains why 'Unknown' would have been the dangerous answer.

**Why it matters.** A host key the client cannot faithfully read is refused rather than presented to the user as a new, trustworthy host, and the trust file can no longer receive a line that silently disappears on the next launch.


#### SH7 — Passwords and key passphrases were left in freed heap memory

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** When the user supplies a private-key passphrase or an account password, the pool converts the text to a UTF-8 byte buffer to hand to libssh (ssh_userauth_publickey_auto, ssh_userauth_password, ssh_pki_import_privkey_file). Those buffers were plain locals that were simply released when the function returned, leaving the plaintext secret sitting in freed heap memory for the remainder of the process's life, where it can surface in a crash dump or core file. The project's security requirements are explicit that credentials must not be persisted or logged (specification sections 12.1 and 12.3).

**Why it was wrong.** No scrubbing step existed; the buffers were declared const and forgotten.

**What was done.** Added a small file-local helper, wipeSecret(QByteArray&), which overwrites the buffer with zero bytes, and called it immediately after each libssh call that consumes a secret (both authentication attempts and the private-key import). The helper's comment is deliberately honest about the limit of the technique: only the locally owned UTF-8 copy can be scrubbed, because the QString it was converted from is reference-counted and shared with the caller that supplied it.

**Why it matters.** The window during which a plaintext credential exists in this process's memory now ends when the authentication attempt ends, instead of lasting until the process exits.


#### SH8 — Failures to apply SSH options (host, port, user, private key file) were discarded

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Four calls to libssh's ssh_options_set() — for the host, the port, the user name and the configured private key file — ignored their return value. If one failed, the setting simply never reached the session, and the only symptom was a later, misleading failure: a private key file that was never applied looks exactly like a private key the server rejected, which sends the user off debugging the wrong thing. The class already maintains a user-visible diagnostic transcript for exactly this kind of explanation.

**Why it was wrong.** Unchecked C-API return values on a code path whose failures are otherwise invisible.

**What was done.** Introduced a small local helper in connectToHost() that applies an option and, on failure, appends a diagnostic line naming the option and libssh's own error text (the private-key case names the file path). All four call sites now go through it.

**Why it matters.** A setting that could not be applied is now visible in the connection log the user can read and copy, instead of masquerading as a server-side rejection.


#### SH9 — Session teardown could re-enter itself and free the same session twice

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** closeSession() freed the channels, disconnected, freed the session and only then cleared the member holding it. Combined with the new teardown notification (see SH2) — and, before that change, with any callback reachable from ssh_channel_close() — a handler that responded by asking for another disconnect would have re-entered closeSession() while the member still pointed at a session the outer call was in the middle of destroying, freeing it twice.

**Why it was wrong.** The member variable holding the session stayed valid for the whole duration of the teardown, so re-entry could not be detected.

**What was done.** closeSession() now moves the session handle into a local and clears the member as its first action, so a nested call sees no session and returns immediately; the remaining work uses the local. The channel list is likewise snapshotted and cleared before the sweep, so handlers that hand channels back during the notification cannot mutate the list being iterated. This also means nothing can open a fresh channel on a session that is going away, because openChannel() checks the member.

**Why it matters.** Teardown is now safe to trigger from a handler reached during teardown, which is what the new sessionClosing() notification makes reachable.


#### SH10 — resizePty() sent a terminal-resize request on channels that have no terminal, contradicting its own documentation

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h`

**What was wrong.** The header documents resizePty() as 'Push a new window size to the remote PTY. False if no PTY channel is live.' The implementation only checked that a channel existed, so calling it on a plain command channel (no pseudo-terminal) issued a window-change request that the remote side has no terminal for, and reported whatever libssh returned. The documented contract was simply not implemented.

**Why it was wrong.** There was no record of whether a pseudo-terminal had actually been negotiated on the channel.

**What was done.** Added a private m_hasPty flag, set when startPty() successfully requests or resizes the pseudo-terminal and cleared whenever the channel is closed or a start is aborted. resizePty() now returns false unless a channel is live AND it has a pseudo-terminal, which is exactly what the header promises.

**Why it matters.** No stray protocol requests on non-terminal channels, and the documented return-value contract is now real, so a future caller can rely on it.


#### SH11 — Comment claimed host-key verification reuses a token that 'pinned the algorithm list' — no such pinning exists

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** In verifyHostKey(), the comment above the trust-store lookup ended with 'The SAME token pinned the algorithm list in connectToHost().' connectToHost() does not pin any host-key algorithm list; the live test unavailableTrustedAlgorithmStillReachesHostVerification() exists precisely to prove it must not (pinning a stored key type the local libssh build does not support would kill the handshake before verification could reject the key). The comment described code that no longer exists and pointed a reader at a design that was deliberately removed.

**Why it was wrong.** Comment left behind when the host-key algorithm pinning was removed.

**What was done.** Replaced the stale sentence with the fact that actually matters at that line: the lookup uses the profile's port, not whatever the user's OpenSSH configuration file asked for, because connectToHost() re-applies the profile port after parsing that file — so the token names the port actually connected to.

**Why it matters.** A reader is no longer sent looking for pinning code that was intentionally removed, and now sees the real reason the port used in the lookup is correct.


#### SH12 — A host-key refusal was logged with an unrelated or empty libssh error appended

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** When verification refused a key, the connection transcript recorded 'Host-key verification failed: ' followed by whatever ssh_get_error() happened to hold. A refusal is CodeHarbor's own policy decision, so libssh usually has nothing to say and the line ended in a bare colon — or, worse, carried a stale message from an earlier step, making the transcript read as though libssh had failed.

**Why it was wrong.** Unconditional interpolation of a diagnostic string that is frequently empty or unrelated on this path.

**What was done.** The libssh error is trimmed and only appended when non-empty; otherwise the line is simply 'Host-key verification failed.'

**Why it matters.** The user-visible connection log no longer implies a libssh failure when the client itself refused the key on policy grounds.


#### SH13 — Private key object would leak if libssh ever returned one alongside an import failure

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** authenticateIdentityFile() called ssh_pki_import_privkey_file() and, on 'result is not OK OR no key returned', returned false without freeing anything. The first half of that condition can be true while a key object exists, in which case the key was leaked. Current libssh does not hand back a key on failure, so this is not reachable today, but the code reads as if it deliberately handles the case and does not.

**Why it was wrong.** A combined error condition that discards one of the two possible states instead of cleaning it up.

**What was done.** The failure branch now frees the key if the pointer is non-null before returning false, with a comment stating plainly that libssh does not currently do this and the free is defensive.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change today; removes a leak that would appear silently if the library's contract changed, on the code path used for every locally unlocked private key.


#### SH14 — Servers that offer only keyboard-interactive authentication cannot be used at all

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`

**What was wrong.** authenticate() tries, in order: the 'none' method, the SSH agent, automatic public-key authentication, a public key unlocked with a passphrase from the user, and finally a plain password. It never attempts keyboard-interactive authentication (libssh's ssh_userauth_kbdint). A great many servers publish the user's password challenge through keyboard-interactive (via PAM) and refuse the plain 'password' method outright — configurations with PasswordAuthentication no and KbdInteractiveAuthentication yes are common. Against such a server CodeHarbor cannot authenticate at all with a password the user is perfectly willing to type; authentication simply fails after the credential prompt.

**Why it was wrong.** The authentication ladder covers only the methods that map one-to-one onto a single secret; keyboard-interactive is a multi-prompt challenge/response protocol and was never implemented.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED, deliberately. A correct implementation is a loop over ssh_userauth_kbdint / ssh_userauth_kbdint_getnprompts / ssh_userauth_kbdint_getprompt / ssh_userauth_kbdint_setanswer that can be re-entered several times, and the prompt text and echo flag come from the server — so the existing credential callback, which asks for exactly one secret of a fixed kind ('key passphrase' or 'password'), cannot express it. Doing it properly means extending that callback contract and the desktop prompt in src/app/AppController.cpp and the QML connect sheet, which belong to other owners, and I am not permitted to run a build or test to validate a new libssh call sequence. Writing an untested single-prompt approximation risked being worse than the honest gap. Recommend routing as a follow-up feature that spans the SSH layer and the connect UI.

**Why it matters.** Users of PAM/keyboard-interactive-only servers cannot connect with a password. Agent and key-based authentication are unaffected, which is the documented preferred path.


#### SH15 — The SSH handshake and channel writes run synchronously on the calling (user-interface) thread

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp`

**What was wrong.** connectToHost() performs the whole TCP connect, key exchange, host-key check and authentication with blocking libssh calls on the calling thread, which in the shipped application is the thread that draws the user interface; the class header states this synchronous design explicitly. Similarly, SshChannelDevice::writeData() uses the blocking ssh_channel_write(), which can stall if the remote side stops consuming. An unreachable or very slow server therefore freezes the window for as long as libssh takes to give up.

**Why it was wrong.** Deliberate synchronous design of the handshake, combined with libssh's default blocking session mode.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. Making the handshake asynchronous is an architectural change well beyond a bug fix, and it would ripple into the connect state machine in src/app (another owner). Two things already bound the damage: the caller performs a plain TCP reachability probe with its own timeout before attempting SSH (SessionBootstrap::probeEndpoint), and libssh applies a default packet timeout when none is configured. I did NOT set an explicit SSH_OPTIONS_TIMEOUT, because doing so would silently override any ConnectTimeout the user set in their own OpenSSH configuration file, and libssh offers no way to ask whether that value was already provided. UNCERTAIN: I did not verify libssh's exact default timeout value in the linked version, so I cannot state the worst-case freeze duration.

**Why it matters.** A user pointing the client at a black-holed address can see the window stop responding for the duration of libssh's timeout. Not a correctness bug; recorded so the next person changing this code knows the constraint rather than discovering it.


#### SH16 — No tests covered wildcard/negated/differently-cased known-hosts entries or channel-slot reclamation

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/tests/tst_knownhosts.cpp`, `src/ssh/tests/tst_livessh.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The known-hosts test suite was thorough about hashed entries, markers, revocation precedence, malformed base64 and key-type downgrades, but had no case for the pattern syntax OpenSSH permits in the host column, and its one case about letter case asserted behaviour that diverged from OpenSSH for a stated reason that was factually wrong. Nothing anywhere covered whether a finished channel gives its slot back to the connection.

**Why it was wrong.** The pattern-matching and slot-reclamation behaviours did not exist, so nothing tested them.

**What was done.** Added three tests that require no server: wildcardHostIsATrustedHost() (match, changed key, changed key type, non-covered hosts, single-character '?', wildcard revocation, verbatim round-trip), negatedWildcardHostIsExcluded() (the excluded host stays first-use, the line stays one entry, the field round-trips), and hostnamesMatchCaseInsensitively() (replacing the old case-sensitivity test, and additionally covering add()'s replacement rule). Added one live test, manyShortLivedChannelsReuseTheirSlots(), which opens and closes 25 sequential command channels on a single session; it self-skips like every other live test when the CH_LIVE_SSH environment variable is unset. Each of these fails against the pre-fix code: the wildcard and negation cases returned 'Unknown' where they now demand a refusal, the case test returned 'Unknown' where it now demands a match, and the live test failed at roughly the eleventh channel.

**Why it matters.** The security-relevant matching rules and the channel-lifetime contract are now defended by tests that fail on a plausible regression rather than being implicit in comments.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope was src/ssh only (connection pool, channel device, known-hosts store, and their tests plus CMake files); the CMake files needed no change. Per instructions I ran NO build, test, or compiler command, so nothing here is claimed as compile- or run-verified. Instead: every edit was re-read after applying, every new or changed declaration was cross-checked against its header (SshConnectionPool::releaseChannel, the sessionClosing signal, the QPointer pool member, the m_hasPty member), brace balance was checked mechanically on all five touched files, and the private-slot declaration list of both test files was diffed against their definition list (both matched exactly). The two highest-impact findings, SH1 (channel slots never reclaimed) and SH2 (use-after-free on a device outliving its session), are corroborated by comments already in the tree written by whoever hit the symptoms: src/app/tests/tst_liveeditor.cpp around line 595 describes the channel exhaustion, and src/app/tests/tst_livereconnect.cpp around line 160 plus src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditorreconnect.cpp around line 354 describe hand-ordering device destruction to dodge the freed-channel hazard. Behaviour changes a reviewer should look at with care, since I could not run the suite: (1) the SSH pool now emits sessionClosing() during teardown, which makes channel devices close and emit end-of-stream at that point — SessionBootstrap already treats a session-wide drop as a loss via the pool's own state change, so this should not add a new reconnect trigger, but it is the change most likely to surprise; (2) known-hosts host matching became case-insensitive, which required rewriting one existing test's assertions (that test's stated justification about OpenSSH was wrong, see SH5); (3) resizePty() now returns false on a channel with no pseudo-terminal, which only the terminal controller calls and only after a successful startPty(). Two findings are honestly left unfixed with reasons (SH14 keyboard-interactive authentication, SH15 blocking calls on the user-interface thread), and SH15's worst-case duration is explicitly marked uncertain because I did not verify libssh's default timeout value.

### 4.6 Desktop client — JSON-RPC client (`RC`)

*Scope:* `src/remote/`: the client half of the JSON-RPC 2.0 conversation over an SSH channel, plus the C++ mirror of the request/response types.

*14 findings:* 6 latent bug, 3 nit, 2 documentation error, 1 missing test, 1 simplification, 1 footgun.

#### RC1 — Swapping or detaching the transport silently abandoned every in-flight request callback

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** CodeharbordClient sends requests over a byte stream (an SSH channel in production) and remembers each one in a map keyed by the numeric request id, together with the callback to invoke when the answer comes back. Its header promised that 'the callback fires exactly once' and that 'no callback is ever registered that cannot fire'. setTransport() broke that promise: it cleared the read buffer and rewired its signal hooks but left the map of outstanding callbacks completely untouched, and its own comment even advertised this ('in-flight pending callbacks are preserved'). Preserving them is not harmless. The remembered ids were minted for, and are only known to, the process on the other end of the stream that was just dropped. When the desktop client reconnects (specification section 5.6) it starts a brand-new copy of the remote service, whose request table has never heard of those ids, so the answers can never arrive. When the transport is simply detached (setTransport(nullptr)) there is nobody left to answer at all. Either way the callbacks sat in the map forever and their callers waited forever.

**Why it was wrong.** setTransport() treated the pending-request map as transport-independent state, when in fact every entry in it is a promise made to one specific peer process. Production code hid the problem by carefully closing the channel first (SessionBootstrap::unwire() closes before detaching, which routes through the transport-closed path and fails everything), so the orphaning was only reachable when a caller detached without closing first — which one live test (src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp) actually does.

**What was done.** setTransport() now rewires the hooks first, then sweeps the pending map and invokes every outstanding callback once with a synthetic internal-error (-32603) failure whose message is 'transport replaced with request pending'. The sweep runs after the new transport is installed and after the internal closed flag is cleared, so a callback that reacts by immediately retrying writes onto the NEW transport rather than being rejected; failAllPending() moves the map out before iterating, so the retry's fresh entry survives its own sweep. Deliberately no transportClosed() signal is emitted for a swap or detach — whoever called setTransport() already knows — and that distinction is now spelled out in the header. Three new tests pin the behaviour: setTransportTwiceDetachesOld (rewritten), detachingTransportFailsPendingOnce, retryFromRebindFailureUsesNewTransport.

**Why it matters.** Removes a class of permanent hangs where a user action (save a file, list sessions) never completes and never errors after a reconnect or teardown races an in-flight request. In current production call paths this is a no-op because the channel is always closed first, so the change is safe; it converts a latent forever-hang into a clean, reported failure.


#### RC2 — Any message containing a "method" field was treated as a server notification, even when it also carried a response id

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Incoming messages are either answers to requests the client sent (identified by an id) or one-way notifications pushed by the server (for example 'a watched file changed'). processLine() decided this with a single check: if the message object contains a member named 'method', emit it as a notification and stop. Its own comment claimed a notification 'carries no id', but that was never verified. So a message carrying BOTH a method name and an id — which the JSON-RPC 2.0 standard defines as a request aimed at the client, and which is also what a buggy server would produce if it echoed the method name back on a response — was swallowed as a notification. The caller waiting for that id was never told anything and hung forever.

**Why it was wrong.** The notification branch tested only for the presence of a method name, not for the absence of an id, so it shadowed the entire response-routing path below it. The code and its comment disagreed, and only the comment was right.

**What was done.** The notification branch now requires the method name to actually be a string AND the id to be absent or explicitly null (which is what JSON-RPC 2.0 section 4.1 defines a notification as). Anything else falls through to the response path, where it is either matched to a pending request and failed with a clear 'neither result nor error' error, or reported through the protocol-warning signal.
