A new test, methodWithIdIsNotANotification, sends a method-plus-id message matching a pending request and asserts the pending callback is failed rather than left hanging and no notification is emitted, then sends the same message with an explicit null id and asserts it IS delivered as a notification.

**Why it matters.** A misbehaving or newer server can no longer wedge a caller permanently. Against the current remote service, which only ever sends id-less notifications, behaviour is unchanged.


#### RC3 — Passing a null callback to call() crashed the program when the answer arrived

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** call() takes a callback object (a std::function) which may legitimately be empty — that is how you write a 'fire and forget' request. The failure path inside call() already guarded against this ('if (cb)'), but the success path stored the empty callback in the pending map with no guard, and every place that later invoked it (three sites in processLine(), one in failAllPending()) called it unconditionally. Invoking an empty std::function throws std::bad_function_call, and here that throw escapes out of a Qt signal handler, which terminates the program.

**Why it was wrong.** An inconsistency inside a single function: call() defended against a null callback on the path where it invokes it immediately, but not on the path where it stores it for later. The four later invocation sites inherited the assumption that the stored callback is never empty.

**What was done.** Every invocation site is now guarded with 'if (cb)', and the reason is documented where the callback is copied out of the map. The header now states explicitly that a null callback is accepted for a fire-and-forget request: the request is still written and still matched, there is simply nothing to invoke. A new test, nullCallbackIsTolerated, issues two such requests — one answered normally, one still outstanding when the transport is torn down — and asserts the pending count drops to zero both times without a crash.

**Why it matters.** Removes a crash reachable by any caller that does not care about the answer. No caller in the repository does this today, so this is a latent hazard rather than an active bug.


#### RC4 — A short write left a half-written message on the wire and the client kept using the corrupted stream

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Requests are framed one-per-line: the client serialises the request to JSON, appends a newline, and writes it in one go. It compared the number of bytes actually written against the number requested and, on a mismatch, reported a failure to the caller — but then carried on using the same transport. If the write was PARTIAL (some bytes went out, not all), a truncated line is already sitting on the wire with no terminating newline. The next request the client writes gets glued onto that fragment, so the server sees one nonsensical line, then another, and every subsequent message is misaligned. The byte stream is unrecoverable, yet the client kept writing into it.

**Why it was wrong.** The write result was treated purely as a per-request success/failure signal, ignoring that a partial write is a stream-level corruption affecting every later request. This is genuinely reachable, not theoretical: src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp's writeData() deliberately returns a short count when the underlying ssh_channel_write() stops making progress (its own comment says 'report the short write').

**What was done.** call() now distinguishes a total write failure (nothing went out — only this request is affected) from a partial one. On a partial write it declares the transport dead by running the normal close path before delivering this call's failure. That fails every other outstanding caller exactly once, emits the transport-closed signal, and latches the client so no further request is written into a stream it can no longer frame. The ordering is safe because nothing after that point touches a member variable, so a callback that destroys the client during the teardown cannot cause a use-after-free. A new test, shortWriteFailsCallAndClosesTransport, uses a purpose-built test device whose write() reports half the bytes and asserts: this call fails once, the previously outstanding call is also failed, transport-closed is emitted exactly once, and a subsequent call fails immediately without being written.

**Why it matters.** Prevents a silent cascade in which every request after one short write is answered with a parse error or not at all, with no indication of why. Turns an unrecoverable corrupted stream into an explicit, reported disconnection that the reconnect logic already knows how to handle.


#### RC5 — A response that had already arrived was thrown away when the peer closed, and its caller was told the transport died

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** When the other end of the stream shuts down, the client marks itself closed and fails every outstanding callback with 'transport closed with request pending'. But Qt is free to deliver the end-of-stream notification while complete, fully-received messages are still sitting unread in the transport's buffer — a server that writes its last answer and immediately exits is exactly that case. The close handler never looked, and the read handler refuses to run once the closed flag is set, so those bytes were discarded. A request that had genuinely been answered successfully was reported to its caller as a transport failure.

**Why it was wrong.** The close handler latched the closed flag as its very first action, which permanently disabled the read path before anything checked whether readable bytes remained.

**What was done.** The close handler now performs a last-gasp drain BEFORE latching the flag: if the transport still reports readable bytes it runs the normal read path once, routing every complete message to its waiting callback, and only then marks itself closed and fails whatever is genuinely still outstanding. Two guards keep this safe: a Qt weak pointer aborts immediately if a callback destroys the client mid-drain, and a second check of the closed flag after the drain prevents a double announcement if a callback (or the oversized-line guard) re-entered the close path and already announced it. A new test, responseQueuedBeforeEofIsStillDelivered, uses a purpose-built test device that can make bytes readable WITHOUT announcing them and then signal end-of-stream — an ordering a real socket cannot be forced into on demand — and asserts the callback receives the real result, not an error, and that transport-closed is still emitted exactly once.

**Why it matters.** Callers now get the answer the server actually sent instead of a spurious 'connection died' error whenever a shutdown races the last response. Affects the reconnect path, where the remote service is deliberately killed and restarted.


#### RC6 — Testing whether a line was blank copied the entire multi-megabyte message

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The read loop skipped blank separator lines with 'if (line.trimmed().isEmpty()) continue;'. QByteArray::trimmed() builds a whole new copy of the byte array with leading and trailing whitespace removed, and that result was used only to ask 'is it empty?'. Since a single message can legitimately be megabytes (an inline file read is capped at 8 MiB of raw content, roughly 11 MiB once encoded), this allocated and copied the whole payload on every single message purely to answer a yes/no question.

**Why it was wrong.** A convenience call whose cost is proportional to the payload was used for a constant-time test.

**What was done.** Added a small file-local helper, isBlankLine(), that scans for a non-whitespace byte and returns false at the first one, allocating nothing. The read loop calls it instead of trimmed(). A comment records why the obvious call was avoided. Behaviour is identical: the existing crlfAndWhitespaceFraming test already covers blank and whitespace-only lines.

**Why it matters.** Removes a full copy of every message received. For large file reads this halves the peak memory touched per message and removes an allocation from the hot path.


#### RC7 — Valid JSON that was not an object produced the nonsensical warning 'malformed RPC message: no error'

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** processLine() had one combined check: if the JSON parser reported an error OR the parsed document was not an object, it emitted a warning containing the parser's error text. When the input parsed fine but was, say, a JSON array (which is what a JSON-RPC 2.0 batch response looks like) or a bare value, there was no parser error, so the warning read 'malformed RPC message: no error' — actively misleading to anyone reading the log.

**Why it was wrong.** Two distinct conditions were merged into one branch that only made sense for one of them.

**What was done.** Split into two branches with their own messages: a genuine parse failure still quotes the parser's error text; well-formed-but-not-an-object now reports 'RPC message is not a JSON object', with a comment explaining why it is reported separately. A new test, nonObjectJsonWarns, feeds a JSON array and a bare JSON string and asserts two warnings are produced, that neither warning text contains the string 'no error', and that the outstanding request is untouched.

**Why it matters.** Diagnostic only, but it removes a log line that would send someone debugging a batch-response incompatibility looking for a parser bug that does not exist.


#### RC8 — Header documentation contradicted the guarantee the class is built around

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h`

**What was wrong.** Three documentation blocks in the public header were wrong or incomplete. (1) setTransport() said 'in-flight pending callbacks are preserved', which directly contradicted call()'s stated guarantee that its callback always fires exactly once — the two statements cannot both be true (see RC1). (2) The transport-closed signal was described as firing only when 'the transport closed/disconnected', omitting that it also fires when the read buffer exceeds the size cap for an unterminated line, and saying nothing about whether a detach or swap also announces a close. (3) call() did not mention that a null callback is allowed, nor what its return value means on the failure paths.

**Why it was wrong.** Documentation drifted as behaviour was added (the size cap, the fail-on-detach semantics) and was never reconciled with the guarantee stated a few lines away.

**What was done.** All three blocks rewritten. setTransport() now states that in-flight requests are failed, explains why (the ids mean nothing to a different remote process), and notes that the failures run synchronously inside the call and may re-enter call() with a retry that goes out on the new transport. The transport-closed signal now documents both triggers, that it fires after any still-readable bytes have been routed, that it fires at most once per bound transport, and that a detach or swap deliberately does NOT emit it. call() now documents the null-callback case and the return value on failure.

**Why it matters.** The next person reading this header gets the real contract instead of two mutually contradictory promises.


#### RC9 — RpcTypes.h claimed the cross-language drift test requires a specific declaration order, which it does not

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/RpcTypes.h`

**What was wrong.** The comment above the workspace method-name constants said: 'Keep this block in the same order as the TypeScript table: remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts parses these kMethodWorkspace* definitions out of this header and fails if the two sides' method-name sets diverge.' Reading that test (remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts) shows both of its helper functions sort their lists before comparing, so declaration order is irrelevant and reordering can never fail the test. The comment also implied the test keys on the C++ identifier prefix 'kMethodWorkspace', when it actually scrapes every constant of the form 'inline constexpr auto k... = "...";' and groups them by the wire name's prefix.

**Why it was wrong.** The comment described an intent (keep the two files visually aligned) as if it were an enforced constraint, and described the test's mechanism inaccurately.

**What was done.** Rewritten to state what the test actually does — scrape every constant of that declaration form, group by wire-name prefix, and compare the sets in both directions — and to say plainly that declaration order here is free because the test sorts both sides, and is kept aligned with the TypeScript table for readability only.

**Why it matters.** Prevents someone from believing a harmless reordering is a test-breaking change, and correctly points at the declaration FORM (which the test's regular expression really does depend on) as the thing that must not change.


#### RC10 — Nine untested contracts in the transport layer

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The existing test file covered message framing, id matching, and malformed-response handling well, but had no coverage for: what happens to outstanding requests when the transport is swapped or detached; whether a retry issued from inside that sweep reaches the new transport; whether binding a replacement transport revives a client that had latched itself closed; whether a message carrying both a method name and an id is misrouted; whether a null callback is survivable; whether non-object JSON is reported sensibly; whether a response already sitting in the buffer at shutdown is delivered; whether a short write is detected; and the precise firing rules for the transport-bound signal.

**Why it was wrong.** These are all edge and teardown paths, several of which required a transport that can be driven more precisely than a real socket allows, so they were never pinned.

**What was done.** Added a small test-local QIODevice subclass, ScriptedDevice, that can make bytes readable without announcing them, signal end-of-stream on command, and report a short write on command — three things a real socket cannot be made to do on demand. It needs no Qt meta-object generation because it only emits signals it inherits. Added a makeSwapPair() helper so the three transport-swap cases do not duplicate socket-pair setup. Added nine tests: detachingTransportFailsPendingOnce, retryFromRebindFailureUsesNewTransport, rebindAfterCloseRevivesClient, methodWithIdIsNotANotification, nullCallbackIsTolerated, nonObjectJsonWarns, responseQueuedBeforeEofIsStillDelivered, shortWriteFailsCallAndClosesTransport, transportBoundFiresOnlyForNonNullBind. Rewrote setTransportTwiceDetachesOld, whose old assertions encoded the incorrect 'pending survives a swap' behaviour fixed in RC1. Every one of these fails on the pre-fix code except transportBoundFiresOnlyForNonNullBind and rebindAfterCloseRevivesClient, which pin behaviour that was already correct but undefended.

**Why it matters.** The transport layer's teardown, rebind, and hostile-input contracts are now defended by tests rather than by comments.


#### RC11 — Every request and result data structure in RpcTypes.h is unused code

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/remote/RpcTypes.h`

**What was wrong.** RpcTypes.h contains roughly fifteen C++ structs (StatParams, StatResult, ReadFileParams, ReadFileResult, WriteFileParams, WriteFileResult, ResolvePathParams, ResolvePathResult, WatchParams, WatchResult, UnwatchParams, UnwatchResult, WatchEvent, ListDirectoryParams, DirectoryEntry, ListDirectoryResult, ServerInfoResult, TmuxSession, SessionExistsParams, SessionExistsResult, KillSessionParams, KillSessionResult) plus three enumerations (Kind, Encoding, WatchEventKind). A repository-wide search finds no code that constructs, reads, or converts any of them — not one. Every consumer (the editor controller, the workspace database wrapper, the layout bridge, the application controller) reads fields straight out of the raw JSON objects. Only the string method-name constants and the revision-mismatch error code are actually used. That is roughly 130 lines of the 236-line header carrying no compiled weight.

**Why it was wrong.** The structs were written up front as a C++ mirror of the frozen TypeScript contract, but the consumers that landed later all chose to work with the JSON objects directly, so nothing ever adopted them.

**What was done.** NOT changed, deliberately. Three reasons. (1) They still function as executable documentation of the wire contract and other modules' comments cite them by name (src/editor/EditorController.cpp and .h both explain a permission decision in terms of 'StatResult.mode'; src/app/tests/tst_appcontroller.cpp refers to 'ServerInfoResult::schemaVersion'). Deleting them would strand those references. (2) Two of the header's includes (<QVector>, <optional>) exist only for these structs, so a deletion is a wider change than it looks. (3) Under a parallel review where fourteen agents share one working tree and no build may be run, deleting a header's public contents is exactly the change most likely to break someone else's in-flight edit. Recorded here so a maintainer can decide: either delete the dead shapes and update the comments that cite them, or adopt them in the consumers so they earn their place.

**Why it matters.** No runtime cost (they are compile-time only), but roughly 130 lines that a reader must decide whether to trust, and a standing invitation to update them and believe something changed.


#### RC12 — The remote library publicly links two libraries it does not use, and other modules silently depend on that

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/remote/CMakeLists.txt`

**What was wrong.** src/remote/CMakeLists.txt declares 'target_link_libraries(ch_remote PUBLIC Qt6::Core ch_models ch_ssh)'. Nothing in the remote library actually uses either: CodeharbordClient.h/.cpp and RpcTypes.h reference only Qt Core types. Because the dependency is declared PUBLIC, it leaks to everything that links the remote library, and several other build targets have come to rely on that leak — src/persistence and src/editor link the remote library without naming the models library, and src/app/tests links it without naming the SSH library even though its cold-start test uses the SSH connection pool directly.

**Why it was wrong.** An over-broad PUBLIC dependency declared before the module's real needs were known, which then became load-bearing as other targets omitted dependencies they get for free through it.

**What was done.** NOT changed. Removing the unused dependencies is the correct cleanup, but it would immediately break the link step of at least src/persistence, src/editor and src/app/tests, whose CMakeLists.txt files are owned by other agents in this review and which I am not permitted to edit. Doing half of a multi-file change would leave the tree unbuildable. Recorded for the build owner: the fix is to add the direct dependencies each of those targets actually needs, then drop ch_models and ch_ssh from ch_remote.

**Why it matters.** Today it only inflates the dependency graph and makes the module look like it needs SSH when it is deliberately transport-agnostic. The footgun is that anyone who does the obvious tidy-up in isolation breaks four other targets' links.


#### RC13 — The remote service can send an error whose id is null, which no client can match to a request

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The remote service (remote/src/codeharbord.ts) answers an unparseable line or a structurally invalid request with an error object whose id is null, because it has no id to echo back. The client cannot route a null id to any outstanding request, so it emits a protocol warning and drops it — and the request that provoked the error stays outstanding until the connection dies. If that ever happens, the caller waits indefinitely.

**Why it was wrong.** An inherent gap in JSON-RPC 2.0 itself: a parse-error response carries no id, so it is unattributable. It is not a client-side defect.

**What was done.** NOT changed, and I judge it unfixable on the client side without guessing which outstanding request caused it, which would be worse than the hang (it could fail the wrong caller). It is also not reachable in practice today: the client always writes syntactically valid JSON containing the required protocol version string, a string method name and an integer id, which is exactly what the server's validity check accepts, so neither the parse-error nor the invalid-request branch can be triggered by this client. The client-side warning already makes the situation visible in logs. I am flagging this as uncertain-in-severity rather than asserting it is a live bug.

**Why it matters.** None observed today. Would become a real hang if the client ever gained a code path that could emit a malformed line, or if it ever spoke to a stricter server.


#### RC14 — Two known-and-accepted inefficiencies in the read path and the id allocator, left alone on purpose

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Two things I examined, judged real but not worth changing, and am recording so the next reader does not have to re-derive the reasoning. (1) Request ids increase by one and wrap back to 1 just before they would overflow the maximum value of a signed 32-bit integer. After a wrap, a reused id could in principle collide with a request that is still outstanding. Reaching that needs roughly 2.1 billion requests, and at a sustained one hundred requests per second that is about eight months of continuous traffic on one connection. (2) The read loop searches the accumulated buffer for a newline from position zero every time and removes consumed bytes from the front, so a single very large message that arrives in many small chunks is scanned repeatedly — quadratic in the message size. For the 16 MiB cap this is a few billion byte comparisons in the worst case, which is why the oversized-input test needs a ten-second allowance.

**Why it was wrong.** (1) A deliberate, documented trade-off. (2) The straightforward formulation of an incremental line splitter.

**What was done.** NEITHER changed. For (1) the existing comment already states the trade-off accurately and the alternative (skip ids that are still outstanding) adds a loop to guard against an eight-month-uptime scenario. For (2) the fix is to carry a 'already searched up to here' offset as extra object state that must be kept consistent across every rebind and buffer reset — new state in the hot path, added to make a malformed-peer path faster. Neither change improves correctness, and under a no-build-allowed review the risk-to-benefit ratio is wrong. Recorded rather than silently skipped.

**Why it matters.** None in realistic use. Documented so a future reader knows these were considered rather than missed.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope covered: src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h, src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp, src/remote/RpcTypes.h, src/remote/CMakeLists.txt, src/remote/tests/CMakeLists.txt, src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp. I read the server side (remote/src/codeharbord.ts, remote/src/rpc-types.ts), the cross-language drift test (remote/test/rpc-mirror.test.ts), specification section 10, and every caller of setTransport()/call() in the tree, but edited nothing outside src/remote/.

Contract mirror check (RpcTypes.h against remote/src/rpc-types.ts): I compared every method-name constant, every field name, every field type, every optional marker, and the one error code. They agree exactly. All seven file method names, all three tmux method names, all nineteen workspace method names, the watch-event notification name and 'server.info' match; the revision-mismatch code is -32001 on both sides; the four fields of the server-info result match what remote/src/codeharbord.ts actually returns; optionality matches on ReadFileParams.offset/length, WriteFileParams.encoding, ResolvePathParams.base and WatchEvent.revision. I found no contract divergence. The only RpcTypes.h defect was a stale comment about the drift test (RC9).

Items I explicitly checked and found already correct, so did not touch: the wrap-before-overflow id allocator; the range check before the floating-point-to-integer id cast and the rejection of fractional ids; treating an explicitly null 'error' field as success rather than failure; treating a null 'result' as a legitimate value; the both-fields-present and neither-field-present handling; erasing the pending entry before invoking its callback so a re-entrant call cannot corrupt the map; moving the pending map out before iterating it in the teardown sweep; the Qt weak-pointer guards around every point where a user callback can destroy the client; the carriage-return tolerance; and the 16 MiB cap on an unterminated line. src/remote/tests/CMakeLists.txt needed no change (it already links Qt Network for the socket pair and defines the repository root the best-effort node test needs).

Verification and honesty statement: as instructed, I ran NO build and NO test command — fourteen agents are editing this tree concurrently. Every claim above about existing behaviour comes from reading the source; every claim about a test comes from reading the assertions I wrote or the ones already present. I have NOT compiled or executed the nine new tests or the modified implementation, so the lead must build and run them. To keep the changes compile-safe I checked by hand: all new includes (<cstring> in the test, none needed in the implementation) are at file top level; the new test device overrides only existing virtual functions of QIODevice with matching signatures and needs no meta-object generation because it emits only inherited signals; the new test slot declarations, their definitions and the helper declaration/definition all match; and the two integer comparisons I introduced (a 64-bit written-byte count against a size, and a boolean derived from it) involve no narrowing or signedness mismatch. Two of my fixes change observable behaviour that other modules see — RC1 (a transport swap or detach now fails outstanding callbacks) and RC5 (the close path drains first). I traced every caller of setTransport() and every test that asserts on pending counts or the transport-closed signal before making them: src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp already closes before detaching so its pending map is empty at the detach, and src/app/tests/tst_livereconnect.cpp asserts precisely the guarantee RC1 strengthens ('answered exactly once, never left hanging'). I found no test outside src/remote/tests that depends on the old behaviour.

### 4.7 Desktop client — data model and workspace repository (`MP`)

*Scope:* `src/models/` (value types, strongly-typed identifiers, lifecycle enumerations, the sidebar list model, the pane split tree) and `src/persistence/` (the client-side repository that turns workspace operations into remote calls).

*15 findings:* 5 missing test, 3 nit, 2 latent bug, 2 footgun, 1 inconsistency, 1 lint, 1 documentation error.

#### MP1 — A corrupt saved pane layout was reported to the rest of the client as a real, valid layout containing one blank pane

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The client asks the server for the saved pane arrangement of a workspace (two arrangements per workspace: one for the file-viewer area, one for the terminal area). Each arrangement is a small tree of JSON objects. The helper that decoded that tree, `parseLayoutTree` in `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp`, could not tell the difference between 'the server sent nothing' and 'the server sent something the tree parser rejected as malformed'. In the malformed case it handed the caller a tree that looked perfectly valid but consisted of a single empty pane.

**Why it was wrong.** `SplitNode::fromJson` (in `src/models/SplitTree.cpp`) signals 'this input is not a valid tree' by returning a default-constructed node, which happens to be a legal value in its own right: a single leaf with an empty pane identifier. `parseLayoutTree` simply wrapped whatever `fromJson` returned into a `std::optional` and therefore always reported 'a layout is present', even for input the parser had rejected (unknown node type, a split whose child count and size-ratio count disagree, a size ratio that is zero/negative/NaN/infinite, or nesting deeper than the 256-level cap).

**What was done.** `parseLayoutTree` now inspects the input as well as the result. A leaf-shaped input always parses successfully, so a rejection can only ever come from an object whose declared `type` is not `"leaf"`. When the parse result is a leaf but the input did not declare itself a leaf, the parse must have been rejected and the function now returns `std::nullopt` ('no layout'). A genuinely stored empty leaf — which is exactly what the application persists when the user closes the last pane in a region — is still reported as a present layout, so nothing regresses. Added `getLayoutMalformedTreeDeliversNullopt` in `src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp`, which drives all three cases (mismatched ratio count, unknown node type, genuine empty leaf) through the real socket transport.

**Why it matters.** The consumer of this data (`src/app/SessionLayouts.h`) deliberately leaves a region empty when its layout cannot be loaded, specifically so the user is never shown a fabricated layout they might then edit and save over the real one. That safeguard was being bypassed: a single corrupt row in the server database would have shown one blank pane, and the first pane edit would have overwritten the user's real, recoverable layout.


#### MP2 — Malformed entries inside a workspace listing became phantom groups, sessions and panes with empty identifiers

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The 'list everything' server call returns nested JSON arrays: groups, each containing sessions, each containing viewer panes and terminal panes. The decoding loops in `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp` called `QJsonValue::toObject()` on every array element without checking that the element actually was a JSON object.

**Why it was wrong.** In Qt, `QJsonValue::toObject()` on a non-object (a number, a string, `null`, a nested array) silently returns an *empty* object rather than failing. Every `parseGroup` / `parseSession` / `parseViewerPane` / `parseTerminalPane` helper then happily mapped that empty object onto a fully default-valued record whose identifier is the empty string, and appended it to the result as if it were real data.

**What was done.** All four loops (`parseGroupList`, `parseGroupNode`, and the two pane loops in `parseSessionNode`) now skip any element that is not a JSON object, with a comment explaining why. Added `listSkipsNonObjectEntries` in `src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp`, which feeds a listing containing a bare number where a group belongs, a `null` where a session belongs, a string where a viewer pane belongs and an empty array where a terminal pane belongs, and asserts that only the four genuine records survive.

**Why it matters.** Only reachable from a buggy or hostile server, hence 'latent'. Had it occurred, the sidebar would have shown a nameless group/session, and any identifier-keyed lookup elsewhere in the client would have had an empty-string key that could collide with a second such phantom entry.


#### MP3 — The sidebar model served a display role that it did not advertise, making it unreachable from the user interface layer

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/SessionsModel.cpp`, `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `SessionsModel` is the data model behind the sessions sidebar. Its `data()` function answers Qt's standard `Qt::DisplayRole` (returning the row's name), but its `roleNames()` function — the only mechanism by which the declarative user-interface layer can address a role by name — did not list that role.

**Why it was wrong.** `roleNames()` returns a brand-new table rather than extending the base class's table. Qt's `QAbstractItemModel::roleNames()` does include an entry naming `Qt::DisplayRole` as `"display"`; overriding it wholesale silently dropped that entry while `data()` kept serving the role.

**What was done.** Added `{Qt::DisplayRole, "display"}` to the returned table in `src/models/SessionsModel.cpp`, with a comment stating the invariant (every role `data()` answers must be named here). Verified by reading the three sidebar user-interface files that no delegate binds a property named `display`, and that all sidebar delegates use Qt's 'required property' binding mode, in which model roles are bound explicitly rather than injected into scope — so adding a role cannot shadow anything. Added `sessionsModelRoleNamesCoverEveryServedRole` in `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`, which asserts the role table has exactly the eight served roles, that each named role is answered by at least one of the two row kinds, that the seven user-interface-facing names are byte-for-byte the ones the delegates bind to, and that roles a given row kind does not carry return an invalid value rather than a plausible-looking default.

**Why it matters.** No user-visible breakage today, because nothing currently asks for the display role by name. It was a trap: the model and its own role table disagreed, so a generic Qt view or a future delegate asking for `display` would have got nothing with no diagnostic.


#### MP4 — Two case labels in the sidebar model were indented one level too deep

- **Kind:** Lint
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/SessionsModel.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Inside the session-row branch of `SessionsModel::data()`, the `case IdRole:` and `case GroupIdRole:` labels and their bodies were indented four spaces further than the sibling labels in the same `switch` statement.

**Why it was wrong.** Almost certainly an editing accident when those two roles were added.

**What was done.** Re-indented the four lines to match the rest of the `switch`. No behaviour change.

**Why it matters.** Cosmetic only, but it made the block read as if those two cases were nested inside the preceding one.


#### MP5 — The precedence reducer for sidebar row colour had only eight sampled test cases out of thirty-two possible inputs

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `ch::aggregateRowState` takes five boolean flags (any terminal in error / waiting for user input / running / finished with output the user has not seen / connected) and reduces them to one of six display states, in a fixed priority order taken from the product specification. Its behaviour is therefore fully described by 32 input combinations. The existing tests exercised roughly eight scenarios, and all of them went through the higher-level wrapper `SessionsModel::aggregateSessionState` rather than calling the reducer directly.

**Why it was wrong.** The original tests were written as illustrative scenarios rather than as exhaustive coverage.

**What was done.** Added `aggregateRowStateCoversEveryInputCombination` in `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`. It loops over all 32 combinations and compares against an independent restatement of the priority order written lowest-priority-first (a chain of unconditional overwrites), so it shares no code structure with the implementation's highest-priority-first early returns; swapping any two priority levels in the implementation now fails the test. It also asserts that the enumeration's declaration order matches the priority order, because the sidebar's colour/glyph lookup table treats the numeric value of the enumerator as the priority.

**Why it matters.** The priority order is what decides the colour of every session row. It is now pinned exhaustively, and the enumerator ordering that the user-interface layer silently depends on is pinned too.


#### MP6 — The state-name strings that cross into the browser pages and the remote service were entirely untested

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `src/models/SessionState.cpp` converts four enumerations into strings (`toString(TerminalState)`, `toString(AgentState)`, `toString(SessionRowState)`, `toString(FileState)`). Nothing tested any of them, yet three of the four are cross-boundary contracts rather than display text.

**Why it was wrong.** The strings were introduced alongside their consumers, and the consumers' own tests compare against `toString(...)` rather than against literal strings, so a rename on both sides at once would go undetected — and a rename on one side only would be caught in a distant, unrelated test.

**What was done.** Added `stateStringsArePinnedWireValues` in `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`, pinning all 33 enumerator-to-string mappings as literals and additionally asserting that within each enumeration no two enumerators produce the same string (a copy-paste in the `switch` would otherwise make two states indistinguishable to the code reading them).

**Why it matters.** Concretely: `toString(AgentState)` must equal the state names the remote Node service emits (`AGENT_STATES` in `remote/src/events.ts`); `toString(TerminalState)` is what the terminal bridge hands the xterm.js page and what `TerminalPaneView.qml` compares against; `toString(FileState)` is what the editor controller publishes to the Monaco editor page (its own tests compare against the literal `"externally_modified"`). A rename here previously broke consumers that this test executable cannot see.


#### MP7 — Two rejection paths in the layout-tree parser had no test coverage

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `src/models/SplitTree.cpp` rejects a layout tree whose `children` array holds an element that is not a JSON object, and one whose `ratios` array holds an element that is not a number. Neither branch was exercised: the existing malformed-ratio test only ever supplied numeric values (negative, zero, not-a-number, infinite), and the existing malformed-child test only supplied a structurally invalid nested split.

**Why it was wrong.** Coverage gap; the tests were written around the shapes the code produces, not around every shape the parser must refuse.

**What was done.** Added `splitTreeRejectsNonObjectChildAndNonNumericRatio` in `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`, covering a numeric value in the children array, a string in the ratios array, and a boolean in the ratios array.

**Why it matters.** These branches matter because the parser reads data the client did not produce (it is stored on, and returned by, a remote server). A silently coerced non-numeric ratio would become zero and divide pane geometry by zero.


#### MP8 — An agent in the 'starting' state counting as 'running' for the sidebar was untested

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `SessionsModel::aggregateSessionState` deliberately treats an agent that has been asked to start but has not yet reported itself running as 'running', so the sidebar does not briefly fall back to 'idle' during the gap. No test covered that mapping.

**Why it was wrong.** Coverage gap.

**What was done.** Added `startingAgentCountsAsRunning` in `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`: a lone starting agent yields 'running'; it outranks a sibling terminal that has finished with unseen output; and it is outranked by a sibling waiting for user input.

**Why it matters.** Deleting or changing that one condition would previously have passed the whole test suite, and the sidebar would have flickered to 'idle' every time an agent started.


#### MP9 — The client's declared database schema version was documented as being kept in step with the server's, but nothing enforced it

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `WorkspaceDb::kSchemaVersion` in `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h` is an informational constant whose comment states it is 'kept in lockstep with remote/sql/schema.sql and WORKSPACE_SCHEMA_VERSION so the three move together'. The remote service's own test suite pins its two sides (the SQL schema file and the TypeScript constant) to each other, but it cannot see the C++ constant, so a server-side version bump could land while the client still advertised the old number.

**Why it was wrong.** The parity check was expressed as a comment rather than as a test. Only two of the three sources were mechanically tied together.

**What was done.** Added `schemaVersionMatchesTheRemoteSchema` in `src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp`. It reads `remote/sql/schema.sql` and `remote/src/workspace.ts` from the repository (the test executable already has the repository path compiled in as `CH_REPO_ROOT`, used by the existing live test), extracts the version number from each with a regular expression, and compares both against `WorkspaceDb::kSchemaVersion`. This mirrors the technique the remote service's `rpc-mirror` and `schema` tests already use — read the sibling-language source and assert parity.

**Why it matters.** Closes the last leg of a three-way consistency claim that was previously only enforced on two legs.


#### MP10 — Nothing warned callers that a pending server-reply callback outlives the repository object that created it

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h`

**What was wrong.** Every method on `WorkspaceDb` is asynchronous and takes a callback. Once handed to the underlying remote-procedure-call client, that callback is owned by the client, not by `WorkspaceDb`. Destroying the `WorkspaceDb` cancels nothing, so a callback can run long after the repository — and, more dangerously, after whatever object the callback captured — is gone. The header said nothing about this.

**Why it was wrong.** Undocumented ownership. The one existing caller (`src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp`) gets it right by capturing a self-guarding weak pointer and checking it before use, but that convention was discoverable only by reading that file.

**What was done.** Added a 'Lifetime' paragraph to the class comment in `src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h`: the client pointer is borrowed and must outlive the repository; each pending callback is owned by the client and runs at most once, possibly after the repository is gone; a callback capturing a long-lived object must guard its own lifetime, and the house pattern is the weak pointer used in `SessionLayouts.cpp`. Documentation only — no declaration changed, so no other agent's file can be affected.

**Why it matters.** The next person to add a `WorkspaceDb` call site now sees the requirement at the point of use instead of discovering it as a crash after a window closes with a request in flight. Note the wording says 'at most once', not 'exactly once', because of the separate issue recorded in `out_of_scope`.


#### MP11 — A comment claimed the server's row timestamps live in the persistence layer, but they are discarded entirely

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/WorkspaceTypes.h`

**What was wrong.** The header comment in `src/models/WorkspaceTypes.h` said that 'persistence-only bookkeeping (created_at/updated_at) stays in the persistence layer'. That is not what happens: the persistence layer (`WorkspaceDb.cpp`) never decodes those two fields at all. They are dropped on the floor and exist nowhere in the client.

**Why it was wrong.** The comment described an intended split of responsibilities that was never implemented, so a reader would go looking in the persistence layer for timestamp data that is not there.

**What was done.** Rewrote that sentence in `src/models/WorkspaceTypes.h` to say plainly that the server's creation/update timestamp columns are deliberately not represented, that nothing on the client reads them, and that the decoder therefore drops them rather than carrying a value no part of the user interface shows.

**Why it matters.** Documentation accuracy. Also records the decision, so the next person who needs a timestamp knows they must add it to the decoder rather than hunt for it.


#### MP12 — The priority order was documented at the wrapper but not at the function that actually implements it

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/SessionState.h`

**What was wrong.** `ch::aggregateRowState` in `src/models/SessionState.h` is the canonical declaration of the sidebar row-priority rule, but its comment was a single line ('Reduce a set of terminal/agent conditions to the highest-priority row state') that stated neither the order nor the behaviour when every flag is false. The full explanation lived only on the convenience wrapper in `src/models/SessionsModel.h`.

**Why it was wrong.** The documentation ended up on the wrapper that callers happened to use first, rather than on the function that defines the rule.

**What was done.** Expanded the comment in `src/models/SessionState.h` to spell out the priority order, to note that it is the same as the enumeration's declaration order, and to state that all-flags-false — which includes a session with no terminals at all — yields 'disconnected'. Additive comment only.

**Why it matters.** Readability. Someone reading the reducer no longer has to find a different header to learn the rule it implements.


#### MP13 — The strongly-typed identifier wrappers cannot be used as hash-map or sorted-map keys, and that is correct as it stands

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/models/Ids.h`

**What was wrong.** The five wrapper types in `src/models/Ids.h` (`GroupId`, `DevSessionId`, `ViewerPaneId`, `TerminalId`, `ServerId`) each hold one string, define only equality, and provide no hash function and no ordering. I checked whether that was a gap, since mixing up two kinds of identifier is exactly what these types exist to prevent.

**Why it was wrong.** Not a defect. I searched the whole `src/` tree and found no code that uses any of these types as a hash-map or sorted-map key, so no hash or ordering is required today. They are simple aggregate types, which means the only way to build one is with explicit braces (`GroupId{someString}`) — there is no implicit conversion from a plain string and no conversion between the five types, so a function that takes one kind cannot silently accept another. Adding an unused hash function purely on speculation would be dead code, which this repository's `no_scaffolding` test exists to keep out.

**What was done.** No change made. Recording the verification so the next reviewer does not repeat it.

**Why it matters.** None. The types already deliver the mix-up prevention their comment claims.


#### MP14 — The layout tree has no mutation operations, so the whole class of node-leak, cycle and orphan bugs cannot arise

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/models/SplitTree.h`, `src/models/SplitTree.cpp`

**What was wrong.** I specifically hunted for leaked or orphaned nodes, cycles, unbalanced size ratios, mishandling of removing a node's last child, unbounded recursion and index invalidation in the layout tree.

**Why it was wrong.** Not applicable by construction. `SplitNode` is a plain value type whose children are held in a `QVector<SplitNode>` by value, not by pointer. Copies are deep, there is no shared ownership and no way to express a cycle, so leaks and orphans are impossible. There are no split, remove or move operations in this file at all (those live in `src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp`, which belongs to a different reviewer), so there is no removal path to get wrong. Unbounded recursion is already handled: the parser enforces a 256-level nesting cap, and the header explains why the two functions that recurse without their own guard (serialisation and equality) are nevertheless safe. Ratio validation is already strict — every ratio must be finite and strictly positive, which also guarantees their sum is positive. The one asymmetry I found, that serialising a hand-built node whose child count and ratio count disagree produces output the parser will refuse, is already documented ('inverses for any valid tree') and already covered by a test.

**What was done.** No change made beyond the two new parser-rejection test cases recorded as MP7.

**Why it matters.** None. Recording the negative result so this ground is not re-covered.


#### MP15 — The sidebar model's only update path is a full reset, which is correct but coarse

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/models/SessionsModel.h`, `src/models/SessionsModel.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `SessionsModel` exposes exactly one mutator, `setGroups()`, which wraps a full model reset. There are no incremental insert/remove/change paths, so I found no mismatched begin/end pairing and no missing change notification — those bugs require incremental paths to exist. The cost is that every change, however small, is a full reset: the application rebuilds and re-publishes the entire two-level tree on every single agent-status change (I traced two signal connections in `src/app/AppController.cpp` that call its row-rebuild function, which ends in `setGroups`). A reset destroys and recreates every delegate in the sidebar.

**Why it was wrong.** Deliberate design, and the user-interface side is written to tolerate it: `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml` documents that delegates re-register themselves on creation and that every consumer reads state off the live delegate, 'so a model refresh needs no invalidation step'. Selection is tracked by identifier string, not by model index, so it survives the reset.

**What was done.** No change made. The clean improvement — an additive method that updates terminal statuses in place and emits a targeted change notification for just the affected rows — is only worth anything if the caller in `src/app/AppController.cpp` is switched over to it, and that file belongs to another reviewer. Adding an unused public method on its own would be dead code. Routed to `out_of_scope` instead.

**Why it matters.** No incorrect behaviour today. It is a scaling and smoothness cost that grows with the number of sessions, and a trap for anyone who later stores a model index across a refresh.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* No build, test, lint or type-check command was run, per instructions; I read conservatively instead. Files I wrote, all inside my slice: src/models/SessionsModel.cpp, src/models/SessionState.h, src/models/WorkspaceTypes.h, src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp, src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h, src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp, src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp. Verified with a scoped `git diff --stat` that nothing outside src/models and src/persistence was touched. I did not touch any CMakeLists.txt (no new source or link dependency was needed: QFile and QRegularExpression are Qt Core, already linked; QSet is header-only Core).

NO BREAKING DECLARATION CHANGES were made to the two shared headers other agents read. src/models/SessionState.h and src/models/WorkspaceTypes.h received comment-only edits — no type, field, function signature or enumerator was added, removed or renamed. src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h received a comment-only edit. So no call site anywhere else in the tree needs adjusting.

Two things the lead should know when running the suites:

1. My new test `schemaVersionMatchesTheRemoteSchema` (in src/persistence/tests/tst_workspacedb.cpp) reads remote/sql/schema.sql and remote/src/workspace.ts at run time and compares the version number it finds in each against WorkspaceDb::kSchemaVersion. I confirmed after my edits that all three currently read 2, so it passes as the tree stands. But remote/sql/schema.sql and remote/src/workspace.ts are being edited concurrently by other agents. If either of them bumps the schema version, this test will fail — and that failure is the test doing its job: WorkspaceDb::kSchemaVersion in src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.h must be bumped to match. The test also fails if someone reformats the schema_version seed statement or the WORKSPACE_SCHEMA_VERSION declaration beyond what its regular expressions tolerate, in which case the assertion message says exactly that.

2. MP1 changes observable behaviour at the boundary between src/persistence and src/app: a layout tree the parser rejects is now delivered as 'no layout' instead of as a present-but-empty layout. I checked the two consumers before making the change. src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp ignores the tree returned by the save path entirely (its own comment says the server echoes the tree verbatim so there is nothing to adopt), so the save path is unaffected. The load path treats 'no layout' as 'region not loaded', which is precisely the documented intent in src/app/SessionLayouts.h. I also deliberately kept a genuinely stored empty leaf — which is what src/app/SessionLayouts.cpp persists when the user closes a region's last pane, and which src/app/tests/tst_sessionlayouts.cpp asserts on — reporting as a present layout, so that existing test is not disturbed.

One uncertainty I want to be explicit about rather than paper over: MP3 adds a new user-interface-visible role name, "display". I convinced myself it is inert by checking that all three sidebar delegate files (src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml, src/qml/GroupHeader.qml, src/qml/SessionRow.qml) use Qt's 'required property' binding mode, in which model roles are bound explicitly rather than injected into the delegate's scope, so a new role cannot shadow the unrelated `display` property that Qt's ItemDelegate base class happens to have. I did not run the sidebar tests to confirm. If src/qml/tests/tst_sidebar.cpp or src/qml/tests/tst_uxshell.cpp reports an unexpected engine warning, revert exactly the one added line `{Qt::DisplayRole, "display"},` in src/models/SessionsModel.cpp and drop `Qt::DisplayRole` from the `served` list plus the final two Qt::DisplayRole assertions in `sessionsModelRoleNamesCoverEveryServedRole` in src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp; everything else in that test stands on its own.

I found no active bug — nothing in this slice is producing wrong behaviour today. Both real defects (MP1, MP2) require input the current server does not produce, which is why they are classified latent rather than active. I did not manufacture findings to pad the list: MP13, MP14 and MP15 are honest negative results from the specific hunts the assignment asked for (identifier mix-ups and hashing, layout-tree leaks/cycles/orphans/last-child-removal, and model-protocol begin/end pairing), and I have left them in as 'not fixed, explained' so the ground is not re-covered.

### 4.8 Desktop client — terminal subsystem (`TM`)

*Scope:* `src/terminal/`: one controller per terminal (connect, attach tmux, ready, reconnect), the bridge to the xterm.js page, and the factory that creates terminals for a Dev Session.

*13 findings:* 3 latent bug, 3 footgun, 3 nit, 2 active bug, 1 inconsistency, 1 missing test.

#### TM1 — TerminalFactory::kill() kept a hash-table iterator alive across a call that can rebuild the hash table

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`

**What was wrong.** TerminalFactory keeps a lookup table (a QHash) mapping each terminal's controller object to the SSH channel and the tmux session name attached to it. kill() looked up the entry, saved a pointer-like iterator into the table, then called detach() (which closes the SSH channel), and only afterwards used that saved iterator to write into the table entry. If anything that ran during detach() had added a new entry to the table, the table would have been resized and the saved iterator would point at freed memory, so the write afterwards would have corrupted the heap.

**Why it was wrong.** detach() closes the SSH channel, which makes the terminal controller move to the 'disconnected' state, which is broadcast to the QWebChannel bridge and from there to the QML pane. Any of those listeners is free to call attach() or kill() on another pane of the same factory, and a single insertion into a QHash can rehash it and invalidate every outstanding iterator. Holding an iterator across a call that emits signals is therefore never safe.

**What was done.** kill() now reads the tmux target through the existing targetFor() accessor before detaching, calls detach(), and then looks the entry up again with a fresh find() inside an if-statement initializer before clearing the target. No iterator survives the detach() call. A comment spells out why.

**Why it matters.** Removes a potential heap corruption / crash path when a terminal pane is killed while another pane on the same factory reacts to the resulting state change. Nothing observable changes on the normal path.


#### TM2 — A failed PTY open reported the real reason and then immediately overwrote it with a generic one

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`

**What was wrong.** When opening a remote pseudo-terminal failed, the user was shown the unhelpful message 'could not open a PTY channel for ch_<session>_<terminal>' instead of the actual reason (for example an SSH-level failure text from libssh, or 'no SSH connection pool'). The specific reason was produced first and then thrown away.

**Why it was wrong.** TerminalFactory::attach() subscribes to the channel object's channelError signal and forwards it to its own error() signal. Every single failure path inside SshChannelDevice::startPty() emits channelError before returning false (abortStart() emits it; acquireChannel() emits 'channel already started' / 'no SSH connection pool' / 'could not open SSH channel'; the build-without-libssh stub emits 'built without libssh'). attach() then emitted a second, generic error() of its own. The QML pane (src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml, its onError handler) simply assigns each message to the pane's status text, so the last message wins and the generic one always replaced the specific one.

**What was done.** Removed the second, generic error() emission from the startPty() failure branch in TerminalFactory::attach(). A comment records that every startPty() failure path already emits channelError, and that a later generic message would only overwrite it in the pane's chrome.

**Why it matters.** A pane that cannot open its terminal now displays the real reason, which is the difference between a user being able to diagnose the failure and not. No test depended on the generic wording.


#### TM3 — Re-attaching a terminal without an explicit size silently shrank the pane to 80x24

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`, `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.h`

**What was wrong.** TerminalFactory::attach() takes a column count and a row count. Values of zero or less mean 'the renderer has not measured itself yet'. In that case attach() used the conventional fallback of 80 columns by 24 rows and wrote that into the controller, discarding any real size the pane had already established. A reconnect that arrived without geometry would therefore snap a large terminal back to 80x24 on the remote host.

**Why it was wrong.** The fallback was written as a flat 'cols > 0 ? cols : 80', which does not consider that the controller may already hold a valid, renderer-measured size from before the disconnect. The controller is deliberately the one component that remembers the pane size across a channel swap (a fresh remote pseudo-terminal always opens at the channel default), so overwriting it with a default defeats that whole mechanism.

**What was done.** attach() now falls back in two steps: use the caller's value when it is positive; otherwise use the size the controller already recorded when that is positive; only a controller that has never been sized falls back to 80x24. Both the remote PTY open and the recorded geometry use the resolved value. The header documentation for attach() now states this contract.

**Why it matters.** A reconnect can no longer shrink a laid-out pane. On the current code path the QML pane always passes the bridge's recorded columns/rows, so today's behaviour is unchanged; this closes the hole for any other caller (including tests and a future host) that passes 0.


#### TM4 — Two independent hand-written copies of 'which terminal states count as connected'

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalController.h`, `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp`, `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The rule for which lifecycle states mean 'this terminal currently has a live connection, so ending the connection should move it to disconnected' was written out twice: once as a switch statement inside TerminalController::onTransportFinished(), and once as a private helper function called isLive() in TerminalFactory.cpp. Both listed the same three states. If someone added a new state to the lifecycle enum, or changed the classification in one place, the two copies would silently disagree and a pane would report a dropped connection in one code path but not the other.

**Why it was wrong.** The classification is policy shared by two components, but it was never given a single home. Both copies also used a 'default:' catch-all in their switch, which means adding a new state to the enum produces no compiler warning at either site.

**What was done.** Added a public static function TerminalController::isLiveState(TerminalState) with a comment saying it exists precisely so the two callers cannot drift. It enumerates every state explicitly rather than using 'default:', so adding a state to the enum now triggers a compiler warning that forces a decision. TerminalController::onTransportFinished() and TerminalFactory::detach() both call it; the private helper in TerminalFactory.cpp was deleted. A new test pins the classification of all nine states one by one.

**Why it matters.** One definition instead of two, and the compiler now enforces that a new lifecycle state is classified. No behaviour change today.


#### TM5 — Three of the nine terminal lifecycle states are never produced, and the reconnect-delay helper has no caller

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalController.h`, `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The terminal lifecycle enumeration (in src/models/SessionState.h) lists nine states. Only five of them are ever produced by shipping code: 'unloaded' (the initial value), 'opening_channel', 'attaching_tmux', 'ready', 'disconnected', plus 'error'. The states 'connecting', 'authenticating' and 'reconnecting' are set only by tests. Separately, TerminalController::reconnectDelaySeconds() (the 1s/2s/5s/10s/30s/then-60s retry ladder) has no production caller at all: the automatic retry ladder that actually runs is a byte-for-byte duplicate in SessionBootstrap::reconnectDelaySeconds() in src/app, and a dropped terminal pane just lands in 'disconnected' and waits for the user to press the Retry button in the QML pane. Nothing in the code said any of this, so a reader would reasonably assume the terminal layer drives its own reconnect ladder and publishes 'connecting'/'authenticating'/'reconnecting'.

**Why it was wrong.** The spec (docs/SPEC.md section 5.6) lists all nine states as suggestions and the enum mirrors them faithfully, but connection progress is tracked at the session level (one shared SSH connection for the whole application), not per terminal pane, so a single pane genuinely cannot observe 'connecting' or 'authenticating'. The retry ladder likewise ended up implemented at the session level. The duplication is deliberate (src/app does not link the terminal library) and is already flagged from the src/app side, but not from this side.

**What was done.** NOT changed behaviourally, on purpose: removing the unreachable states would mean editing src/models/SessionState.h (another agent's file) and removing the helper would break a cross-library comment and a test outside this slice. Instead the two doc comments now state the facts: setState()'s comment records exactly which states src/terminal produces, that the other three exist so a session-level host can publish its progress onto a pane, and that no caller may infer anything from their absence; reconnectDelaySeconds()'s comment records that nothing here schedules a reconnect, names SessionBootstrap::reconnectDelaySeconds() as the ladder that is actually driven, and states that both are pinned by their own test against the same spec vector so they cannot drift.

**Why it matters.** No runtime change. The next reader is no longer misled into thinking the terminal layer owns reconnect, and the deliberate duplication is now documented from both sides. Whether to prune the unused states/helper is a cross-slice decision for the lead.


#### TM6 — The bridge's geometryChanged signal is the QML pane's attach trigger, and nothing said so

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalBridge.h`

**What was wrong.** TerminalBridge emits a signal called geometryChanged() when the web page reports a new terminal size. It reads like a routine property-change notification, so the obvious 'improvement' is to also emit it whenever the C++ side records a size change (which would keep the exposed columns/rows properties' change notifications honest). That change would cause infinite recursion: the QML pane (src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml) responds to this exact signal by calling attachNow(), and TerminalFactory::attach() itself sets the controller's size as part of attaching, so attach() would re-enter attach() before it had finished.

**Why it was wrong.** The signal serves double duty: it is nominally the change notification for the exposed columns and rows properties, but it is really 'the renderer has measured itself, so it is now safe to open a remote terminal at the right size'. That second, load-bearing meaning existed only in the QML file, one directory away.

**What was done.** Rewrote the signal's documentation in TerminalBridge.h as an explicit warning: it must remain a report of what the page asked for, it must never be re-broadcast from geometry the C++ side records itself, and the reason given is the re-entrancy into TerminalFactory::attach(). I deliberately did NOT 'fix' the notification gap, because doing so is the bug.

**Why it matters.** Prevents a plausible and very destructive future change. No runtime change.


#### TM7 — The controller's entire transport seam and connection-drop state machine were untested

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The existing unit test for TerminalController covered output batching, the hidden-view buffer, plain state transitions, tmux command quoting and the retry ladder, but nothing at all about the piece that connects it to a real remote terminal: attaching and detaching a byte transport, ingesting bytes from it, sending keystrokes to it, the size-recording rules, and what happens to the lifecycle state when the connection ends. Those behaviours were only exercised by the two tests that need a real SSH server and skip themselves when the environment variable CH_LIVE_SSH is unset, so in practice they were never run.

**Why it was wrong.** The seam takes a plain QIODevice specifically so it can be driven without SSH, but no test double existed that behaved like a real channel. The nearest thing, a QBuffer, is unusable for output tests because it hands back everything written into it as readable data (see TM8).

**What was done.** Added a FakeChannel test double (a sequential QIODevice that can be handed bytes as if a remote terminal produced them, records what was written back, and can end its read channel either while still readable or after closing, matching the two real shapes) plus eight new test cases: (1) isLiveState classifies all nine lifecycle states; (2) output is ingested both through the readyRead notification and from bytes already sitting in the channel when the controller subscribes (that first batch is the whole visible screen after tmux redraws, so losing it leaves a blank terminal); (3) detaching really unsubscribes, so a released channel's trailing bytes are not ingested; (4) a transport destroyed under a live controller detaches instead of dangling, and both a keystroke and a later re-attach stay safe; (5) the reconnect case: swapping in a new channel preserves both the retained scrollback and the recorded pane size, and old output stays ordered before new; (6) a channel that ends while still readable has its final bytes claimed before the drop is reported; (7) a channel ending moves a live pane to disconnected but leaves all six non-live states completely untouched and emits no state change at all; (8) keystrokes are refused without an open writable transport, an empty send is not a failure, and a closed channel refuses the write; (9) non-positive sizes are refused and never overwrite the last real size, which starts at 0.

**Why it matters.** The reconnect and disconnect behaviour the whole terminal design rests on is now covered by tests that run everywhere, with no server required.


#### TM8 — Two test doubles used a read-write memory buffer as a terminal transport, which echoes keystrokes back as terminal output

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp`, `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalpage.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Two tests stood in for the SSH terminal channel with a QBuffer opened for reading and writing. A QBuffer makes everything written into it readable again, so once the event loop turned, a keystroke sent through the bridge came straight back to the controller as terminal OUTPUT. In tst_terminalfactory.cpp this was latent (no test spun the event loop after writing), but it meant any future assertion about output in that test would silently have been measuring the test's own echo. In tst_terminalpage.cpp, which runs a real browser engine and a real event loop, the echoed carriage return actually reached the rendered terminal screen that a later test in the same file inspects.

**Why it was wrong.** QBuffer is the obvious stand-in for 'a QIODevice I can inspect', but a terminal channel is inherently one-way per direction, and QBuffer is not.

**What was done.** Both doubles are now opened write-only. The controller refuses to read a transport that is not readable, so the direction stays one-way, while sending keystrokes and inspecting the buffer's contents both still work unchanged. Comments at both sites explain the trap.

**Why it matters.** Removes a stray carriage return from the real rendered terminal in the browser-engine test, and removes a trap that would have made future output assertions in the factory test meaningless. All existing assertions still hold.


#### TM9 — Reloading the terminal web page loses everything the terminal printed during the reload

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalBridge.cpp`, `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The C++ side stops sending output to the page and buffers it instead whenever the page reports that it is not visible; it releases the buffer as one batch when the page reports that its renderer has mounted. When the page is reloaded (or navigated away and back), the old renderer is destroyed but nothing tells the C++ side, so the C++ side keeps happily pushing output at a page that no longer exists. Those bytes are gone: the freshly loaded page starts with a blank screen and stays blank until the remote terminal happens to redraw.

**Why it was wrong.** The page's teardown handler (the 'pagehide' listener in src/web/terminal/src/index.ts) disposes the terminal renderer but does not first call the bridge's notifyViewVisible(false) slot. The C++ bridge has no other way to learn that the renderer went away — there is no signal from the browser engine that it listens to.

**What was done.** NOT fixed, because the one-line fix belongs in a file this slice may not write to. The fix is: in the 'pagehide' handler in src/web/terminal/src/index.ts, call bridge.notifyViewVisible(false) before host.dispose(). Once that is in place the existing C++ machinery handles the rest with no change: the controller buffers during the gap and the new page's mount handshake replays it. I verified there is nothing the bridge can do on its own — by the time the new page calls the mount handshake, the lost bytes were already emitted at the dead page and are not recoverable.

**Why it matters.** Today a page reload silently blanks a terminal pane. Routed to the owner of the TypeScript page.


**Follow-up.** **Resolved later in this round by WB1**, which made the terminal web page tell the C++ side that its renderer is going away, so the output is buffered and replayed instead of lost.

#### TM10 — A keystroke dropped because the channel is dead is discarded with no explanation in the code

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalBridge.cpp`

**What was wrong.** TerminalBridge::sendInput() ignores the true/false result of the controller's send. A reader cannot tell whether that is an oversight or a decision.

**Why it was wrong.** The page-facing contract for this slot returns nothing and is frozen, so there is genuinely nowhere to report a refused write; the reasoning was just never written down.

**What was done.** Added a comment stating that the result is deliberately discarded: the contract is void and frozen; the pane's status strip is already showing 'disconnected' or 'error', which is the feedback the user needs; and buffering keystrokes to replay later would type a stale command at whatever prompt happens to appear next.

**Why it matters.** Documentation only.


#### TM11 — Trimming the oldest bytes from the hidden-terminal buffer can cut a character or an escape sequence in half

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** While a terminal pane's renderer is suspended, output accumulates in a rolling buffer capped at 2 MiB, with the oldest bytes dropped once the cap is reached. The drop happens at an arbitrary byte offset, so it can land in the middle of a multi-byte UTF-8 character (which then renders as a replacement character) or in the middle of a terminal escape sequence (which can leave the replayed screen with wrong colours or a wrong cursor position until the next full redraw).

**Why it was wrong.** A rolling byte buffer has no knowledge of character or escape-sequence boundaries. Making it boundary-aware would require a full terminal-escape parser in C++, which is exactly the job the browser-side terminal emulator already does and which this layer deliberately does not duplicate.

**What was done.** NOT fixed. Correcting it properly means parsing terminal escape sequences in the controller, which contradicts the design (the C++ side is a byte pump; emulation lives in the renderer). The damage is cosmetic, self-heals on the next full redraw, and only occurs for a pane that has been hidden long enough to overflow 2 MiB. I am flagging it rather than papering over it.

**Why it matters.** At most one garbled glyph or a briefly wrong colour on the replayed screen of a pane that was hidden for a very long time.


#### TM12 — A remote terminal that produces no output leaves the pane stuck in 'attaching_tmux' forever

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Uncertain
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`

**What was wrong.** TerminalFactory::attach() decides the pane is fully up ('ready') when the first byte arrives from the remote pseudo-terminal, on the reasoning that tmux redraws the entire pane the moment it attaches. If a remote terminal ever attaches without producing any output, the pane stays in the 'attaching_tmux' state indefinitely. There is no timeout. The QML pane treats anything other than 'ready' as not-live, so the user sees a permanent warning banner over an otherwise working terminal.

**Why it was wrong.** 'Ready' is inferred from a side effect (tmux drawing itself) rather than from an explicit acknowledgement, and no deadline backs the inference up.

**What was done.** NOT fixed. Adding a timer that promotes the pane to 'ready' after N milliseconds would be inventing a policy the spec does not specify and would mask a genuinely stuck attach; adding an explicit acknowledgement means a new remote round trip, which is outside this slice. I could not construct a case where a real tmux attach produces nothing, so I am reporting this as uncertain rather than 'fixing' working code.

**Why it matters.** If it ever happens, a working terminal permanently wears a 'not live' banner. No data loss: output and keystrokes both flow normally in the 'attaching_tmux' state.


**Follow-up.** **Resolved later in this round by X10**, which confirmed the case is genuinely reachable and fixed it.

#### TM13 — The one-shot channel used to destroy a remote tmux session is only freed when that channel reports it finished

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`

**What was wrong.** TerminalFactory::kill() opens a separate short-lived SSH channel to run the tmux kill command, and arranges for it to delete itself when its read channel finishes. If that never happens — for example the SSH session dies at exactly that moment — the object stays alive until the factory itself is destroyed, which in practice means until the application exits.

**Why it was wrong.** The self-deletion is driven only by the channel's own end-of-stream signal, with no deadline behind it.

**What was done.** NOT fixed. Each leaked object is a few hundred bytes, one per killed terminal session, in a case that requires the SSH session to die during the kill. Adding a watchdog timer for it is more machinery than the problem justifies, and the objects are all reclaimed when the factory dies. Reported for completeness rather than patched.

**Why it matters.** Negligible: a few hundred bytes per killed terminal session in a rare failure case, reclaimed at shutdown.
