*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope: src/terminal only (TerminalController, TerminalBridge, TerminalFactory and their five tests plus the two CMakeLists). I read docs/SPEC.md sections 5.1-5.6 and 6.4, src/web/terminal/src/index.ts (to check both halves of the QWebChannel contract agree), src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml, src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.{h,cpp} and src/models/SessionState.h as read-only context. No file outside the slice was written; the two CMakeLists files in the slice needed no change (no new source or test binary was added — the new tests went into the existing tst_terminalcontroller target).

Bridge contract check (both sides agree, no mismatch found): the C++ object is registered under the name 'terminal', which is what the page looks up. The page-to-C++ slots sendInput(string), resize(number,number), notifyViewVisible(boolean) and the optional ready() all exist with matching names and argument types. The C++-to-page signals write(string), connectionStateChanged(string) and clearRequested() all match the page's expectations, and the connectionState property is read from the page's cache on mount. Argument ORDER for resize is columns-then-rows consistently on both sides and all the way down through the controller to the SSH window-change request. Output is transported as text and decoded with a stateful UTF-8 decoder, so a multi-byte character split across two output batches is completed rather than corrupted — already covered by an existing test, which I confirmed rather than duplicated.

Things I checked and found genuinely correct, so did not touch: the coalescing thresholds and hidden-buffer cap are within the ranges the spec suggests; ordering across the suspend/resume boundary (retained buffer replays before newer pending output); output produced before the page mounts is buffered rather than dropped; the transport pointer is a weak pointer so a caller-destroyed channel detaches instead of dangling; every signal connection either names a receiver object that dies with the connection or is removed explicitly, and I found no duplicated or leaked connection across repeated attach/detach cycles; the controller, bridge and channel are all parented so that closing a pane releases all three; two concurrent attaches are impossible because attach() detaches first; the upper bound the bridge puts on page-supplied terminal dimensions is enforced on both dimensions independently while deliberately leaving non-positive values non-positive; the shell-quoting and tmux exact-match hardening in the tmux command builders are correct and already well covered.

On the factory and identifier collisions: terminal identifiers cannot collide, because a pane's remote tmux target is 'ch_<devSessionId>_<terminalId>' built from server-assigned identifiers, and the lookup table is keyed on the controller object itself with an entry removed when that object is destroyed, so a stale key cannot alias a new pane. There is no 'session with no terminal panes' case in the factory to test: the factory has no session-level entry point at all, it only ever acts on one pane at a time, and building the pane tree is the QML layer's job.

I ran no build and no test command, per instructions. All edits are comment changes, local logic changes inside single functions, one new static function with its declaration, and new test cases; I re-read every edited region afterwards to confirm the result is well-formed and that the new test code only uses APIs I verified exist.

### 4.9 Desktop client — editor subsystem (`ED`)

*Scope:* `src/editor/`: one controller per open remote file (load, unsaved-change tracking, save, revision and conflict detection, crash-recovery snapshots) and the bridge to the Monaco editor page.

*18 findings:* 6 active bug, 5 latent bug, 3 inconsistency, 2 documentation error, 1 footgun, 1 nit.

#### ED1 — Crash-recovery snapshots were never actually written on a real server (missing parent directory)

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditor.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditorreconnect.cpp`, `remote/src/files.ts`

**What was wrong.** The editor is supposed to keep a copy of unsaved edits on the server so they can be recovered after a client crash. It writes that copy to a hidden sub-directory next to the file being edited (for /repo/note.txt it writes /repo/.codeharbor-recovery/note.txt). Nothing ever created that sub-directory, and the server's write operation does not create missing parent directories, so every single snapshot write failed with 'no such file or directory'. The editor deliberately ignores snapshot write failures (they are best-effort), so this failed in complete silence: the whole unsaved-work recovery feature was dead in production.

**Why it was wrong.** The server's writeFile creates a temporary file in the target's directory and renames it over the target; it never called mkdir for the directory. The client cannot create it either, because the frozen remote-procedure catalogue exposes no createDirectory method. Both live end-to-end tests actually pre-created the directory with 'mkdir -p' in their fixtures and their comments said outright that writeFile does not do it — so the tests passed while the product did not work.

**What was done.** The one-line server fix is outside my slice, so I reported it to the agent who owns remote/src/files.ts; they applied it (writeFile now does mkdir with recursive=true and mode 0700 before creating its temporary file, plus a new server-side test). On my side I refreshed the now-stale comments in both live tests so they explain that the fixture still pre-creates the directory on purpose (so an unexpected failure there cannot silently swallow the snapshot and let the gate pass without exercising it).

**Why it matters.** Before: a client crash lost every unsaved edit, despite a whole recovery mechanism existing for it. After: snapshots are really written, so unsaved work survives a crash.


#### ED2 — An open file never reported the 'disconnected' state, so a pane advertised 'clean' through an outage

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The file lifecycle has ten states, one of which is 'disconnected'. It was the state the controller started in and nothing ever entered it again. When the connection to the server dropped, the pane kept displaying whatever state it was in — usually 'clean' — even though the file could then be neither read nor written. The terminal panes in this same project already report a dropped connection honestly, so the editor was the odd one out.

**Why it was wrong.** The handler for the transport-closed signal only forgot the server-side file-watch identifier; it never touched the file state. The matching handler for a new transport never restored anything either.

**What was done.** In EditorController::onTransportClosed(), when a file is open the state now moves to Disconnected. In onTransportBound() the state is un-parked before any new request goes out. Un-parking is deliberate rather than naive: a 'conflict' or 'externally modified' state is remembered across the outage (it describes the server's copy diverging from the buffer, which an outage does not resolve), while every other state is re-derived from whether the buffer has unsaved edits, because the load or save the drop killed has no outcome left to report. A new member, m_resumeState (a std::optional<FileState>), holds that decision. New test: transportDropParksTheFileStateUntilRebind, which pins the exact state sequence for both a clean and a dirty buffer.

**Why it matters.** The pane header (and anything else reading the state) now tells the truth during an outage instead of claiming the file is safely saved and in sync.


#### ED3 — A failed save left the buffer marked as clean, so the next external change silently overwrote unsaved work

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The controller tracks a 'dirty' flag meaning 'the buffer holds bytes that are not on the server'. That flag is the only thing that stops the editor from automatically re-reading the file (and replacing what the user sees) when the file changes on the server. The flag was raised only by the editor page's debounced 'here is my current buffer' report, which fires 500 milliseconds after typing stops. Pressing Ctrl+S inside that 500ms window meant the controller had never heard about the edits. If that save then FAILED (an I/O error, a permissions error, or the connection dying with the write in flight), the buffer still held edits that existed nowhere but inside the page — yet the controller believed the file was clean, so the next external change to the file triggered an automatic reload that discarded them without a word.

**Why it was wrong.** Only reportContent() set the dirty flag; save() did not, even though the content handed to save() is by definition not yet on the server.

**What was done.** save() now sets the dirty flag before issuing the write, and only the successful reply clears it. New test: failedSaveLeavesTheBufferDirty — it saves without any prior content report, fails the write, then fires an external-change notification and asserts the file is flagged rather than re-read.

**Why it matters.** Removes a silent data-loss path: edits whose save failed are no longer thrown away by the next automatic reload.


#### ED4 — Text typed while a save was in flight was treated as saved, and its recovery copy was deleted

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** A save sends a snapshot of the buffer taken at the moment Ctrl+S was pressed. If the user kept typing while that write travelled to the server and back, the reply used to clear the dirty flag outright and delete the server-side recovery copy. Those extra keystrokes were then unprotected in two ways at once: the next external change to the file triggered an automatic reload over them, and the recovery copy that held them had just been erased.

**Why it was wrong.** The save reply assumed that what was written is what the buffer holds. Nothing recorded whether an edit arrived between issuing the write and receiving its reply.

**What was done.** Added a monotonically increasing counter, m_editSerial, incremented by every content report. save() captures its value; when the reply arrives, a changed value means the buffer moved on. In that case the dirty flag stays raised, the recovery copy is NOT deleted, and the state settles on 'modified' rather than 'clean' (the new revision is still adopted and the 'saved' signal still reaches the page, because the write really did land). New test: editsDuringASaveSurviveTheSaveReply.

**Why it matters.** Removes a second silent data-loss path around saving, and stops the recovery copy from being destroyed while it is still the only place those keystrokes exist.


#### ED5 — A stale file-read reply from a superseded load could overwrite the newer file's content and revision

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Opening file A and then file B before A's read had answered left two reads outstanding. Neither reply checked whether it was still wanted, so A's late reply would push A's text into a pane showing B, and — much worse — store A's revision token as B's save guard. Every later save of B would then be refused as a conflict against a token that belongs to a different file. The same hole existed on the reload path.

**Why it was wrong.** The read callbacks only guarded against the controller having been destroyed. They did not check the path, and even a same-path check would not order two overlapping replies.

**What was done.** Added a load-generation counter, m_loadGeneration, bumped by every open() and every reload() (the same idiom the file-watch code already used). Both read callbacks drop their reply unless the generation still matches, and the generation is carried through the chained permission check and recovery probe so a superseded load cannot offer one file's unsaved edits as another's. New test: staleLoadReplyNeverWins.

**Why it matters.** Fast file switching can no longer show the wrong file's text or poison the save guard.


#### ED6 — A save, conflict-stat or recovery reply arriving after a file switch was applied to the new file

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The write callback, its conflict-fallback stat callback, and the two recovery-snapshot callbacks all identified themselves only by 'the controller still exists'. If the pane moved to a different file while any of them was in flight, the reply was applied to the NEW file: the old file's revision became the new file's save guard, a 'saved' signal was pushed to a page that never asked for one, and the new file's recovery bookkeeping was overwritten with the old file's.

**Why it was wrong.** Missing supersede checks in four asynchronous callbacks. Two neighbouring callbacks in the same file (the permission refresh and the reconnect reconciliation) already did exactly this check, so the omission was also an internal inconsistency.

**What was done.** The write callback and its stat fallback now return early if the open path changed. The recovery-write and recovery-clear callbacks compare the recovery path they were issued for against the recovery path of the file now open. New test: saveReplyAfterAFileSwitchIsDropped.

**Why it matters.** Switching files with a save in flight no longer corrupts the new file's save guard or its recovery state.


#### ED7 — A truncated (partial) read could be written back, deleting everything past the part that was read

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The server's read reply carries a 'truncated' flag meaning 'this content is only a prefix of the file'. The controller ignored that flag completely. Because saving writes the buffer as the file's entire new content, saving a truncated buffer would delete every byte past the prefix. Today the controller never requests a byte range, so the flag is always false — but the flag exists, the specification asks for a streaming view of very large files, and the first caller to pass a range would have caused silent, total data loss with no warning.

**Why it was wrong.** Only one of the two 'the bytes we hold are not the file's bytes' conditions was handled. A binary (base64) read already marked the buffer as not writable back; a truncated read did not.

**What was done.** Both the open and the reload paths now set the buffer-not-writable flag when the reply is base64 OR truncated, which makes the editor read-only and makes save() refuse with a message. Updated the member comment to state both reasons. New test: truncatedReadIsNotWritableBack.

**Why it matters.** Closes a total-data-loss trap before anyone can step in it.


#### ED8 — The conflict payload field name did not match the server, so every conflict paid for a needless extra round trip

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** When a save is refused because the file changed, the server attaches the file's current revision token to the error so the user can be offered 'reload or overwrite'. The client looked for a field named currentRevision; the server was actually sending it as actual. The field the client wanted was therefore never present, so the fast path was dead code and every conflict triggered an extra file-status request. If that extra request also failed, the client handed the page an empty revision — from which the page's 'Overwrite' button could never succeed on an existing file.

**Why it was wrong.** The two halves of the wire contract drifted apart; nothing tested the real payload shape.

**What was done.** Coordinated with the agent who owns the server file: the server now emits currentRevision (chosen because the C++ controller, the browser editor page and an existing RPC-client test all already used that name), and the client reads exactly that one field with a comment naming both payload shapes and the deleted-file case where the field is absent. I also documented, in the fallback path, why handing the page an empty revision is still correct for a deleted file (an empty expected-revision is exactly the create-only write that recreates it). New test conflictKeepsItsStateWhenTheWatchEventLands sends the server's exact error payload and asserts no extra request is made.

**Why it matters.** Conflicts resolve in one round trip instead of two, and the contract is now pinned by a test on both sides.


#### ED9 — A debounced content report arriving during a file switch snapshotted the wrong file's buffer

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The editor page reports its buffer 500 milliseconds after the user stops typing. That delay outlives a file switch. A report landing while the new file was still loading was accepted, so the PREVIOUS file's text was written to the NEW file's recovery path and the new, untouched buffer was marked as having unsaved edits. On the next open of that file the user would be offered 'recovered unsaved changes' that actually came from a different file.

**Why it was wrong.** reportContent() had no notion of 'a load is in flight, so the page is not showing this file yet'.

**What was done.** reportContent() now returns immediately while the state is Loading (the state used by open() and by an explicit reload — both cases where the page's current buffer is not this file's). New test: reportContentDuringALoadIsIgnored, which also proves the freshly loaded buffer is still treated as clean afterwards.

**Why it matters.** No more cross-file contamination of recovery snapshots, and no more phantom 'unsaved changes' on a file nobody edited.


#### ED10 — An editor page that connected late never learned the file state, leaving its status bar blank

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The browser-side editor page attaches its event handlers well after the controller exists, so every signal emitted before that is lost. The controller already held back the file content and replayed it, and already re-sent the read-only flag, but it never re-sent the file state. A page that connected after the load therefore rendered an empty status label until the next state change happened to occur.

**Why it was wrong.** The ready-handshake replay covered content and read-only-ness but not the state signal.

**What was done.** ready() now re-emits the current file state whenever a file is open (nothing open means nothing to report, so the state is not emitted then — which also keeps it from firing before a file is chosen). New test: lateReadyReplaysTheFileState.

**Why it matters.** The status bar is correct from the moment the page appears.


#### ED11 — A conflict was silently downgraded to the weaker 'externally modified' state

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** After a save was refused because the file changed on the server, the file-change notification for that very same external write arrived moments later and moved the state from 'conflict' back to 'externally modified'. 'Conflict' claims everything 'externally modified' claims plus 'a save was already refused', and it is the state the page's reload/overwrite buttons belong to, so this reported a refused save as a routine external change. One live test even contained a comment describing the resulting label as racy and unreliable.

**Why it was wrong.** Both the notification handler and the reconnect reconciliation set 'externally modified' for a dirty buffer unconditionally, with no notion of state precedence. This also contradicted the conflict-preserving behaviour I added in ED2.

**What was done.** Both places now leave an existing 'conflict' state alone. Extended the new conflict test to fire the follow-up notification and assert the state stays 'conflict' with no re-read, and rewrote the stale live-test comment.

**Why it matters.** The pane keeps showing the stronger, actionable state instead of sliding back to a weaker description of the same situation.


#### ED12 — Factory comments described 'recovery timers' that do not exist

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorFactory.h`, `src/editor/EditorFactory.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Both the header and the implementation of EditorFactory claimed that parenting a controller to its pane avoids leaking 'file watches or recovery timers'. There is no timer anywhere in the editor module — the recovery snapshot is debounced by the browser page, not by C++ — so a reader would go looking for a lifetime hazard that does not exist.

**Why it was wrong.** Comment written against an earlier design.

**What was done.** Both comments now say precisely what is released with the pane: the server-side file-watch subscription.

**Why it matters.** Documentation matches the code.


#### ED13 — refreshPermissions comment promised its continuation always runs

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.h`

**What was wrong.** The comment said the supplied continuation runs 'whether the stat succeeded or not, so a caller can chain work behind it'. There is a third case: if the open file changed while the status request was in flight, the callback returns early and the continuation never runs at all. That matters because open() chains the crash-recovery probe behind it.

**Why it was wrong.** The supersede check was added later than the comment.

**What was done.** Rewrote the comment to name the one case where the continuation is deliberately skipped, and why (the answer describes the previous file, and so would anything chained behind it).

**Why it matters.** Nobody will chain must-run work behind that call on a false promise.


#### ED14 — Test fixtures used wire values the real server never sends

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The fake-server fixtures replied with an encoding of 'utf8', but the wire contract only ever uses 'utf-8' or 'base64'. One file-change notification also used a field named 'type' where the real notification uses 'event'. Neither mattered to today's code (the controller only compares encoding against 'base64' and ignores the event kind), but a future change such as 'if encoding is not utf-8, treat it as binary' would pass every test while breaking production.

**Why it was wrong.** Fixtures hand-written from memory rather than from the wire type definitions.

**What was done.** All fixture replies now use 'utf-8', and the notification uses 'event'. New tests use the same spellings.

**Why it matters.** The fake server now mirrors the real one, so tests cannot bless a value the real server never sends.


#### ED15 — Recovery snapshots are stored somewhere other than the location the specification names

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `docs/SPEC.md`

**What was wrong.** Section 11.3 of the specification says recovery snapshots live under the user's data directory, one file per editor pane, with restrictive file permissions. The implementation instead writes a sibling hidden directory next to the file being edited. That means recovery files appear inside the user's repository (they will show up as untracked files), and per-pane identity is lost — two panes editing the same path share one snapshot.

**Why it was wrong.** Implementation diverged from the specification; nothing reconciled them.

**What was done.** NOT fixed deliberately. Changing the on-disk layout is a product decision that also needs the specification updated (a file I am not allowed to edit) and touches the live-test fixtures; the write also has no way to request a file mode, since the write parameters carry none. Recording it so the lead can route the decision. Note the mode gap was independently reported by the agent who owns the server file.

**Why it matters.** Recovery works but pollutes repositories and cannot distinguish two panes on the same file. No data loss.


**Follow-up.** **Resolved later in this round by X35**, which corrected the specification to match where the code actually stores recovery snapshots.

#### ED16 — One state in the file lifecycle enumeration is unreachable

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/models/SessionState.h`, `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The file lifecycle enumeration lists ten states. After my ED2 fix, nine of them are reachable; 'ReadOnly' is not. Read-only-ness is deliberately modelled as a separate boolean property instead, which is the better design (a file can be read-only AND modified at the same time, so it is not really a state) — but the enumeration value sits there unused, inviting someone to 'fix' it by wiring it up and thereby losing the clean/modified distinction for read-only files.

**Why it was wrong.** The enumeration was transcribed from the specification's list of things a pane should expose, which mixes states and modes.

**What was done.** NOT fixed by me: the enumeration lives in src/models, which another agent owns, and either resolution (delete the value, or document it) is their edit. Recorded for routing.

**Why it matters.** No behavioural impact today; a trap for the next person.


#### ED17 — No size limit on opening a file: a huge file is read entirely into memory

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** open() always requests the whole file, with no byte range and no prior size check. Opening a multi-gigabyte file therefore tries to load it whole on the server, ship it whole over the connection, and hold it whole in the editor page. The specification asks for very large text files to get a streaming, read-only viewer instead.

**Why it was wrong.** No size policy exists anywhere in the editor path.

**What was done.** NOT fixed. A real fix means asking for the file's size before reading it, choosing a threshold, and requesting a byte range past that threshold — which reorders the request sequence and needs the streaming viewer the specification describes, i.e. a design decision plus work outside this slice. What I did do is close the data-loss half of it (see ED7): if a ranged, truncated read is ever introduced, the buffer is now automatically read-only instead of silently truncating the file on save.

**Why it matters.** Opening a very large file can exhaust memory or stall the session. It cannot corrupt the file.


**Follow-up.** **Resolved later in this round by X11**, which added an 8 MiB ceiling to editor reads and made an over-size file open read-only.

#### ED18 — A file deleted on the server while open leaves a stale revision baseline

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** If the file is deleted on the server while open, the file-change notification triggers a re-read, the re-read fails, and the pane goes to the 'error' state with its old revision still recorded. The buffer's text is untouched (nothing is pushed to the page on a failed read), so nothing is lost. Trying to save then produces a conflict, and the page's 'Overwrite' button re-saves guarded by an empty revision, which the server treats as create-only and which therefore recreates the deleted file. In other words the recovery path happens to work correctly.

**Why it was wrong.** The delete case is not distinguished from any other read failure.

**What was done.** NOT changed, because the observable behaviour is already correct and safe. I did add a comment on the conflict-fallback path explaining why handing the page an empty revision is the right instruction for a deleted file, so the next reader does not 'fix' it into something worse.

**Why it matters.** None today. Documented so it stays correct.


**Follow-up.** **Resolved later in this round by X13**.

*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope: all of src/editor (controller, factory, both CMake files, three test files). I also read src/web/editor/src/index.ts, remote/src/files.ts, remote/src/rpc-types.ts, src/models/SessionState.h, src/remote/CodeharbordClient.{h,cpp} and specification sections 8 and 11.3 for the contracts, and edited none of them.

Bridge contract check (C++ side against src/web/editor/src/index.ts): the web-channel object name ('editor'), all six signal names and signatures, and all four slot names and signatures match exactly. No mismatch found.

Eight new tests in tst_editorcontroller.cpp, each one failing on the specific bug it defends: transportDropParksTheFileStateUntilRebind, failedSaveLeavesTheBufferDirty, editsDuringASaveSurviveTheSaveReply, reportContentDuringALoadIsIgnored, staleLoadReplyNeverWins, saveReplyAfterAFileSwitchIsDropped, truncatedReadIsNotWritableBack, lateReadyReplaysTheFileState; plus conflictKeepsItsStateWhenTheWatchEventLands, which pins the server's exact conflict payload and the sticky-conflict rule.

As instructed I ran NO build or test command, so nothing here is claimed as executed. I kept the changes compile-safe by hand: no new includes were needed (<optional> already comes in through the header), the one new member is a std::optional<FileState> assigned via a plain if/else rather than a conditional expression to avoid any type-deduction doubt, the static path helper is called from lambdas that need no captured 'this', and I dropped the now-unused 'path' capture from the two read callbacks that superseded it with a generation counter. I re-checked every pre-existing test in the three test files plus the editor assertions in src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp against each behavioural change; the states and request sequences they assert still hold. Two behavioural changes are worth the lead's attention when the suite runs: the file state now passes through 'disconnected' on every transport drop (asserted state sequences in the reconnect tests are unaffected because the rebind restores the previous state synchronously, before SessionBootstrap reports itself wired), and a save whose buffer changed mid-flight now settles on 'modified' instead of 'clean'.

I coordinated with the agent owning remote/src/files.ts on two cross-boundary items (the missing directory creation and the conflict payload field name); both are now aligned on their side, and my client reads only 'currentRevision' as they asked.

### 4.10 Desktop client — viewer subsystem (`VW`)

*Scope:* `src/viewers/`: the viewer pane list, the registry that picks a viewer for a URL or file type, the two Qt WebEngine browser profiles, and the custom URL scheme handler that serves remote files into the embedded browser.

*14 findings:* 3 active bug, 3 latent bug, 2 footgun, 2 nit, 2 missing test, 1 simplification, 1 inconsistency.

#### VW1 — Dead, misleading `resolveScheme()` function in the viewer handler registry

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.h`, `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `ViewerHandlerRegistry` exposed a second, scheme-only classifier called `resolveScheme(QString)` alongside the real `resolve(QUrl)`. Its comment claimed it was "retained for the bootstrap callers", but a repository-wide search for the name found only its own declaration and definition — there were zero callers anywhere, including tests. Worse, it disagreed with the real resolver: it mapped every `file://` URL to `TextEditor`, whereas `resolve()` correctly classifies a `file://` URL by trailing slash (directory) and then by file extension. Anyone who found it and used it would have got the wrong viewer for images, PDFs and directories.

**Why it was wrong.** Left-over from an earlier bootstrap phase of the code; the comment describing callers was never updated after the callers moved to the URL-based resolver.

**What was done.** Deleted the declaration from `ViewerHandlerRegistry.h` and the definition from `ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp`. Nothing else references it, so no call sites needed updating.

**Why it matters.** Removes a trap that would have silently returned a wrong viewer type, and removes a stale comment claiming callers that do not exist.


#### VW2 — Common source-file extensions were classified as unviewable binary downloads

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp`, `src/viewers/tests/tst_viewers.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `ViewerHandlerRegistry::resolveByExtension()` held a hard-coded list of 21 extensions that open in the text viewer. Anything not in that list fell through to the `Download` resolution, which the user interface renders as a 'binary' pane showing only metadata and a download button. That meant ordinary readable files — `.jsx`, `.java`, `.rb`, `.php`, `.kt`, `.swift`, `.cs`, `.lua`, `.bash`, `.zsh`, `.scss`, `.htm`, `.ini`, `.conf`, `.sql`, `.csv`, `.log`, `.diff`, `.patch`, `.cxx`, `.hxx`, `.cjs`, `.mts`, `.cmake` and others — could not be read in the application at all. The product specification (docs/SPEC.md section 7.5) says the source editor handles 'source code and text' generically, so the narrow list contradicted the spec.

**Why it was wrong.** The extension table was written as a minimal starter list and never widened as the viewer matured.

**What was done.** Widened the `kText` set in `ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp` from 21 to 60 entries covering mainstream languages, shells, style sheets, configuration and structured-data formats, with a comment stating the intent (anything a developer expects to read as text must not fall through to Download). Added a loop to the existing `resolveByExtensionTable()` test in `tst_viewers.cpp` asserting 25 of the newly covered extensions resolve to `TextEditor`. The image list was deliberately left alone because docs/SPEC.md section 7.5 enumerates exactly five image formats.

**Why it matters.** Files a developer would obviously want to read now open in the text viewer instead of being offered as an opaque download.


#### VW3 — Files with no extension (Makefile, Dockerfile, LICENSE, .bashrc) still resolve to a binary download

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerHandlerRegistry.cpp`, `src/viewers/tests/tst_viewers.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Extension-based dispatch cannot classify extensionless files. `Makefile`, `Dockerfile`, `LICENSE`, `README`, `CHANGELOG`, `.bashrc` and `.gitignore` all resolve to the `Download` resolution and therefore display as an opaque binary pane instead of text. The helper `extensionOf()` also deliberately treats a leading dot as 'no extension', so every dotfile lands here.

**Why it was wrong.** The registry classifies purely by file extension; there is no table keyed on whole file names.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED, deliberately. The proposed fix is a small additional lookup in `ViewerHandlerRegistry::resolve()`: when `extensionOf(path)` returns empty, consult a name table (`Makefile`, `Dockerfile`, `LICENSE`, `COPYING`, `README`, `CHANGELOG`, `AUTHORS`, `NOTICE`, `.bashrc`, `.zshrc`, `.profile`, `.gitignore`, `.gitattributes`, `.editorconfig`, `.env`) before falling back to `Download`. I did not apply it because the current behaviour is explicitly asserted by an existing test — `tst_viewers.cpp` `resolveUrlTable()` checks `file:///home/yc/Makefile` resolves to `Download` under the comment 'Extensionless / known-binary file -> download' — so it is a documented intentional decision, not an oversight. Reversing a deliberate, tested product decision needs a human call, not a bug-hunt edit.

**Why it matters.** Users cannot preview a project's Makefile, Dockerfile or licence text inside the application. Low severity: the download action still works.


#### VW4 — Internal URL scheme handler answered any HTTP method, not just GET

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`, `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h`

**What was wrong.** `InternalUrlSchemeHandler::requestStarted()` is the entry point through which the embedded browser engine (Chromium, via Qt WebEngine) fetches remote files for the privileged internal viewer. It never looked at the request method. A document rendered inside that privileged browsing context could therefore submit an HTML form, or issue a fetch with method POST/PUT/DELETE, to a `codeharbor-internal://file/<id>` address and the handler would happily answer it as though it were an ordinary read.

**Why it was wrong.** The handler was written for the navigation case only and the method field of `QWebEngineUrlRequestJob` was never consulted.

**What was done.** Added an early gate at the top of `requestStarted()`: if `job->requestMethod()` is not `GET` (case-insensitively) the job is failed with `QWebEngineUrlRequestJob::RequestDenied` and no remote call is made. Documented the read-only contract in the class comment in `InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h`.

**Why it matters.** The privileged origin is now explicitly read-only. No behaviour change for real navigations and subresource loads, which are always GET.


#### VW5 — Internal URL scheme handler ignored the URL authority, letting one file appear under many web origins

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The only documented address shape is `codeharbor-internal://file/<opaque-id>` — the authority (host) part is the fixed string `file`. `requestStarted()` extracted the identifier from the path and never checked the host, so `codeharbor-internal://anything/<id>` served the exact same bytes. In web-browser terms scheme+host+port is the security origin, so a rendered page could mint an unlimited family of distinct origins that all serve the same privileged content, which is exactly the kind of thing used to sidestep same-origin restrictions.

**Why it was wrong.** The handler parsed only the path component of the incoming URL and assumed the authority was always the one the application mints.

**What was done.** Added a check immediately after reading the request URL: if `requestUrl.host()` is not `file` (case-insensitively) the job is failed with `QWebEngineUrlRequestJob::UrlNotFound`, before any identifier lookup or remote read. Production code only ever mints the `file` authority (see `InternalUrlMap::prefix()`), so nothing legitimate is affected.

**Why it matters.** Closes an origin-multiplication surface. Latent rather than active because scripting is disabled in the shipped internal viewer views, so nothing can currently forge such a URL.


#### VW6 — Malformed base64 payload was served to the browser as a successful but empty document

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`, `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Both the scheme handler and `ViewerModel::readTextFile()` decoded a base64-encoded file body with `QByteArray::fromBase64()`. That function has no error reporting: given corrupt input it silently returns an empty or partially decoded byte array. The scheme handler would then reply success with zero bytes, so the user sees an apparently empty file rather than an error; `readTextFile()` would emit `textFileRead` with an empty string for the same reason.

**Why it was wrong.** Use of the lenient `fromBase64()` overload instead of the checked `fromBase64Encoding()` overload.

**What was done.** Both call sites now use `QByteArray::fromBase64Encoding(std::move(encoded), QByteArray::Base64Encoding | QByteArray::AbortOnBase64DecodingErrors)` and test the returned result object. On failure the scheme handler calls `job->fail(RequestFailed)` and `ViewerModel::readTextFile()` emits `textFileError(path, "file contents could not be decoded")`. Added `#include <utility>` at the top of both files for `std::move`.

**Why it matters.** A corrupted or truncated response can no longer masquerade as a legitimately empty file. Latent because the remote service currently always produces valid base64.


#### VW7 — Text and XML responses were served without a character-set declaration

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`, `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h`

**What was wrong.** The scheme handler replied with the bare MIME type derived from the file extension, for example `text/plain`, `text/html` or `text/markdown`, with no `; charset=...` parameter. The bytes it hands over are UTF-8 (they come from `QString::toUtf8()` or a base64 decode of UTF-8 content). With no declared character set the browser engine falls back to a locale-derived guess, so a UTF-8 file containing accented letters, CJK text or emoji renders as garbled characters.

**Why it was wrong.** `mimeForPath()` returns a bare type and its result was passed straight to `QWebEngineUrlRequestJob::reply()` without adding response parameters.

**What was done.** In the reply path, compute a `textual` flag (the type starts with `text/`, or ends with `+xml`, or is exactly `application/xml` or `application/json`) and append `; charset=utf-8` to the content type in that case. The security gate `isActiveContentMime()` is still evaluated against the bare type, and it already strips any trailing parameter, so the Content-Security-Policy decision is unchanged. Updated the `mimeForPath()` comment in the header to say it returns the bare type and that the handler adds the charset when replying.

**Why it matters.** Non-ASCII text and markup now render correctly instead of as mojibake.


#### VW8 — `ViewerModel::readTextFile()` performed an unbounded remote read

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp`, `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h`, `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h`, `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The scheme handler caps a single inline render at 8 MiB by sending `offset` and `length` with its read request, and fails the request if the server reports the file was truncated. `ViewerModel::readTextFile()`, which the text viewer pane calls (see `src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml`), sent neither field. Reading the remote service (`remote/src/files.ts`) confirms that with no `offset`/`length` it takes the whole-file branch and returns the entire file in one JSON-RPC message. Opening a large log file or database dump in the text pane therefore tried to pull the whole thing across the SSH channel and into memory in a single message, on both machines.

**Why it was wrong.** Two independent code paths read remote files and only one of them was given a size bound; the bound was a file-local constant in the scheme handler's anonymous namespace, so the other path could not reuse it.

**What was done.** Promoted the constant out of the anonymous namespace in `InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp` into the class as a documented public constant, `InternalUrlSchemeHandler::kMaxInlineReadBytes` (8 MiB). `ViewerModel::readTextFile()` now sends `offset: 0` and `length: kMaxInlineReadBytes`, and when the server's reply carries `truncated: true` it emits `textFileError(path, "file is too large to display inline")` instead of showing a silent prefix of the file. Updated the `readTextFile()` documentation comment in `ViewerModel.h`. Verified against `remote/src/files.ts` that `truncated` is computed as `offset + length < size`, so it is true exactly when the file exceeds the window.

**Why it matters.** A huge file now produces a clear error instead of a very large memory spike and a long stall, and the two remote-read paths behave identically.


#### VW9 — `ViewerModel::setProfiles()` could delete the object it was being handed

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `ViewerModel` lazily creates and owns a `ViewerProfiles` object if nobody injects one. `setProfiles()` deleted the owned object before storing the new pointer. If a caller ever passed back the very pointer `ViewerModel` already owned — for example `model.setProfiles(model.profiles())` in a re-initialisation path — the object would be deleted and then stored, leaving a dangling pointer that every later call to `externalProfile()` / `internalProfile()` would dereference.

**Why it was wrong.** Classic missing self-assignment guard in an ownership-transferring setter.

**What was done.** Added `if (profiles == m_profiles) return;` as the first statement of `ViewerModel::setProfiles()`, with a comment explaining why.

**Why it matters.** Removes a use-after-free that no current caller triggers but that a plausible refactor would.


#### VW10 — Directory listing sort had an unspecified order for names differing only in letter case

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerModel.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `ViewerModel::listDirectory()` sorts the entries it hands to the user interface with `std::sort` and a comparator that compares names case-insensitively. Two entries in the same directory whose names differ only in case (for example `Readme` and `readme`, which coexist fine on a Linux server) compare as equal in both directions. `std::sort` is not a stable sort, so their relative order is unspecified and can change between runs or between library versions, making the pane order non-reproducible.

**Why it was wrong.** A case-insensitive comparison used as a strict weak ordering without a tie-break.

**What was done.** The comparator now falls back to a case-sensitive comparison of the same two names when the case-insensitive comparison reports equality, giving a total order. Comment updated to say why.

**Why it matters.** Directory listings are now deterministic.


#### VW11 — No unit test covered `ViewerModel` at all

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/tests/tst_viewers.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `tst_viewers.cpp` tested the handler registry, the URL map, the MIME helpers and the browser-profile isolation, but never instantiated `ViewerModel` — the single object the QML user interface talks to. In particular the strings returned by `viewKind()` (`web`, `markdown`, `text`, `image`, `pdf`, `directory`, `binary`) form a hard contract with `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml`, which switches on them; renaming one would silently degrade every pane to the fallback with no test failure. The 'no remote client connected' behaviour was also untested.

**Why it was wrong.** Test coverage grew around the pure helper classes and never reached the facade.

**What was done.** Added two test slots. `viewKindStringsMatchQmlContract()` pins all seven view-kind strings, including that unknown schemes and download-only files both collapse onto `binary`. `viewerModelWithoutClientReportsErrors()` constructs a `ViewerModel` with no remote client, calls `readTextFile()` and `listDirectory()`, and uses `QSignalSpy` to assert that exactly one error signal fires for each, that no success signal fires, and that the failing path is echoed back in the signal so a view can tell whether the error concerns the file it is showing.

**Why it matters.** The QML-facing contract is now defended by tests that fail on a plausible rename or on a regression that makes the disconnected case silently do nothing.


#### VW12 — Documented 'bare identifier' lookup on the URL map was untested

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/viewers/tests/tst_viewers.cpp`

**What was wrong.** `InternalUrlMap::fileUrlFor()` documents that it accepts either a full `codeharbor-internal://file/<id>` address or a bare identifier. Every existing test passed a full address, so the bare-identifier branch — which is the branch the scheme handler effectively exercises through `fileUrlForId()` — had no coverage, and neither did the requirement that an unknown identifier yields an invalid result rather than being aliased onto some other entry.

**Why it was wrong.** Tests were written from the QML caller's perspective, which always has the full address.

**What was done.** Added the `urlMappingBareId()` test slot: it mints an address for a file, splits off the raw identifier, and asserts that the full address, the bare identifier via `fileUrlFor()`, and the identifier via `fileUrlForId()` all return the same original file address, and that a made-up 32-character identifier returns an invalid result.

**Why it matters.** Both documented spellings of the lookup are now pinned.


#### VW13 — Scheme handler and viewer model hold raw pointers to the remote client

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h`, `src/viewers/ViewerModel.h`

**What was wrong.** `InternalUrlSchemeHandler` and `ViewerModel` both store an unowned raw `CodeharbordClient *`. If the client object is ever destroyed while the viewer objects are still alive, the next request dereferences freed memory. Today this cannot happen: in `src/app/main.cpp` the client is declared before `ViewerProfiles` and `ViewerModel`, so it is destroyed after them, and the live and QML tests follow the same order.

**Why it was wrong.** Plain unowned pointers with a lifetime rule enforced only by declaration order in `main()`.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. The mechanical fix is to change both members to `QPointer<CodeharbordClient>`, which self-clears when the client is destroyed; the existing null checks in both classes would then handle it. I did not apply it because there is no reachable failure today, the rest of the code base consistently uses raw unowned pointers for injected collaborators, and changing an ownership convention in one module only would introduce a second way of doing the same thing — which the project's conventions explicitly forbid.

**Why it matters.** None today. Recorded so that a future reordering in `main.cpp` is recognised as dangerous.


#### VW14 — Verified: the Qt 6.9 reasoning in ViewerProfiles is still accurate, and the request lifetime handling is correct

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/viewers/ViewerProfiles.cpp`, `src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Two things the assignment asked me to scrutinise turned out to be correct, and I am recording that rather than inventing a problem. (1) `ViewerProfiles::internalProfile()` constructs the privileged browser profile through the storage-name overload with an empty name, with a long comment explaining that the plain parent-only constructor emits a deprecation warning from Qt 6.9 onward. I checked the installed Qt 6.10.2 header `qquickwebengineprofile.h`: both constructors still exist and both are still public, so the comment's premise holds and the workaround is still needed. (2) The request lifetime in `requestStarted()` is handled correctly: a `QPointer` guards the job across the asynchronous remote call so a job Chromium already destroyed is never touched; the reply device is a `QBuffer` parented to the job, which is Qt's documented ownership pattern; and I read `CodeharbordClient::call()` in `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp` to confirm its documented contract — it either registers the callback or invokes it exactly once with a transport error, and the response dispatcher erases the pending entry before invoking. So the job is always replied to exactly once and never twice, including when the connection is closed.

**Why it was wrong.** Not applicable; this is a verification note.

**What was done.** No change made.

**Why it matters.** None.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* All edits are confined to src/viewers/ as assigned: ViewerHandlerRegistry.h/.cpp, InternalUrlSchemeHandler.h/.cpp, ViewerModel.h/.cpp and tests/tst_viewers.cpp. Neither CMakeLists.txt under src/viewers needed a change (no files added or removed from the build) and tst_liveviewers.cpp was read but needed no change. Per the instructions I ran no build, compile or test command; correctness was checked by reading the installed Qt 6.10.2 headers for every API I introduced (QByteArray::compare with a QByteArrayView and case sensitivity, QByteArray::fromBase64Encoding with AbortOnBase64DecodingErrors, QWebEngineUrlRequestJob::RequestDenied, QQuickWebEngineProfile's two constructors), by confirming brace balance in every edited file, and by confirming declaration/definition parity for the three new test slots. Two new top-level includes were added, both in the include block at the top of their file: <utility> in InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp, and <QByteArray> plus <utility> in ViewerModel.cpp. One behavioural change is worth the lead's attention when the suite runs: ViewerModel::readTextFile now sends offset/length and rejects files over 8 MiB, so any live test that reads a very large fixture file through the text pane would now see an error signal instead of content; I found no such fixture in tst_liveviewers.cpp. No scaffolding, placeholders or TODO comments were introduced.

### 4.11 Desktop client — agent-status subsystem (`AG`)

*Scope:* `src/agent/`: parsing agent-status events off the wire and tracking, per terminal, the last state its coding agent reported plus an "unseen finished work" flag.

*14 findings:* 4 missing test, 3 footgun, 2 nit, 1 active bug, 1 simplification, 1 lint, 1 documentation error, 1 latent bug.

#### AG1 — A second agent completion is silently swallowed after the user clears the first, so the "finished, unseen work" badge never comes back and no notification is raised

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp`, `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h`, `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The class AgentStatusMonitor tracks, for each terminal, the last state its coding agent reported (starting / running / waiting for input / finished-with-unseen-output / idle / error / stopped / unknown), and separately keeps a per-workspace flag meaning "this workspace has finished work the user has not looked at yet". The user clears that flag by invoking markSeen(). markSeen() deliberately clears ONLY the flag; it leaves the terminal's last reported state at 'finished-with-unseen-output' (the display layer, AppController::rebuildRows, downgrades it to plain 'idle' for rendering while the flag is clear). The old applyEvent() computed `changed = (previous state != new state)` and returned early when `changed` was false, BEFORE doing anything with the unseen flag or the notification. So the sequence "agent finishes -> user clicks Mark Agent Output Seen -> agent finishes again" was lost: the second completion event carried the same state the monitor already had stored, so it was discarded as a duplicate. The badge stayed off and no desktop notification was raised for work the user genuinely had not seen. This is reachable today whenever two completion events arrive with no other state in between — the two harness adapters remote/src/adapters/oh-my-pi.ts and remote/src/adapters/pi.ts both map two distinct harness events ('agent_end' and 'settled') to the same 'finished' state, so a harness that fires both produces exactly that back-to-back pair; a re-fired or retried hook does the same.

**Why it was wrong.** The unseen-work bookkeeping and the notification were placed behind a state-transition guard, but they are properties of the EVENT (a completion happened), not of the stored state. Because markSeen() intentionally does not reset the stored state, the stored state is a lossy proxy for "has the user been told about the latest completion".

**What was done.** In src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp, applyEvent() was restructured. It still computes `changed` and still emits agentStateChanged() only on a real transition, but the early `return` was removed. A new local `armedUnseen` is true when the incoming event's state is 'finished-with-unseen-output' AND the workspace was not already flagged; when true the flag is set and unseenChanged(id, true) is emitted, regardless of whether the stored state changed. The notification now fires when either (a) there is a genuine transition into 'waiting for input' or 'finished-with-unseen-output', or (b) `armedUnseen` is true — one combined `if`, so a normal first completion still produces exactly one notification. Duplicate-suppression is preserved: a repeat completion while the workspace is STILL flagged unseen changes nothing and emits nothing. A comment was added marking the point past which the QHash reference and iterator must not be touched, because a connected slot can re-enter applyEvent() and rehash the container. The header documentation for markSeen(), hasUnseen() and notify() was updated to state these semantics. A regression test, completionAfterMarkSeenReArmsUnseen(), was added to src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp.

**Why it matters.** Before the fix, a user who reviewed one agent result and then let the agent run again could get no badge and no notification for the second result — the exact failure the whole agent-awareness subsystem exists to prevent. After the fix the badge re-arms and one notification carrying the new completion's own summary text is raised.


#### AG2 — The list of valid wire state words was written out twice, so adding a new state to one copy and not the other silently corrupts terminal state

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentEvent.h`

**What was wrong.** src/agent/AgentEvent.h had two independent hand-written copies of the eight state words that can appear on the wire ('starting', 'running', 'waiting_input', 'idle_unseen', 'idle', 'error', 'stopped', 'unknown'): agentStateFromWire(), which converts a word to the C++ enum, and detail::isAgentStateWire(), which decides whether a word is valid at all. The two exist for a real reason — the converter cannot distinguish the legitimate word 'unknown' from a word it does not recognise, since both come out as the enum value Unknown, while the validator must reject only the latter. But keeping them as two literal lists means a future person adding a ninth state to the wire contract has two places to edit. Editing only the validator makes the new state parse but be recorded as 'unknown', wiping whatever the terminal was really doing; editing only the converter makes every event carrying the new state be thrown away as malformed.

**Why it was wrong.** Two derived views of one contract were each written as a separate literal table instead of being derived from a single table.

**What was done.** Introduced ch::agentStateFromWireStrict(QStringView) returning std::optional<AgentState> as the single token table: it returns the enum for a recognised word (including the enum value Unknown for the word 'unknown') and std::nullopt for anything else. agentStateFromWire() is now a one-line wrapper returning .value_or(AgentState::Unknown), and detail::isAgentStateWire() is a one-line wrapper returning .has_value(). Both public behaviours are byte-for-byte unchanged. All eight words were re-checked against AGENT_STATES in remote/src/events.ts and against the eight-value list in docs/SPEC.md section 6.4 — identical, same spelling, same order. The four harness names in detail::isHarnessWire() were likewise re-checked against HARNESSES in remote/src/events.ts ('generic', 'oh-my-pi', 'pi', 'claude-code') — identical. Added test strictWireMappingSeparatesUnknownFromGarbage() covering the genuine-vs-garbage distinction, exactness (no case folding, no trimming, no prefix matching), and that the strict and lenient mappings agree on every valid word.

**Why it matters.** There is now one place to edit when the wire contract grows a state, and the previously untested distinction between a legitimate 'unknown' and an unrecognised word is pinned by a test.


#### AG3 — The parser looked the state word up in the table twice per event

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentEvent.h`

**What was wrong.** parseAgentEventLine() validated the state field by calling detail::isAgentStateWire(vState.toString()), and then, roughly ten lines later, converted the same string again with agentStateFromWire(vState.toString()). That is two string-to-enum table walks and two QString temporaries per event, and it leaves open the possibility of the validating call and the converting call drifting apart.

**Why it was wrong.** Validation and conversion were written as separate passes over the same field.

**What was done.** parseAgentEventLine() now checks that the field is a JSON string, then resolves it once with the new agentStateFromWireStrict(); std::nullopt means malformed and the event is dropped, and the resolved enum is assigned directly (`ev.state = *state;`).

**Why it matters.** One lookup instead of two, and the validated value and the stored value can no longer disagree.


#### AG4 — The read-buffer size cap was typed `int` while the size it is compared against is a 64-bit type

- **Kind:** Lint
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** kMaxLineBytes, the 1 MiB cap on how much of an unterminated line the monitor will hold, was declared `constexpr int`. It is compared against m_readBuffer.size(), which returns Qt's qsizetype (a 64-bit signed integer on this platform). The comparison is correct today because the int is promoted, but a mixed-width comparison against a container size is exactly the shape that some compilers and static analysers flag, and the type is wrong in principle for a byte count.

**Why it was wrong.** The constant was typed for its literal value rather than for what it is compared with.

**What was done.** Changed to `constexpr qsizetype kMaxLineBytes = 1 * 1024 * 1024;` in src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change; removes a mixed-width comparison.


#### AG5 — Header documentation understated or omitted three contracts of the monitor: same-pointer rebinding, what markSeen does not clear, and that the unseen flag is per workspace

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h`

**What was wrong.** Three inaccuracies/omissions in the public documentation of src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h. (1) setTransport() was documented as "Rebinding rewires readyRead and resets the partial-line buffer", but the function starts with `if (m_transport == transport) return;` — handing it the device that is already bound resets nothing, buffer included. A reader taking the comment at face value could use setTransport(sameDevice) as a way to flush a stuck partial line, and it would do nothing at all. (2) markSeen() was documented purely as "clear the flag", with no mention that it deliberately leaves each terminal's stored state at 'finished-with-unseen-output' and relies on the display layer to downgrade it — the single most surprising thing about this class, and the thing bug AG1 came from. (3) hasUnseen() did not say whether the flag is global, per workspace, or per terminal; it is per workspace, so any one terminal finishing flags the whole workspace and one markSeen() clears it for all of that workspace's terminals.

**Why it was wrong.** Comments described the intended shape rather than the code as written, and left the most surprising invariant implicit.

**What was done.** Rewrote the three doc comments in src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h: setTransport() now states that a half-received line is dropped (bytes from a dead producer must never be spliced onto a new producer's first frame), that accumulated per-workspace state is deliberately preserved across the swap because it is the user's state and not the wire's, and that passing the already-bound device is a no-op including the buffer. markSeen() now states that the terminals' stored states are untouched, names AppController::rebuildRows as the downgrading consumer, and states that a later completion re-arms the flag (AG1). hasUnseen() now states the per-workspace scoping and cites SPEC section 4.2, which is what folds a workspace's terminals into one sidebar row state. The notify() signal comment now also mentions the re-arm case from AG1.

**Why it matters.** The three contracts a future maintainer is most likely to break are now written down at the declarations.


#### AG6 — Nothing recorded that the event timestamp is untrusted wall-clock data and must never be used to order or discard events

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentEvent.h`

**What was wrong.** Each event carries a `timestamp` field. remote/src/events.ts documents it as "ISO-8601 timestamp with milliseconds" but its validator only checks that it IS a string; the C++ parser matches that exactly, storing the raw string and never parsing it. Nothing in the C++ code said why. That is a trap: the obvious-looking hardening for a reader worried about out-of-order events is "compare timestamps and ignore anything older than what we already applied". That would be actively harmful here. The timestamps are wall-clock readings taken independently inside separate short-lived hook processes on the remote machine (see remote/src/events.ts makeEvent(), which defaults to `new Date().toISOString()`), so they are not monotonic and not guaranteed distinct, and a clock adjustment going backwards would cause live state to be thrown away. Ordering is already guaranteed structurally: one producer writes to one socket which is relayed over one channel, a single ordered byte stream, and the monitor applies events strictly in arrival order with the last one winning for a given terminal.

**Why it was wrong.** An intentional decision (treat the timestamp as opaque display data, rely on stream order for sequencing) was implemented but never written down.

**What was done.** Added an explanatory comment to the AgentEvent struct in src/agent/AgentEvent.h stating that the timestamp is intentionally kept as the producer's opaque string, that events.ts validates only that it is a string and the client matches that exactly, that ordering comes from the single ordered byte stream, and explicitly warning not to start comparing timestamps to reorder or drop events, with the monotonicity and clock-step reasons.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change. Removes the most likely wrong "improvement" a future maintainer would make to this parser.


#### AG7 — Per-terminal state entries and unseen flags are never removed, so the maps grow for the lifetime of the client

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h`

**What was wrong.** m_states (a map from workspace id to a map from terminal id to that terminal's agent state) and m_unseen (a set of workspace ids) only ever gain entries. Closing a terminal, deleting a workspace, or disconnecting leaves the entries behind, and there is no API to drop them. The growth is bounded by the number of distinct (workspace, terminal) pairs ever observed in one run of the client, and each entry is two short strings plus an enum, so the practical cost is negligible; the ids are minted by the server and never reused, so a stale entry can never be mistaken for a live one and there is no correctness bug. I did NOT add an eviction API, for two reasons. First, evicting on terminal close would destroy the very 'finished-with-unseen-output' state the sidebar badge is derived from (see AG1 and AppController::rebuildRows), so naive eviction would be a regression. Second, the only place that knows a workspace was deleted is src/app/AppController.cpp, which belongs to another agent in this review, so any API I added would have no caller and would be dead code.

**Why it was wrong.** The class has no lifecycle notification for terminal or workspace removal, and adding one crosses a module boundary owned by another slice.

**What was done.** Not fixed by code change. Instead the retention policy is now documented at the two member declarations in src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h: entries are never evicted, why a stale entry is inert, why evicting on terminal close would be wrong, and the guidance that if a workspace ever grows large enough to matter the correct unit of eviction is a whole workspace subtree on deletion, never a single terminal.

**Why it matters.** No user-visible impact today. The next person to look at the maps now knows the retention is deliberate and what the safe eviction unit would be.


#### AG8 — The event parser's per-field type checks were largely untested; a wrong-typed field would have been silently coerced to an empty string

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** parseRejectsMalformed() covered blank input, non-JSON, a JSON array, three bad `version` shapes, an invalid harness name, one missing required field, an invalid state word, and `metadata` being JSON null. It did NOT cover a wrong TYPE in timestamp, harness, devSessionId, terminalId, state, event or summary, nor `metadata` being a JSON array, nor the forward-compatibility rule that unknown extra fields must be ignored rather than rejected. Those checks matter because Qt's QJsonValue::toString() returns an empty string for a non-string value rather than failing — so if the type check for devSessionId or terminalId were ever dropped, an event carrying a number there would be filed under the empty id, invisible to the sidebar, instead of being rejected.

**Why it was wrong.** The existing test asserted a representative sample of rejections rather than the field-by-field contract that remote/src/events.ts validateEvent() defines.

**What was done.** Extended parseRejectsMalformed() in src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp with: a metadata-as-JSON-array rejection; a table-driven loop that substitutes the number 7 into each of timestamp, harness, devSessionId, terminalId, state, event and summary in turn and asserts each one is rejected (the failure message names the offending field); and a positive case proving an event carrying an unrecognised extra field still parses, matching the structural (not exhaustive) validation in remote/src/events.ts so a newer producer cannot break an older client. Added the <QJsonArray>, <QJsonValue> and <QStringView> includes at the top of the file with the existing includes.

**Why it matters.** Every field of the wire contract is now pinned on the C++ side, field for field against the TypeScript validator.


#### AG9 — No test proved the unseen-work flag is scoped to one workspace rather than being global

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The flag is stored in a QSet of workspace ids, so it is per workspace. The existing tests only ever used a single workspace id, so a change that made the flag global — for example a single bool member — would have left the whole suite green while lighting up the "finished, unseen work" badge on every workspace in the sidebar at once, and letting one markSeen() clear all of them.

**Why it was wrong.** Test coverage used one workspace id throughout.

**What was done.** Added unseenIsPerDevSession() to src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp: a completion in workspace A flags A and leaves B unflagged; a completion in workspace B flags B; marking A seen leaves B still flagged; both terminals' stored states remain in their own sub-maps; and a terminal id from one workspace queried under the other workspace reports 'unknown', proving the two-level map is not being flattened.

**Why it matters.** Per-workspace scoping is now a pinned contract instead of an accident of the data structure.


#### AG10 — No test proved the monitor tolerates carriage-return-plus-newline line framing

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** onReadyRead() splits on the newline byte and then strips a trailing carriage return, and the parser additionally trims whitespace, so carriage-return-plus-newline framing works. Nothing tested it, so the tolerance could have been removed without any test failing — and the code path is not obviously dead, since the framing tolerance exists precisely for a relay or shell wrapper that translates line endings.

**Why it was wrong.** Test coverage assumed the ideal producer, which frames with a bare newline.

**What was done.** Added crlfFramedLinesAreAccepted() to src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp: feeds a bare carriage-return-newline (which must count as a blank line and be skipped, not treated as a malformed event) followed by a valid event re-framed with carriage-return-newline, and asserts the event is delivered and the terminal's state is recorded.

**Why it matters.** The line-ending tolerance is now an asserted contract.


#### AG11 — Duplicate-event suppression was only asserted for the unseen flag, not for the state signal or the notification

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** In runningToIdleUnseenSetsUnseenAndNotify(), the block feeding a second identical completion event asserted only that the unseen-flag signal count stayed at 1. It did not assert that no extra agentStateChanged and no extra notification were emitted. Since I widened the notification condition as part of the AG1 fix, that block is exactly where an over-broad condition would show up as notification spam, and it was not being checked.

**Why it was wrong.** The existing assertion checked one of the three signals the duplicate must not produce.

**What was done.** Strengthened the block in src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp to also assert the agentStateChanged count and the notification count are unchanged after the duplicate, with a comment explaining that a repeat while the workspace is still flagged must produce nothing at all because the user is already being told about unseen work.

**Why it matters.** The notification rate-limiting behaviour of the widened condition from AG1 is now pinned in both directions: it fires for a genuinely new completion (AG1's test) and stays silent for a duplicate (this test).


#### AG12 — Assertion helpers in the live agent test do not abort the calling test function, producing misleading cascade failures

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp`

**What was wrong.** src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp has a void helper fireHook() containing three QVERIFY2 assertions. In the Qt Test framework, a failing QVERIFY only returns from the function it textually appears in. So when a hook firing fails inside fireHook(), the test is correctly marked failed, but control returns to the calling test function, which then proceeds to wait for a state change that can never arrive and reports a second, confusing timeout failure that buries the real cause. The same shape appears in the runRemote() usage inside the helper. I did NOT change it: the whole-repository grep for the standard Qt remedy (checking QTest::currentTestFailed() after each helper call) found zero uses anywhere in this codebase, so introducing it here would be a second convention in a project whose live tests consistently use this idiom, which the review brief explicitly forbids. Noted for the maintainers as a repository-wide test-idiom decision rather than a local fix. Note also that this entire test file skips itself unless the environment variable CH_LIVE_SSH is set, so it does not run in the default suite.

**Why it was wrong.** A Qt Test framework property: QVERIFY expands to a `return` in the enclosing function only, so assertions inside a void helper cannot abort the caller.

**What was done.** Not fixed. The remedy is to guard each fireHook() call site with a check of QTest::currentTestFailed(), or to make fireHook() return bool and wrap each call in QVERIFY. Either is a repository-wide test-convention change and is out of scope for a single module.

**Why it matters.** Diagnostic quality only, in a test that only runs against a real SSH fixture. No production behaviour is affected.


#### AG13 — The 1 MiB line cap bounds retained buffer size, not peak allocation, and cannot be covered by a behavioural test

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** onReadyRead() appends everything the transport currently has (readAll()) to m_readBuffer, consumes whole lines, and only then clears the buffer if it exceeds the 1 MiB cap. Two consequences. First, the cap bounds what is RETAINED between reads, not the peak: a single delivery of, say, ten megabytes with no newline in it is appended in full before the check trims it, so peak allocation is one transport chunk plus one megabyte. Tightening that would mean reading a bounded amount per call and leaving the rest in the device, which for a sequential device risks never receiving another readyRead and stalling the stream — a worse failure than the one being prevented. Second, the cap has no behaviourally observable effect: with or without it, a runaway unterminated line ends up being discarded (either mid-flight by the cap, or as one giant unparseable line once a newline finally arrives), and a valid event following it is delivered either way. I therefore deliberately did NOT add a test for it, rather than write a test that appears to cover the cap but would pass with the cap deleted. Flagging that honestly instead. The existing comment on the constant is accurate as written — it claims only that the read buffer must not grow without bound, which is true.

**Why it was wrong.** The guard is a memory-safety backstop against a malformed peer, and memory ceilings are not observable through the class's signals or accessors.

**What was done.** Not fixed and intentionally not tested. Restructuring to bound peak allocation would risk stalling the stream, and a behavioural test for the cap is not possible.

**Why it matters.** None in practice: agent events are a state word plus a short summary, so the cap is never approached by a well-behaved producer.


#### AG14 — The carriage-return strip in onReadyRead is redundant with the trimming the parser already does

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
