- **Files:** `src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp`

**What was wrong.** onReadyRead() strips a single trailing carriage return from each extracted line, but every path out of it either skips the line (after calling QByteArray::trimmed() on it) or hands it to parseAgentEventLine(), which itself starts by trimming. So removing the strip would change no behaviour. I left it in place: it is two lines, it is self-documenting about the framing the monitor tolerates, and deleting it in the middle of a bug-hunting pass with no compiler run available buys nothing. It is now covered by the new crlfFramedLinesAreAccepted() test (AG10), which asserts the tolerance rather than the mechanism, so the test stays valid whichever layer provides it.

**Why it was wrong.** Whitespace tolerance is implemented at two layers, the framing layer and the parsing layer.

**What was done.** Not fixed; left as intentional, self-documenting redundancy and now covered by a test that does not depend on which layer does the work.

**Why it matters.** None.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope: I wrote only to src/agent/AgentEvent.h, src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.h, src/agent/AgentStatusMonitor.cpp and src/agent/tests/tst_agentmonitor.cpp. src/agent/CMakeLists.txt and src/agent/tests/CMakeLists.txt were read and are correct as they stand: ch_agent links ch_models publicly so the test target inherits it, tst_agentmonitor links Qt6::Network for the QLocalServer/QLocalSocket pair it uses, and tst_liveagent is registered unconditionally with the 'live' label and self-skips. No new source or test file was added, so no CMake change was needed. src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp was read in full and left unmodified (see finding AG12 for the one issue found in it).

Wire-format cross-check performed against remote/src/events.ts, remote/src/bridge.ts, remote/src/hooks/oh-my-pi-hook.ts and all four files in remote/src/adapters/, plus docs/SPEC.md sections 4.2, 6.1-6.6. Results: the eight state words and the four harness names agree exactly between src/agent/AgentEvent.h, remote/src/events.ts and docs/SPEC.md section 6.4. The version constant is 1 on both sides. The version comparison in C++ deliberately uses a double compare so a fractional 1.5 is rejected the way TypeScript's strict equality rejects it, which is correct and already tested. Field-level acceptance now matches validateEvent() exactly, including accepting unknown extra fields and rejecting JSON null or an array for metadata. Every state word produced by every adapter is within the eight. The live test's expected bridge banner string matches the literal in remote/src/bridge.ts line 140, and the environment variable names it sets match the ones the hook reads.

One uncertainty stated plainly: for finding AG1 I am confident the swallowing behaviour existed and is wrong, and confident the fix is correct and keeps all pre-existing assertions in this file, in src/app/tests/tst_appcontroller.cpp (markSeenClearsFinishedUnseenBadge), in src/app/tests/tst_notifier.cpp (whose duplicate-suppression case uses 'waiting_input', which my change does not touch) and in src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp satisfied. What I cannot state as verified is the exact real-world frequency of two back-to-back completion events: the two harness adapters do map two distinct harness events to the same completion state, which makes it clearly reachable, but I did not run the live gate to observe it, so I have labelled the mechanism rather than claimed a field-observed frequency.

Per the brief I ran no build, no ctest, no npm and no tsc. All changes were made to be compile-safe by inspection: no new includes beyond <QJsonArray>, <QJsonValue> and <QStringView> at the top of the test file; std::optional was already included in AgentEvent.h; the QJsonObject::insert overload in the new table-driven test takes a QString to avoid any QLatin1StringView overload ambiguity; and no signal, slot, or public function signature changed, so no consumer outside src/agent/ needs recompiling for a signature reason.

### 4.12 Desktop client — QML user interface (`QM`)

*Scope:* `src/qml/`: the whole window (sessions sidebar, viewer region, terminal region), the connect sheet, the command palette, the pane views, and the six viewer views.

*18 findings:* 5 active bug, 4 inconsistency, 2 latent bug, 2 footgun, 2 missing test, 1 documentation error, 1 lint, 1 simplification.

#### QM1 — The sessions sidebar footer says "Not connected" while the application is actually waiting for the user's password

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml`

**What was wrong.** The sidebar has a one-line status footer at the bottom that reports whether the remote server is reachable. It translates a state word supplied by the C++ layer into a colour, a small glyph and a sentence. The C++ layer publishes seven different words, but the footer only had cases for six of them. The missing one is "credential", which the application enters when the SSH connection attempt was deliberately stopped so the user could be asked for a password or a private-key passphrase. Because that word fell through to the catch-all branch, the footer painted a grey dash and the sentence "Not connected" — i.e. it denied that anything was in progress at the exact moment the application was waiting for the user to type a secret. Once the connection sheet is closed this footer is the only connection status visible anywhere in the window.

**Why it was wrong.** Three `switch` statements in SessionsSidebar.qml (linkColor, linkGlyph, linkWords) enumerate the connection-state words by hand. The word "credential" was added to ch::AppController::setConnectionState() and to the connection sheet's own status chip, but never to these three switches; nothing checked that the two lists agree.

**What was done.** Added a `credential` case to all three functions (amber, an asterisk glyph standing for the password mask, and the sentence "Password or passphrase needed"), matching the vocabulary the connection sheet already uses. Also extended the footer's status dot so it is drawn squared-off rather than round for `credential`, which is the existing rule for "this state is waiting on the user", and expanded the comment above the switches to say that every word the C++ controller publishes needs a case here and why.

**Why it matters.** While the application is asking for an SSH password or key passphrase, the sidebar now says so instead of claiming there is no server. Colour-blind and greyscale users get the same information because the word and glyph change too.


#### QM2 — The server-connection sheet's host-key and password prompts blocked the mouse but not the keyboard, so a keyboard user could dismiss the sheet and strand the connection attempt

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ConnectSheet.qml`, `src/qml/tests/tst_uxshell.cpp`

**What was wrong.** When an SSH server presents an unknown host key, or asks for a password, the connection attempt is suspended and a panel is raised over the connection sheet asking the user to decide. That panel covers the sheet and contains a transparent click-catcher, so no mouse click can reach the sheet underneath. Keyboard navigation was not blocked at all: every button behind the panel (Close, Cancel, Connect, Save, Remove, the error banner's Dismiss/Details, and the saved-server list) was still enabled and therefore still reachable with the Tab key and activatable with Space or Enter. Pressing Space on the hidden Close button dismissed the whole sheet while the C++ controller was still holding the half-finished connection open, waiting for an answer that no longer had any user interface to come from: the attempt then sat in its "connecting" guard forever, refusing new connection attempts, with nothing on screen to resolve it.

**Why it was wrong.** The two prompt panels achieved modality purely visually (an overlay rectangle plus a MouseArea that swallows button presses). Nothing disabled the items underneath, and in Qt Quick an item that is merely covered is still focusable and still activatable from the keyboard.

**What was done.** Added a read-only `promptActive` property on the sheet's root that is true whenever either prompt object is set (using the same truthiness test the panels' own `visible` bindings use, so the two can never disagree), and bound `enabled: !root.promptActive` on the sheet's three top-level chrome items: the header (which holds Close and the status chip), the body (the saved-server list and the whole edit form), and the error banner. Disabling an item in Qt Quick removes it from keyboard navigation as well as from mouse handling, and the sheet's shared button style already renders a disabled button in a dimmed colour, so the blocked state is also visible. The prompt panels themselves are siblings of these items, so their own answer buttons stay live. Added a new test, tst_uxshell::sheetChromeIsDisabledWhileAPromptIsUp, that raises each prompt in turn and asserts Close, Connect and the profile list all go disabled while the prompt's own Reject button stays enabled, and that everything comes back once the prompt is cleared.

**Why it matters.** A host-key or password prompt is now genuinely modal. A keyboard-only user can no longer abandon a suspended SSH handshake into a state where the application refuses further connection attempts and offers no way to recover short of a restart.


#### QM3 — A binary file's Download button could permanently stop working because the pane minted the download's identifier twice

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml`

**What was wrong.** A pane showing a file that cannot be previewed offers a single Download button. Clicking it asks the C++ viewer layer for an opaque internal URL for the file and navigates a hidden web view at it, which makes the browser engine raise a download request. A handler then checks that the request belongs to this pane before accepting it — but it performed that check by asking the C++ layer for the file's internal URL a SECOND time. The identifier map behind that call keeps only a bounded number of entries and evicts the least recently used one, and re-minting an evicted entry produces a DIFFERENT identifier. If the file's entry was evicted between the click and the callback (which needs only enough other files to have been opened in the meantime), the two strings no longer matched, the download was silently refused, and because the pane's "a download is pending" flag is only cleared on acceptance, the Download button stayed disabled for the rest of that pane's life. The second call also pushed the map's usage order around for no reason, making it more likely to evict some other pane's file.

**Why it was wrong.** The comparison recomputed a value that must be captured once. `ViewerModel::internalUrlFor()` is a minting call with a bounded cache, not a pure function of its argument.

**What was done.** Added a `pendingDownloadUrl` string property. The click handler now mints the internal URL once, stores it, and navigates the hidden view at the stored value; the download handler compares the incoming request against that stored string and clears both it and the pending flag on acceptance. A long comment records why the URL must not be minted a second time.

**Why it matters.** Downloading an unpreviewable remote file no longer depends on how many other files have been opened since, and the pane's only action can no longer wedge itself permanently.


#### QM4 — Remote directory listings were drawn in near-black text on the dark pane background, making them effectively invisible

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml`

**What was wrong.** The pane that lists a remote directory rendered each entry with a stock list-row control and set only its text, never its colours. The Qt "Basic" control style — which this application explicitly selects — draws a list row's label in the system palette's default text colour, and the application installs no dark palette, so that colour is black. The pane's own background is a very dark blue-grey (#1e1e2e). The result is black text on a nearly black background: the directory listing was there, hit-testable and selectable, but essentially unreadable. Every other row-like element in this application (sidebar session rows, the server list in the connection sheet, command-palette rows) states its own colours explicitly for exactly this reason; this one file did not.

**Why it was wrong.** The delegate relied on the control style's default `contentItem`, whose colour comes from the platform palette, in a view whose background is hard-coded dark.

**What was done.** Gave the delegate an explicit content label in the same colours the rest of the application uses (light foreground #cdd6f4, and blue #89b4fa for directory entries so the kind is not carried by the trailing slash alone), a fixed row height, and a hover background matching the sidebar rows. The label is also marked as plain text, because a file name comes from the remote server and must never be interpreted as markup. The delegate keeps its `text` property, so existing tests that read rendered text still see the same strings.

**Why it matters.** Remote directory contents are now legible, and directories are distinguishable from files by colour as well as by the "/" suffix.


#### QM5 — Every pre-filled dialog text field left the cursor after the default text, so typing produced "New sessionapi" instead of "api"

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml`, `src/qml/SessionRow.qml`

**What was wrong.** Three dialogs pre-fill a text field when they open: "New group" (default text "New group"), "New Dev Session" (default "New session") and "Rename session" (the session's current name). All three set the field's text from code when the dialog opens, which leaves the text cursor at the END of the text and nothing selected. A user who opened the New Dev Session dialog and typed "api" therefore created a session called "New sessionapi", and renaming a session appended to the old name instead of replacing it. On top of that, the New group and Rename dialogs never moved keyboard focus into their field at all, so the first keystrokes went nowhere until the user clicked the field.

**Why it was wrong.** Assigning to a text field's `text` from code moves the cursor to the end and clears any selection; the code assigned the default value without then selecting it, and only one of the three dialogs called forceActiveFocus().

**What was done.** All three dialogs now, on open, set the default text, move keyboard focus into the field, and select all of it, so the first keystroke replaces the default. Also removed a redundant second reset of the group-name field in its accept handler (the open handler already does it), gave the group dialog and its field object names to match the sibling dialogs' convention, and explained the cursor behaviour in the comments.

**Why it matters.** Creating a group, creating a Dev Session and renaming a session now behave the way every other application's pre-filled dialog does: type and the default is replaced. This was reachable on the primary path into the product's core object.


#### QM6 — The remote text and directory viewers had no loading or empty state, and the text viewer rendered failures as if they were file content

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml`

**What was wrong.** Both read-only remote viewers ask the server for content and then show whatever arrives. Neither had any notion of "the request is still in flight": while a read was outstanding the pane was an empty box, which is exactly what it also looks like for an empty file, an empty directory, and a request that quietly went nowhere. In addition, the text viewer rendered failure messages INSIDE its read-only text area, in the same monospace face as file content, as the string "Error: <message>" — indistinguishable from a file whose first line happens to read "Error: ...", and selectable/copyable as though it were part of the file. The directory viewer, by contrast, already drew its failures as a separate red centred message, so the two files disagreed with each other as well.

**Why it was wrong.** Neither view tracked request state, and the text view's single text binding conflated two different things (file bytes and a diagnostic) into one string.

**What was done.** Added a `loading` flag to both views, set when a request is issued for a non-empty URL and cleared by both the success and the failure handler. The text view's text area now shows ONLY file content, and both views gained one centred status label that reports, in priority order, the failure message (red), "Loading…"/"Listing…" while a request is in flight, or "This file is empty."/"This directory is empty." when the answer really was nothing. Both status labels are marked plain text because the failure string comes from the server.

**Why it matters.** A slow link, an empty file and a failed read are now three distinguishable states instead of one blank rectangle, and a diagnostic can no longer be mistaken for the contents of the file being viewed.


#### QM7 — The sidebar group header sized itself from a list view it is not a delegate of

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/GroupHeader.qml`

**What was wrong.** The group header row computed its own width as "the width of the list view I am a delegate of, otherwise my natural width". It is not a list-view delegate: the sidebar builds a column per group and puts the header inside that column, so the list-view reference is always empty and the fallback (natural width) is what the expression actually returns. Today this is invisible because the sidebar explicitly assigns the header's width where it creates it, which overrides the expression entirely. If that assignment were ever dropped or refactored, the header would silently shrink to the width of its text. The sibling file SessionRow.qml has a comment spelling out exactly this situation and uses the correct expression, so the two files disagreed about their own structure.

**Why it was wrong.** Left over from an earlier version in which the sidebar was a flat list view whose delegates were the headers themselves.

**What was done.** Changed the header to use the same expression and the same explanatory comment as SessionRow.qml: the parent's width when there is a parent, otherwise the natural width.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change today; the header now degrades correctly instead of collapsing if its width is ever no longer assigned from outside, and the two sidebar row types now describe their situation identically.


#### QM8 — Sidebar rows, group headers and command-palette rows exposed no name to assistive technology

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/SessionRow.qml`, `src/qml/GroupHeader.qml`, `src/qml/CommandPalette.qml`

**What was wrong.** A Dev Session row and a group header are built from a stock list-row control, but they never use that control's own `text` property — the display name arrives in a separate custom property fed from the sidebar data model. Assistive technology derives a row's spoken name from `text`, so both announced as unnamed items, and the row's state (which is conveyed on screen by a coloured dot with a glyph and a distinct silhouette) had no textual equivalent at all in the accessibility tree. The command palette's result rows are plain rectangles, which carry no accessibility information whatsoever — and that list is the application's primary keyboard surface.

**Why it was wrong.** The delegates render through custom content items and custom properties, which bypasses the automatic name the stock controls would otherwise provide.

**What was done.** Set the standard accessibility attached properties: session rows announce their name with the state (and repository subtitle, when present) as the description; group headers announce their name with "Collapsed group"/"Expanded group"; command-palette rows announce the command title with its keyboard shortcut as the description and report themselves as selected when they are the row Enter would run. All three declare the list-item role. I verified against this Qt installation's type metadata that every attached property and enum value used here exists.

**Why it matters.** A screen-reader user can now tell which session a row is, what state it is in, which group is collapsed, and which command the palette will run.


#### QM9 — The connection sheet's own documentation comment described a state vocabulary and a data shape it no longer had

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ConnectSheet.qml`

**What was wrong.** The connection sheet is deliberately self-contained: everything arrives as a property and everything leaves as a signal, and a long header comment documents that contract for whoever hosts it. Three parts of that documentation were wrong. (1) The list of recognised connection-state words omitted the words the C++ controller actually publishes today and listed only older lower-level ones. (2) The description of the pending-credential object omitted its `kind` field, even though the sheet reads that field to decide whether it is asking for a password or a private-key passphrase. (3) A comment further down asserted the C++ controller publishes "six states" and enumerated six, when it publishes seven. Separately, the paragraph explaining that a typed secret is handed up and wiped in the same turn had drifted onto the wrong function — it sat above the small helper that merely reports which KIND of secret is wanted, rather than above the function that actually reads and clears the secret.

**Why it was wrong.** The `credential` state and the `kind` field were added to the code without updating the header comment, and an inserted helper function pushed a comment away from the function it describes.

**What was done.** Rewrote the state list to name all seven words the controller publishes plus the older lower-level ones it still tolerates, documented `kind` on the pending-credential shape, corrected "six states" to seven with the full enumeration, and moved the secret-handling paragraph back onto the function it describes while giving the helper its own accurate comment.

**Why it matters.** The file's contract documentation now matches the code, so the next person wiring this sheet to a host is not misled about which states to publish or which fields to supply.


#### QM10 — A viewer pane's comment claimed its first content report reaches the host, when the region deliberately subscribes too late to hear it

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerRegion.qml`

**What was wrong.** A viewer pane announces what file it has open so the host can record it and reopen the same files next time. The pane's comment stated that it also announces this when it is given its identifier, specifically so the very first announcement (made before the pane has a name) is not lost. In fact the region that creates the pane connects to that announcement only AFTER assigning the identifier, so the first announcement has no listener and is discarded. I checked whether to change the order instead, and it must NOT be changed: that first announcement is pure echo (the URL came out of the stored layout in the first place), and delivering it would misfire during the window between switching Dev Session and its layout arriving — a pane created for the temporary placeholder layout would report into a layout store that has nothing loaded, which answers with an error, and the host would paint "layout not loaded" at a user who merely clicked another session.

**Why it was wrong.** The comment described the intended mechanism; the ordering in the region implements a deliberately different (and correct) one, and neither file said so.

**What was done.** Left the behaviour exactly as it is and corrected both comments: the pane now says the birth announcement is echo that nobody is subscribed to, and the region's creation code now spells out that the subscription is made after naming the pane on purpose, including the spurious-error scenario that ordering avoids. I confirmed the error path by reading ch::SessionLayouts::setPaneUrl, which emits an error when the region's layout is not loaded.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change. A future reader is no longer invited to "fix" the ordering into a user-visible spurious error on every Dev Session switch.


#### QM11 — The test that claims to cover "every connection state the application publishes" was missing one, and nothing tested the sidebar footer or prompt modality

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/tests/tst_uxshell.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The shell usability test drives the connection sheet through each connection-state word and asserts the colour, the busy spinner and that the state is also spelled out in words. Its data table carries the comment "the exact strings the controller emits" but listed only six of the seven, omitting `credential` — the same omission that caused the sidebar bug in QM1. Nothing at all tested the sidebar's status footer, and nothing tested that the sheet is unusable while a host-key or password prompt is waiting.

**Why it was wrong.** The table is maintained by hand and drifted from the C++ controller; the footer and the prompt modality were added without accompanying assertions.

**What was done.** Added the `credential` row to the existing table (amber, spinner running), and added two new tests. sheetChromeIsDisabledWhileAPromptIsUp raises each prompt and asserts the sheet's Close/Connect/profile list go disabled while the prompt's own buttons stay live, and that everything is restored afterwards. sidebarFooterNamesEveryConnectionState is data-driven over all seven published words and asserts the footer produces a non-empty wording that is NOT the same wording it uses when there is no server at all — which is precisely the failure mode of an unhandled state falling through to the catch-all branch.

**Why it matters.** The next state word added to the C++ controller and forgotten in the user interface now fails a test instead of quietly reading as "not connected", and the keyboard modality of the two prompts is now defended.


#### QM12 — Nothing tested that a viewer region tells the host what its panes have open

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/tests/tst_paneidentity.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Reopening a saved workspace is supposed to restore the FILES the user had open, not just the pane geometry. That works only because a viewer region emits a report naming the pane and the URL it opened, which the host writes into the saved layout. The pane-identity test file thoroughly covers pane survival across splits and which pane has focus, but nothing anywhere exercised the content report. A broken wire there is invisible in normal use: the pane still shows the file, and only the NEXT open of that workspace comes up with blank panes.

**Why it was wrong.** The content report was added alongside the focus report but only the focus report got tests.

**What was done.** Added tst_paneidentity::openingAFileInAPaneIsReportedToTheHost: it opens a region on a single empty pane, attaches a signal spy to the region's report signal, then republishes the same pane now carrying a file URL (exactly what "open this file in that pane" publishes) and asserts a report arrived naming that pane and that URL. Added the QSignalSpy include at the top of the file with the others.

**Why it matters.** The mechanism behind "my session reopens with my files" is now defended by a test that fails if the report is disconnected, renamed, or given the wrong arguments.


#### QM13 — The main window called its own functions without qualifying them, against the file's own convention

- **Kind:** Lint
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/Main.qml`

**What was wrong.** Main.qml consistently prefixes references to its own properties and functions with the window's identifier — except in six places, where four calls to the toast-message helper and one recursive call to the split-tree walker were unqualified. Unqualified access resolves at run time through the scope chain rather than being resolved statically, which the QML linter flags and which is fragile if the same name ever appears in an enclosing scope.

**Why it was wrong.** Inconsistent editing over time; the same file uses both spellings.

**What was done.** Qualified all six call sites with the window identifier. I confirmed with the Qt QML linter that Main.qml's remaining unqualified-access reports are now exclusively the injected C++ context objects (`app`, `agentMonitor`), which are inherently unqualified in this architecture and cannot be changed from QML.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change; the file is internally consistent and its self-references are statically resolvable.


#### QM14 — Remote file names in the unpreviewable-file pane were drawn as rich text

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml`

**What was wrong.** The pane shown for a file that cannot be previewed prints the file's base name as a heading and its full remote path underneath. Both labels used the default text format, which auto-detects markup: a string that merely looks like HTML is promoted to styled text, and styled text honours an image tag by FETCHING its source URL and a link tag by making the text clickable. These two strings are file names chosen on the remote server. A file named with an image tag pointing at an external address would therefore cause the client to issue that request. Every other place in this codebase that displays server-supplied text (terminal pane chrome, the connection sheet's error banner and host-key panel, the pane identifier label) already pins itself to plain text for exactly this reason.

**Why it was wrong.** Two labels added without the project's established plain-text rule for server-supplied strings.

**What was done.** Set both labels to plain text with a comment naming the rule and the reason, matching the wording used elsewhere in the codebase. The new directory-entry label added under QM4 is pinned the same way.

**Why it matters.** A hostile or careless remote file name can no longer turn this pane into an outbound network request or a live link.


#### QM15 — There is no shared colour palette; roughly two hundred hard-coded colour literals are spread across eighteen QML files

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/qml/Main.qml`, `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml`, `src/qml/SessionRow.qml`, `src/qml/GroupHeader.qml`, `src/qml/ConnectSheet.qml`, `src/qml/CommandPalette.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerRegion.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerPane.qml`, `src/qml/TerminalRegion.qml`, `src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerBinaryView.qml`

**What was wrong.** The user interface uses one coherent colour scheme (the Catppuccin Mocha set: #1e1e2e surfaces, #181825 chrome, #11111b wells, #cdd6f4 text, #6c7086 dimmed text, #89b4fa accent, #f38ba8/#f9e2af/#a6e3a1 for error/warning/success), but there is no single definition of it anywhere. Every file repeats the literals, and the same semantic colour appears with slightly different companions in different files. The same is true of the recurring type sizes (10/11/12/13/14 pixels) and corner radii (3/4/6). A single theme change today means editing every file, and a mismatch is invisible until someone looks at two surfaces side by side. Note for anyone going by file names: tst_palette.cpp is about the COMMAND palette (the searchable command list), not about colours — there is no colour palette in this project.

**Why it was wrong.** The user interface grew file by file with no theme singleton, and nothing forces a new file to source its colours from a shared place.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED, deliberately. Introducing a theme singleton means adding a new QML file to the module definition and rewriting colour references in thirteen files at once. That is a large, unreviewable diff in a tree that thirteen other agents are editing concurrently, and I am not permitted to run a build or the QML-load test to prove the new module registration and every rewritten reference are correct. It should be done as one focused change with the test suite available. The colours I added in this pass reuse the existing literals exactly, so no new value was introduced.

**Why it matters.** Theme changes stay expensive and drift between surfaces stays possible. No user-visible defect today.


#### QM16 — The editor pane uses two injected C++ objects without the guard its sibling terminal pane applies

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/qml/EditorPaneView.qml`

**What was wrong.** The terminal pane checks whether the injected terminal-service object exists before using it, so a bare load of that component (a test, or a tool inspecting the user interface) yields inert chrome instead of a run-time reference error; the comment explains this is also the seam a test injects a stub through. The editor pane creates its controller straight from the injected editor-factory object with no such check, and both panes use the injected viewer object unguarded to obtain their browser profile. So the guard convention exists but is applied to exactly one of three injections.

**Why it was wrong.** The guard was added to the terminal pane when a test needed it, and never generalised.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. Guarding only the editor factory would be cosmetic: the pane still cannot function without the viewer object, which is unguarded in this file and in five others, so a bare load would fail a moment later anyway. A real fix is a decision about whether these panes must survive with no injected services at all, which affects several files, and it cannot be validated without running the QML-load test. Recorded rather than half-done.

**Why it matters.** No defect on any current path — every host and every test that loads the editor pane installs both objects. A future bare load of this component would fail with a reference error rather than degrade to inert chrome.


#### QM17 — The same remote-path helper function is written out three times

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/qml/ViewerTextView.qml`, `src/qml/ViewerDirectoryView.qml`, `src/qml/EditorPaneView.qml`

**What was wrong.** Converting a file URL into the plain server-side path the remote service expects (strip the "file://" prefix, then percent-decode) is implemented three separate times: identically in the text viewer and the directory viewer, and once more in the editor pane using a regular expression instead of a prefix test. Three copies of one rule about a security-relevant conversion is three places for it to drift.

**Why it was wrong.** Each view was written independently and the helper is three lines, so it was copied rather than shared.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. Sharing it requires a new shared JavaScript or singleton file registered in the QML module definition — the same build-visible change as QM15, which I cannot validate here. The two prefix-based copies are byte-for-byte identical, and I verified all three produce the same result for the inputs this application generates.

**Why it matters.** A future change to remote-path handling must be made in three places or the views will disagree.


#### QM18 — The four modal dialogs are drawn in the control style's default light theme inside an otherwise dark application

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml`, `src/qml/SessionRow.qml`, `src/qml/ConnectSheet.qml`

**What was wrong.** Four dialogs (New group, New Dev Session, Rename session, SSH connection details) use the stock dialog and text-field controls with no styling, so they inherit the control style's default palette. Because the application installs no dark palette, these appear as light panels with dark text and dark-on-light buttons, dropped into an otherwise uniformly dark window. They are readable — unlike the directory listing in QM4, which was dark-on-dark — but they plainly do not belong to the same product.

**Why it was wrong.** The dialogs predate the hand-styled surfaces around them and were never restyled; nothing in the project defines a styled dialog to reuse.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. Doing it properly means one reusable styled dialog component that all four adopt, which is new-file territory (see QM15) and touches three files' visual output; doing it improperly means a fourth hand-rolled set of colour literals. Recorded for the theming work instead of guessed at.

**Why it matters.** Cosmetic inconsistency on four secondary surfaces. No functional or legibility defect.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Method: read all eighteen QML files end to end, then all six QML test files, then cross-checked EVERY property, signal and invokable the QML references against the C++ headers it binds to. I extracted the complete set of references to injected objects (32 top-level plus 15 nested plus the per-pane terminal/editor objects) and verified each one exists with the exact name and arity in AppController.h, SessionsModel.h, UiStateStore.h, SessionLayouts.h, ServerProfiles.h, ViewerModel.h, AgentStatusMonitor.h, TerminalFactory.h, TerminalBridge.h, EditorFactory.h and EditorController.h. No mismatch was found — that class of silent failure is genuinely absent from this slice. I also verified the newly used accessibility attached properties and role enum against this Qt 6.10.2 installation's own type metadata, and confirmed the Qt Basic style's list-row default text colour by reading the installed style source (this is what establishes finding QM4 as a real defect rather than a guess).

I ran no build and no test command, per the constraints. I did run the Qt QML linter read-only over the eighteen files as a syntax check: no errors anywhere, and the only remaining unqualified-access reports are the injected C++ context objects (unavoidable in this architecture) and pre-existing identifier references from inside nested delegate scopes in five files, which I left alone because changing them means enabling bound component behaviour on files whose delegate patterns rely on dynamic scoping.

One fix I made and then deliberately reverted, because it is the kind of change that looks correct and is not: I first reordered the viewer region so a pane's very first content report reaches the host (matching what the pane's comment claimed). Reading ch::SessionLayouts::setPaneUrl showed that would emit "layout not loaded" whenever a pane is created for the temporary placeholder layout — i.e. an error toast on every Dev Session switch. I restored the original order and documented why it must stay that way (finding QM10).

Two behaviour changes are worth the lead's attention when the suite is run. First, disabling the connection sheet's chrome while a prompt is up (QM2) also changes the appearance of the existing screenshot fixture "connect-hostkey": the sheet behind the panel is now visibly dimmed. That is intended and, I would argue, more honest. Second, the sidebar's new "credential" wording and the extra data row in the connection-state test (QM1, QM11) are the pair that must agree; if the C++ controller's state words ever change, both fail together, which is the point.

### 4.13 Browser bundles loaded into the embedded browser (`WB`)

*Scope:* `src/web/terminal/` (the xterm.js terminal page) and `src/web/editor/` (the Monaco editor page), including their build scripts.

*16 findings:* 6 active bug, 2 latent bug, 2 nit, 1 inconsistency, 1 simplification, 1 footgun, 1 lint, 1 documentation error, 1 missing test.

#### WB1 — Closing or reloading the terminal web page silently loses terminal output

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The terminal is an HTML page (xterm.js) running inside the desktop app; the C++ side streams shell output to it over a bridge. When the page went away (the pane was closed, or the app reloaded the page), the JavaScript tore down its own observers and destroyed the terminal widget, but never told the C++ side that the renderer was gone. The C++ side therefore kept believing a live renderer was listening and kept pushing output at a page with no handler attached. That output was not buffered anywhere, so it was permanently lost: after a reload the user saw a gap in their shell output.

**Why it was wrong.** mountTerminal()'s returned dispose() disconnected the ResizeObserver and IntersectionObserver and called term.dispose(), but did not call bridge.notifyViewVisible(false). On the C++ side (src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp, setViewVisible) 'view not visible' is exactly the switch that makes the controller retain output in its rolling buffer and replay it as one batch when a renderer next announces itself. Without that call the controller stayed in 'visible' mode and emitted its write() signal into nothing.

**What was done.** dispose() now reports the renderer hidden before tearing anything down: if the last reported visibility was not already false it sets it to false and calls bridge.notifyViewVisible(false), then disposes. The page-unload hook in connectTerminal() (the 'pagehide' listener) therefore hands buffering responsibility back to C++ on its way out. Comment updated to say so.

**Why it matters.** Output produced between the moment a terminal page starts going away and the moment the replacement page finishes loading is now retained by the C++ controller and replayed into the new page instead of being dropped.


#### WB2 — Terminal grid stayed one row too small after the connection became ready

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`, `src/web/terminal/index.html`

**What was wrong.** The terminal page shows a one-line status strip above the terminal itself, and that strip is hidden by CSS once the connection reaches the 'ready' state. When the strip disappeared, the terminal area grew by one line's height, but the terminal was never re-measured: it kept using the old, smaller row count and kept telling the remote machine that the window was that smaller size. The bottom line of the terminal was effectively wasted and the remote program's idea of the window size was wrong until something else resized the pane.

**Why it was wrong.** The ResizeObserver that triggers a re-fit was attached to the outer container element only. Hiding the status strip does not change the outer container's size (it is sized by the host window), only the inner terminal surface's size, so the observer never fired. src/web/terminal/index.html contains the rule '.ch-terminal-status[data-state="ready"] { display: none; }' that causes this.

**What was done.** The same ResizeObserver now observes both boxes: the outer container (host resized the pane) and the inner terminal surface (the strip appeared or disappeared above it). Re-fitting twice for one real resize is free because the fit addon only emits a resize event when the computed columns/rows actually change. The comment explains both targets.

**Why it matters.** The terminal now re-measures and re-reports its true size the moment the status strip is hidden or shown, so the remote program always sees the real window size.


#### WB3 — A terminal page hidden by the window manager was still reported as visible

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The design says a hidden terminal view may be suspended while its output keeps being collected on the C++ side. The page decided 'am I visible?' purely from an IntersectionObserver, which only answers 'is this element inside the scrollable viewport'. When the embedded browser view stops being drawn (its window is minimised, or the pane is not the active one in a stacked layout), the element still sits inside the viewport, so the page kept reporting 'visible' and the C++ side kept streaming into a renderer nobody could see. Separately, every observer callback re-sent the same value even when nothing had changed.

**Why it was wrong.** Visibility was derived from one signal (element intersection) that cannot observe page-level visibility. The browser's own page visibility state (document.visibilityState plus the 'visibilitychange' event), which Qt WebEngine drives from whether the view is being rendered, was not consulted. There was also no memory of the last value sent, so identical updates were re-sent.

**What was done.** Visibility is now the AND of two sources: the IntersectionObserver result and document.visibilityState === 'visible'. A small reportVisibility() helper computes the combined value, drops it if it equals the last value already reported, and only then calls bridge.notifyViewVisible(). The 'visibilitychange' listener is added on mount and removed in dispose() so no listener leaks. Note that the QML host independently reports pane visibility through the same slot (src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml, onVisibleChanged); the two now agree instead of contradicting each other, and the C++ setter ignores a repeated value anyway.

**Why it matters.** A terminal whose window is minimised or whose view stops being rendered is now correctly suspended, and its output is buffered and replayed when it comes back, as the specification's section 5.4 requires. Redundant bridge calls are gone.


#### WB4 — Editor: a delayed crash-recovery snapshot fired after a successful save and left the file looking unsaved

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** While the user types, the editor page waits 500 milliseconds and then sends the buffer to the C++ host, which writes it to a server-side 'unsaved work' snapshot and marks the file as having unsaved changes. If the user pressed Ctrl+S within that 500 ms window, the save went out first and succeeded (the host then deletes the snapshot), and the timer fired afterwards anyway — re-creating the snapshot for a file that was already saved and re-marking it as dirty. Two visible consequences: reopening that file later offers to restore 'unsaved changes' that do not exist, and because the host now thinks the buffer is dirty it stops automatically reloading the file when it changes on the server.

**Why it was wrong.** The debounce timer and the save path were unrelated pieces of code: the Ctrl+S handler, the 'Overwrite' button and the 'Retry' button all called bridge.save() directly and never cancelled the pending timer. On the C++ side (src/editor/EditorController.cpp, reportContent) each report unconditionally sets the dirty flag and rewrites the snapshot, and a successful save had already run its snapshot cleanup, so the late report won.

**What was done.** Introduced three small helpers next to the timer — cancelReport(), scheduleReport(), flushReport() — and a requestSave(revision) used by all three save paths. requestSave() cancels the pending snapshot before sending the save, which makes cancelling part of saving rather than an afterthought. Because a failed save leaves the edits genuinely unsaved, the save-conflict and save-error handlers re-arm the snapshot with scheduleReport(). scheduleReport() additionally refuses to arm when the buffer is not dirty, so a Ctrl+S on an unmodified buffer can never mark the file dirty on the host. A newly loaded buffer also cancels any pending snapshot, since those edits have been superseded.

**Why it matters.** After a successful save the host no longer holds a stale recovery snapshot, the file is no longer wrongly flagged as having unsaved changes, and automatic reload of a clean buffer on an external change works again.


#### WB5 — Editor: the last edits before closing a pane never reached crash recovery, and the editor was never released

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The editor page had no teardown path at all. If the user typed and then closed the pane (or the host pointed the pane at a different file, which reloads the page) within half a second of the last keystroke, the pending crash-recovery snapshot was thrown away and those edits existed nowhere but in a page that was being destroyed. The Monaco editor instance and its text model were also never disposed.

**Why it was wrong.** mountEditor() returned nothing, so the page bootstrap had no handle to tear anything down, and nothing registered a page-unload hook (the terminal page does). The only cleanup was editor.onDidDispose(), which cleared the timer — and its comment claimed it flushed the pending snapshot, which it never did (see WB14).

**What was done.** mountEditor() now returns an EditorHost object with a dispose() method, mirroring TerminalHost in the terminal page. dispose() first calls flushReport(), which sends a still-pending snapshot immediately, then calls editor.dispose() (which also disposes the text model Monaco created for it). connectEditor() registers a one-shot 'pagehide' listener that calls host.dispose(), exactly as the terminal page does.

**Why it matters.** Edits made in the last half-second before a pane is closed or retargeted are now snapshotted for crash recovery, and the editor and its model are released deterministically instead of relying on page destruction.


#### WB6 — Both pages could end up as a blank rectangle with no explanation

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`, `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** Two failure modes produced a completely blank pane with no message. First, the editor page cast the bridge object out of the channel without checking it existed; if the host had not registered it, the very next line threw a TypeError and nothing was ever drawn. Second, both pages referenced the two globals the host injects (the QWebChannel constructor and the qt transport object) without checking they exist; a page opened outside the host, or one whose injected qwebchannel.js script failed to load, threw a ReferenceError at startup and rendered nothing. The surrounding QML can only report a page that failed to LOAD, so neither case produced any user-visible message.

**Why it was wrong.** Missing existence checks, plus type declarations that asserted the injected globals are always present ('declare const qt: {...}'), which hid the possibility from the type checker.

**What was done.** Both globals are now declared as possibly undefined and guarded with 'typeof' (the only safe way to touch a global that may never have been declared). Each page gained a small showFatal(element, message) helper that replaces the pane content with one explanatory line, styled with the page's existing status-strip class (the terminal one uses the existing red 'error' styling). It is used for 'no WebChannel transport' and for 'the bridge is missing from this window'. Both write text with textContent, never innerHTML.

**Why it matters.** These failures now show a readable one-line message instead of an unexplained blank pane, and the editor page can no longer die with an uncaught TypeError.


#### WB7 — Editor status label was blank when the file finished loading before the page connected

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The editor's status strip shows the file's lifecycle state (loading, clean, modified, saving, read-only, ...). It was only ever populated from the C++ 'state changed' signal. Because the C++ controller starts loading the file before the page's channel is even open, all of those transitions can have already happened by the time the page attaches its handlers, so the strip stayed empty (and, for an already-saved file, stayed empty indefinitely) even though the content showed up correctly.

**Why it was wrong.** The page read no initial value. The C++ controller does publish the current state as a readable property ('fileState' in src/editor/EditorController.h), which the channel runtime caches when the channel opens; the page ignored it. The terminal page already solves the identical problem by reading its cached 'connectionState' property on connect.

**What was done.** Added 'readonly fileState?: string' to the additive (non-frozen) part of the EditorBridge interface, documented as the cached property, and mountEditor() now seeds the label from it just before the ready handshake. Optional, so a host that does not publish the property simply leaves the label as before.

**Why it matters.** The status strip shows the correct file state immediately on mount instead of waiting for the next state change that may never come.


#### WB8 — Editor: syntax highlighting could pick a language by extension when an exact filename rule should have won

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** languageForPath() picks a syntax-highlighting language for the opened file. It looped once over all registered languages and, for each one in turn, checked that language's exact-filename rules and then its extension rules. That means a language listed earlier could claim a file on a generic extension even though a later language claimed the exact file name — the more specific statement. Concretely, a language registering the extension '.txt' would beat a language registering the file name 'CMakeLists.txt'.

**Why it was wrong.** Specificity was decided by the order languages happen to be registered in rather than by rule type, because both checks lived in the same single pass.

**What was done.** Split into two passes: first look for an exact filename match across all languages, then (only if the file has an extension) look for an extension match, then fall back to plain text. Also documented that a leading dot is part of the name and not an extension, which is why the existing 'dot > 0' test is correct for names like '.gitconfig'.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change with the currently bundled Monaco version — I checked its registrations and only four languages declare exact filenames (Dockerfile, config/.gitattributes/.gitconfig/.editorconfig, jakefile, rakefile/Gemfile) and none of those names carry an extension, so no collision is reachable today. The fix removes the trap for the next version or the next added language.


#### WB9 — Editor disabled the minimap that the specification requires

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The product specification's editor section explicitly lists the minimap (the small overview of the whole file down the right edge) among the features the embedded editor should provide, alongside find and replace, multiple cursors, folding and bracket matching. The page created the editor with 'minimap: { enabled: false }', contradicting that.

**Why it was wrong.** An explicit option that disagreed with the specification, with no comment explaining why.

**What was done.** Changed to 'minimap: { enabled: true }' with a comment citing the specification section and noting that the other listed features are Monaco defaults.

**Why it matters.** The editor now provides the specified feature set. If the project has deliberately decided against the minimap, the specification text should change instead — I could not find any comment, issue or note stating such a decision.


#### WB10 — Editor page set ten layout styles from JavaScript, duplicating and fighting its own stylesheet

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`, `src/web/editor/index.html`

**What was wrong.** mountEditor() assigned ten inline styles (flex layout, font, padding, gap, margins) to the elements it created, while the page's own stylesheet in index.html carried other rules for the same class names. The two overlapped and in one case actively conflicted: the JavaScript set a 6-pixel flex gap on the notice bar while the stylesheet also gave every button in it a 6-pixel left margin, so the gap was doubled. The neighbouring terminal page keeps all of its layout in its stylesheet, so this was also a second way of doing the same thing.

**Why it was wrong.** Presentation was split across two files with no rule about which owns what.

**What was done.** Moved all static layout and appearance into the stylesheet in src/web/editor/index.html next to the existing rules, with a comment explaining the column layout (the editor surface needs a definite height, hence flex:1 plus min-height:0) and dropped the duplicated button margin in favour of the flex gap. The script now sets exactly one style itself: whether the notice bar is currently shown, which is genuine dynamic state (and which a live test asserts on). The script also creates its elements through element.ownerDocument, matching the terminal page, instead of the global document.

**Why it matters.** One place to change the page's appearance, no doubled gap, and the two web pages now follow the same convention. No visual behaviour is lost.


#### WB11 — Both bundles stripped the third-party MIT copyright notices they are obliged to keep

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/build.mjs`, `src/web/editor/build.mjs`

**What was wrong.** Both bundle scripts passed legalComments: "none" to the bundler, which deletes license and copyright banners from the bundled third-party code. xterm.js and monaco-editor are both MIT licensed, and the MIT license requires its copyright notice to be included with the distributed code. The shipped bundles therefore carried none of the notices for the libraries that make up almost all of their bytes.

**Why it was wrong.** The option was presumably chosen for size; neither script commented on the licensing consequence.

**What was done.** Both scripts now use legalComments: "eof", which collects the banner comments at the end of each generated file, with a comment in each script explaining that this is a licence obligation and not a style preference. Bundle size grows by a few hundred bytes.

**Why it matters.** The shipped application now carries the copyright notices of the libraries it bundles. I have not run the build, so I have not measured the exact size delta; it is bounded by the size of the retained banner comments.


#### WB12 — Web bundle TypeScript settings were looser than the rest of the repository

- **Kind:** Lint
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/tsconfig.json`, `src/web/editor/tsconfig.json`

**What was wrong.** Both web bundles' TypeScript configurations were missing two settings that the repository's other TypeScript project (the remote service) sets: 'noEmit' and 'verbatimModuleSyntax'. Without 'noEmit', anyone who runs the compiler without the exact flags in the package script silently writes generated .js files next to the sources. Without 'verbatimModuleSyntax' the compiler tolerates import statements that would be rewritten rather than left alone, which is a mismatch with how the bundler actually treats them.

**Why it was wrong.** The two configurations were written independently of the remote service's configuration and drifted from it.

**What was done.** Added "noEmit": true and "verbatimModuleSyntax": true to both, matching remote/tsconfig.json. Both sources already comply: every import in them is a value import or a bare side-effect import, so no source change was needed.

**Why it matters.** Compiler settings are now consistent across the repository's three TypeScript projects and a stray compiler invocation cannot litter the source tree. I did not run the type checker (build and test commands were prohibited for this pass), so this is reasoned from reading the sources rather than observed.


#### WB13 — Both pages' Content-Security-Policy left two directives undefined

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/index.html`, `src/web/editor/index.html`

**What was wrong.** Both pages ship a deliberately tight Content-Security-Policy built on 'default-src none'. Two directives are not covered by that fallback and were therefore unset: 'base-uri' (which controls whether a <base> element may re-point every relative URL on the page, including the editor page's worker script) and 'form-action' (which controls where a form may submit, i.e. navigate this privileged page to).

**Why it was wrong.** A known gap in how the default-src fallback works; both directives have to be stated explicitly.

**What was done.** Added "base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'" to both policies and explained in each page's security comment why they are listed separately. The editor page's worker URL is resolved from document.baseURI, which falls back to the document URL when no <base> element exists, so denying <base> does not affect it.

**Why it matters.** Two small additional restrictions on a page that already loads nothing from the network. No functional change.


#### WB14 — Comment claimed the editor flushed a pending snapshot and released Monaco; it did neither

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The comment above the editor's only cleanup hook read 'Flush any pending snapshot and release Monaco when the surface goes away'. The code below it merely cancelled the timer, which throws the pending snapshot away rather than flushing it, and nothing in the file ever released the editor. A reader trusting the comment would have concluded the teardown path in WB5 already existed.

**Why it was wrong.** The comment described intended behaviour that was never implemented.

**What was done.** The behaviour now exists in the returned host's dispose() (WB5), and the hook's own comment was rewritten to say what it actually does: if some other code disposes the editor directly, do not leave a timer that would read the value of a dead editor.

**Why it matters.** Comments now match the code, and the behaviour the old comment promised is genuinely implemented elsewhere.


#### WB15 — Neither web bundle has any automated test

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`, `src/web/editor/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** There is no test of any kind for these two pages. The only coverage they get is indirect, from C++ tests that drive the real editor page through a live SSH connection and are skipped unless a live-test environment variable is set. Two pieces of pure logic in my slice deserve direct unit tests: languageForPath() (the filename-beats-extension precedence fixed in WB8 has no test to defend it) and the interaction between saving and the delayed crash-recovery snapshot (the bug fixed in WB4 would be caught by a test using a fake bridge object and a fake timer).

**Why it was wrong.** The two packages were set up with build and type-check scripts only; the repository has no test runner wired up for browser-side TypeScript, and the C++ test directory covers the C++ halves.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED, and deliberately so: adding a runnable test requires adding a test script to src/web/*/package.json, and every package.json in this repository is owned by a different agent in this review pass, so I am not permitted to edit one. Writing a test file that nothing can execute would be dead weight. Recommendation, routed as out-of-scope: add "test": "node --test" to both packages and a test file per package using node:test with node:assert/strict (the convention the remote service already uses), covering at minimum languageForPath precedence and 'a save cancels the pending snapshot, a failed save re-arms it'.

**Why it matters.** Two recently fixed defects have no regression guard. Everything else in this report is defended only by reading.


#### WB16 — Terminal enables the xterm.js proposed-API flag without using any proposed API

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Uncertain
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/src/index.ts`

**What was wrong.** The terminal is created with 'allowProposedApi: true'. That flag exists to let a page opt in to unstable xterm.js APIs. This page uses only the stable surface (write, clear, resize events, data events, the fit addon and a link handler), so the flag appears to serve no purpose and widens what future code may reach for without noticing.

**Why it was wrong.** Most likely copied from an example configuration.

**What was done.** NOT FIXED. Removing it would be a behaviour change I cannot verify without running the app: xterm.js raises an error only at the moment a proposed API is touched, so if any bundled addon or internal path touches one, dropping the flag turns a working terminal into a throwing one. Since build and test commands were prohibited for this pass and a wrong 'fix' here is worse than a surviving nit, I left it alone. I am not certain either way; verifying is a matter of removing the flag and launching one terminal pane.

**Why it matters.** None today. Purely a tidiness and future-proofing observation.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope covered: both web page sources, both page shells (index.html), both bundler scripts, both TypeScript configurations and both ambient module declaration files, read in full; plus the two C++ halves of the bridges (src/terminal/TerminalBridge.h/.cpp, src/editor/EditorController.h and the relevant parts of its .cpp), the two QML host panes, the state-string table in src/models/SessionState.cpp, and specification sections 5 and 8. I wrote only to files listed in my Target section.

Bridge contracts verified name by name and found consistent on both sides: the terminal page uses channel object name 'terminal' with signals write/connectionStateChanged/clearRequested, slots sendInput/resize/notifyViewVisible/ready and the cached property connectionState — all present in TerminalBridge with matching argument types. The editor page uses channel object name 'editor' with signals contentLoaded/fileStateChanged/readOnlyChanged/saved/saveConflict/saveError and slots save/reportContent/requestReload/ready — all present in EditorController with matching types; I additionally started consuming its existing readable 'fileState' property (finding WB7). Object names match what the QML hosts register, and both hosts register before the first navigation. The lifecycle state strings the terminal stylesheet keys on (ready, error, disconnected, reconnecting) all exist in the C++ state-to-string table.

Checked and found genuinely fine, no change made: no innerHTML or other markup-from-string path anywhere in either page (all text goes through textContent, so the remote-content cross-site-scripting surface is closed); no 'any', no type-assertion escape hatches and no compiler-suppression comments in either source; the terminal's deliberate no-op link handler that neutralises clickable escape-sequence hyperlinks from remote output; the stateful UTF-8 decoding of terminal bytes, which is done on the C++ side so a multi-byte character split across two batches cannot become a replacement character; bounded terminal scrollback (5000 lines); the initial-size handshake ordering (fit, then attach the resize listener, then send the size exactly once); the editor's worker URL resolution from the page's base URL and the fact that a worker construction failure degrades rather than breaks; the editor's undo-history preservation when a reload brings back identical content; per-file page reload by the QML host, which means there is no model-switching leak inside a page; and the absence of source maps in the shipped bundles. Content hashing of the output files is not needed here because the bundles are embedded in the binary's resource system and loaded from a resource URL, not fetched over HTTP with caches in between.

Honesty note on verification: as instructed I ran no build, no type check and no test suite. The only command I ran against my edits was a syntax-only parse of both TypeScript sources (node --check, which strips types and checks syntax); both parse cleanly. That validates syntax, not types, and nothing at all about runtime behaviour. Everything else in this report is reasoned from reading the code on both sides of each bridge. The changes most worth a second pair of eyes at build time are the two TypeScript configuration additions (WB12) and the possibly-undefined redeclaration of the two injected globals (WB6), since those are the only edits that could plausibly produce a type error.

### 4.14 Build system, continuous integration, packaging, scripts and documentation (`BD`)

*Scope:* Root `CMakeLists.txt`, `CMakePresets.json`, `.github/workflows/*`, `packaging/*`, the npm manifests, the shell scripts under `tests/`, the release-version script, and `README.md` + `docs/*.md`.

*28 findings:* 10 documentation error, 6 footgun, 4 latent bug, 2 inconsistency, 2 active bug, 1 lint, 1 simplification, 1 missing test, 1 nit.

#### BD1 — Stray generated Qt meta-object file left in the repository root

- **Kind:** Lint
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `moc_SessionsModel.cpp`

**What was wrong.** An untracked file named `moc_SessionsModel.cpp` sat in the top level of the repository. Files whose names start with `moc_` are generated by Qt's meta-object compiler and belong exclusively in the build output directory.

**Why it was wrong.** Someone ran Qt's meta-object compiler by hand from the repository root instead of letting the build system generate it under `build/`. `.gitignore` already lists `moc_*.cpp`, so git never reported it and it simply accumulated.

**What was done.** Deleted the file. No build rule references it (the build regenerates its own copy inside `build/<preset>/`), and `.gitignore` already prevents it from ever being committed.

**Why it matters.** Cosmetic, but it is exactly the kind of stale generated artifact that later confuses a reader into thinking it is a source file, or gets accidentally committed.


#### BD2 — Developer documentation stated the wrong number of remote tests

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`

**What was wrong.** `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` told the reader that `cd remote && npm test` runs "99 tests". The suite actually runs 106.

**Why it was wrong.** The count was written down once and never updated as tests were added; nothing checks a hand-written number against reality.

**What was done.** Changed the annotation to `node --test -> 106 tests`.

**Why it matters.** A reader who counted 106 could reasonably suspect they had a broken or unexpected checkout. Small, but the whole value of that page is that its numbers can be trusted.


**Follow-up.** Re-pinning the number was the wrong fix. Within this same review the count moved again (106, then 130, then 136) because later work added tests, so the line was stale a third time before the work was finished. A figure written by hand that no test checks cannot stay true. The count has therefore been removed from `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` entirely, with a comment saying why, rather than corrected for a fourth time.

#### BD3 — Build required the Qt SQL module even though nothing in the project uses it

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `CMakeLists.txt`, `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`

**What was wrong.** The top-level `CMakeLists.txt` demanded the `Sql` component of Qt 6 (`find_package(Qt6 6.9 REQUIRED COMPONENTS ... Sql ...)`). Nothing in the codebase links `Qt6::Sql` or uses any Qt SQL class: a repository-wide search for `QSqlDatabase`, `QSqlQuery` and `<QtSql` returns zero hits, and `src/persistence/CMakeLists.txt` carries an explicit comment saying there is deliberately no Qt SQL dependency because all storage happens on the remote server over remote procedure calls.

**Why it was wrong.** Left over from an early design in which the desktop client opened the workspace database directly. When that was moved server-side, the component requirement (and the matching row in the setup documentation and the `libqt6sql6-sqlite` package in the install command) was never removed.

**What was done.** Removed `Sql` from the component list and added a comment stating why it is absent. Also removed the corresponding table row and the `libqt6sql6-sqlite` package from the Ubuntu install command in `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`, and replaced the note with an explicit statement that no Qt SQL driver is needed, so the documented package set still maps one-to-one to the components actually required.

**Why it matters.** On a machine with a Qt installation that omits the SQL module, configuration failed with a confusing missing-component error for a module the program never touches. It also made everyone install a database driver package for nothing.


#### BD4 — Qt Test module was required even when tests were switched off

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `CMakeLists.txt`

**What was wrong.** `find_package(Qt6 ... REQUIRED COMPONENTS ... Test)` ran unconditionally, but every test executable in the tree is created inside an `if(CODEHARBOR_BUILD_TESTS)` block. The `release` build preset sets that option to OFF, so a release configuration insisted on a Qt module it would then never use.

**Why it was wrong.** The component list was written as one flat list before the tests-off release preset existed, and was never split.

**What was done.** Split the call: the always-needed components stay in the first `find_package`, and `Test` is requested in a second `find_package(Qt6 6.9 REQUIRED COMPONENTS Test)` guarded by `if(CODEHARBOR_BUILD_TESTS)`. The option is declared above the calls, so the guard evaluates correctly. Both branches are exercised by continuous integration, which configures the `dev` preset (tests on) and the `release` preset (tests off) in the same job.

**Why it matters.** Latent: it only bites on a Qt installation without the Test module — common for slimmed-down runtime/SDK images used to produce release builds. There, packaging a release would have failed at configure time for no real reason.


#### BD5 — Declared minimum CMake version could not actually run the project's own presets

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `CMakeLists.txt`, `CMakePresets.json`, `README.md`, `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`

**What was wrong.** The project declared `cmake_minimum_required(VERSION 3.24)`, `CMakePresets.json` declared `cmakeMinimumRequired` 3.24.0, and both `README.md` and `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` advertised "CMake 3.24+". But `CMakePresets.json` uses preset schema `"version": 6`, which was introduced in CMake 3.25. CMake 3.24 rejects a version-6 presets file outright with an unrecognised-version error.

**Why it was wrong.** The presets file was upgraded to schema version 6 at some point without revisiting the version floor declared in the build files and documentation.

**What was done.** Raised the floor to 3.25 in `cmake_minimum_required`, in the presets file's own `cmakeMinimumRequired`, and in both documents (the setup table now names the reason: "3.25 (CMakePresets schema v6)"). A comment in `CMakeLists.txt` records why the number is 3.25 so it is not silently lowered again.

**Why it matters.** Anyone taking the documentation literally and installing CMake 3.24 could not run a single documented build command — every documented invocation goes through `cmake --preset ...`, which is precisely what 3.24 cannot read. The stated minimum was unusable.


#### BD6 — The two web-bundle package manifests were stuck at version 0.1.0, and the release script never touched them

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/web/terminal/package.json`, `src/web/editor/package.json`, `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh`, `.omp/skills/bump-version/SKILL.md`

**What was wrong.** The project version is 0.1.7 (top-level `CMakeLists.txt`, root `package.json`, `remote/package.json`, and the newest git tag `v0.1.7` all agree). The two browser-bundle manifests, `src/web/terminal/package.json` and `src/web/editor/package.json`, still said 0.1.0. The version-bump helper script only rewrote `CMakeLists.txt`, `package.json` and `remote/package.json`, so those two files could never catch up.

**Why it was wrong.** The bump script hard-coded a three-file list written before the two browser bundles became real npm workspaces. Nothing cross-checks the manifests against each other, so the drift was invisible.

**What was done.** Set both manifests to 0.1.7. Replaced the hard-coded list in `bump.sh` with a single `VERSION_FILES` array containing all five files, and used that same array both for the "do not clobber uncommitted edits" guard and for the rewrite loop, so the two can no longer disagree. Updated `SKILL.md` to describe the real file set. Verified the script still parses with `bash -n` (the script itself was NOT executed — the release lead runs it).

**Why it matters.** Every release shipped two build artifacts stamped with a version seven patch releases old. Anyone debugging which bundle a client was running got a misleading answer.


#### BD7 — Release script aborted with no message when `--set` was given without a value

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh`

**What was wrong.** The argument loop handled `--set` as `shift; SET_VERSION="${1:-}"`. If `--set` was the final argument, that `shift` consumed the last argument, leaving zero arguments; the loop's own trailing `shift` then failed. Because the script runs under `set -e`, the whole script exited immediately with a non-zero status and printed nothing at all.

**Why it was wrong.** A `shift` inside the loop body plus a `shift` at the end of the loop, with no check that an argument is actually present to consume.

**What was done.** Rewrote the `--set` branch to shift, then verify an argument remains (`[ $# -gt 0 ] || die "--set requires a version (X.Y.Z)"`) before assigning. There is an explanatory comment so the guard is not removed as redundant. Verified with `bash -n`.

**Why it matters.** A person mistyping the release command got a silent exit with no diagnosis, in a script whose whole job is to produce a release tag. The subsequent validation that would have caught an empty version never ran.


#### BD8 — Release script silently skipped any version file that was missing

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh`

**What was wrong.** The loop that rewrote each `package.json` began with `[ -f "$f" ] || continue`, so if a manifest were renamed or moved, the script would quietly stop bumping it and still report success.

**Why it was wrong.** Defensive-looking `continue` used where the condition actually indicates a mistake.

**What was done.** Changed it to `[ -f "$f" ] || die "expected version file is missing: $f"`, with a comment explaining that a silently un-bumped version is the exact failure this list exists to prevent. All five listed files exist today, so behaviour is unchanged in practice.
