**Why it matters.** Prevents a repeat of the drift described in BD6: a moved manifest can no longer fall out of the release process without anyone noticing.


#### BD9 — Documented client build steps built only one of the two required web bundles, and described a failure mode that no longer exists

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`

**What was wrong.** The "Build & run" section told the reader to run `npm run build --workspace src/web/editor` and then configure. It described only the Monaco editor bundle. In reality `src/qml/CMakeLists.txt` embeds two bundles — the Monaco editor page and the xterm.js terminal page — and raises a hard configure error (`FATAL_ERROR`) if either is missing and cannot be built.

**Why it was wrong.** The terminal bundle was added later, with the same embed-and-refuse-to-configure treatment as the editor, but this page was only ever updated for the editor.

**What was done.** Changed the command block to plain `npm run build` (the root workspace script that builds both bundles) and rewrote the surrounding prose to name both bundles, both embedded resource addresses, and both source directories that re-trigger the rebuild, and to state that configuration fails if either bundle is unavailable.

**Why it matters.** Following the documentation exactly still worked by luck (the build system builds the missing bundle itself when npm is available), but the description of what happens when it cannot was wrong, and a reader looking for the terminal bundle found no mention of it.


#### BD10 — Both continuous-integration workflows carried comments claiming a missing web bundle only produces a warning

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/release.yml`

**What was wrong.** A comment in `ci.yml` read "It must exist before `cmake --preset dev` or CMake only warns and produces a client whose editor pane has nothing to load - a silently broken editor, not a build failure", and `release.yml` had a similar comment. That is no longer true: `src/qml/CMakeLists.txt` now issues `FATAL_ERROR` when a bundle is missing and cannot be built, unless `-DCODEHARBOR_SKIP_WEB_BUNDLE=ON` is passed.

**Why it was wrong.** The build system was changed from warn-and-continue to hard failure; the comments that motivated the workflow step were not updated.

**What was done.** Rewrote both comments to state the current behaviour accurately: the bundles are embedded at configure time, configuration fails outright when they are absent, and the escape hatch is `-DCODEHARBOR_SKIP_WEB_BUNDLE=ON`. Both files still parse as valid YAML (verified).

**Why it matters.** A maintainer trusting the comment would think deleting the bundle-build step merely degrades the artifact, when it actually breaks the build. That misreading is how the step gets removed.


#### BD11 — Release workflow installed CMake from a floating, always-moving action reference

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/release.yml`

**What was wrong.** The release build used `uses: lukka/get-cmake@latest`. Unlike every other action in the two workflows (all pinned to a major version such as `@v4`), `latest` is a tag the action author moves with each new CMake release, so the CMake version that produces published binaries changes without any commit to this repository.

**Why it was wrong.** `@latest` is the usage the action's own documentation suggests, and it was copied verbatim.

**What was done.** Pinned to `lukka/get-cmake@v4.2.3` — that tag exists (confirmed on the action's tag list) and delivers CMake 4.2.3, the exact version `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` records as verified for this tree. Added a comment stating that the pin should be bumped deliberately, together with that table.

**Why it matters.** Previously a release could start failing, or silently start being built by a different toolchain, with no change to this repository — the hardest class of build failure to diagnose. Now the toolchain is part of the reviewed history.


#### BD12 — Windows dependency cache used a hard-coded path while the install step used an environment variable

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/release.yml`

**What was wrong.** The cache step saved and restored `C:\vcpkg\installed`, but the following step that installs the SSH library derived the same location from the runner's `VCPKG_INSTALLATION_ROOT` environment variable. The two agree on today's Windows runner image and nowhere guarantees they will keep agreeing.

**Why it was wrong.** The literal path was easier to write in a step field, because a runner-image environment variable is not reachable from a workflow expression context (the file already documents this limitation for a different variable).

**What was done.** Used the same trick the file already uses for the image version: the existing "Resolve cache key" step now also writes `vcpkg=$env:VCPKG_INSTALLATION_ROOT` to its step outputs, and the cache step's path is `${{ steps.imgver.outputs.vcpkg }}\installed`. One source of truth, matching the pattern already established in the file. The path resolves to the same location today, so behaviour is unchanged.

**Why it matters.** If a future runner image moves the package manager, the cache would silently store an empty directory: every release build would pay the full nine-to-ten minute from-source rebuild of the SSH library while appearing to use a cache.


#### BD13 — Neither workflow restricted the default permissions granted to its jobs

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/release.yml`

**What was wrong.** Neither workflow declared a top-level `permissions` block, so every job received whatever the repository default is — potentially write access to repository contents. Only the release workflow's `publish` job actually needs write access, and it already requests `contents: write` for itself.

**Why it was wrong.** Omission; the job-level grant on `publish` was added without also tightening the default for everything else.

**What was done.** Added `permissions: contents: read` at the top of both workflows, with a comment noting that `publish` grants itself write access explicitly. The job-level grant on `publish` overrides the top-level default, so publishing is unaffected.

**Why it matters.** Standard hardening: the three cross-platform builders, the remote packager, and both continuous-integration jobs execute build scripts and third-party actions and now cannot modify the repository even if one of them is compromised.


#### BD14 — The two optional-feature descriptions in the in-tree package definition were swapped

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `packaging/vcpkg-ports/libssh/vcpkg.json`

**What was wrong.** In the in-tree package overlay that pins the SSH library to version 0.12.2, the feature named `pcap` was described as "SSH server support" and the feature named `server` as "Pcap generation support" — each carrying the other's description.

**Why it was wrong.** A copy-and-paste transposition when the overlay was authored.

**What was done.** Swapped the two description strings so each names its own feature. The file still parses as valid JSON (verified). Nothing selects features by description, so no build behaviour changes.

**Why it matters.** Purely descriptive, but these strings are what the package manager prints to a person choosing features, so it actively misinforms.


#### BD15 — The anti-scaffolding gate could be tripped by third-party code, and its comment claimed exclusions it did not have

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `tests/no_scaffolding.sh`

**What was wrong.** `tests/no_scaffolding.sh` fails the test suite if markers such as `MUTATION-TEST` are found in the tree. It searched `src` and `remote` recursively with `grep -R`, restricted only by file extension, and its comment claimed "Build output, node_modules and this script itself are excluded" — but there was no exclusion of any kind for dependency directories or generated bundles.

**Why it was wrong.** The comment described intent rather than the command. It happened to hold only because the package manager currently hoists all dependencies to a single top-level `node_modules` directory outside the searched paths — a layout decision that can change at any time (a version conflict makes the package manager install into `src/web/<bundle>/node_modules` instead).

**What was done.** Added explicit `--exclude-dir=node_modules --exclude-dir=dist`, switched `-R` (which follows symbolic links, and dependency trees are full of them) to `-r`, and rewrote the comment to state the real reasoning. Verified two ways: run against the real tree it still reports no markers; run against a temporary tree containing a marker in a source file and another inside a `node_modules` directory, it fails with exit status 1 and reports only the source file. The temporary tree was deleted.

**Why it matters.** Latent, but the failure mode is nasty: a marker string inside some dependency's TypeScript file would fail the whole test suite with a message accusing the developer of leaving debug scaffolding in code they have never seen.


#### BD16 — The repository-layout map in the README omitted two of the client's modules

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `README.md`

**What was wrong.** The `Repository layout` diagram listed nine entries under `src/` but the tree has eleven directories; `src/editor/` (the remote-file editing controller) and `src/agent/` (the coding-agent status monitor) were missing. The `docs/` line listed only `SPEC.md` and `PLAN.md`, omitting `DEVELOPMENT.md`, which that very README links to twice.

**Why it was wrong.** The map was written when those modules did not exist and was not revisited when they landed.

**What was done.** Added a one-line description for `editor/` and `agent/` in the right positions and added `DEVELOPMENT.md` to the `docs/` line.

**Why it matters.** The first orientation a new contributor gets was silently incomplete, hiding two whole subsystems.


#### BD17 — README claimed there is no way to create a group; the user interface has had one for some time

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `README.md`

**What was wrong.** The README's honest-gaps paragraph said "no UI for creating, renaming or deleting a group". `src/qml/SessionsSidebar.qml` contains a "+ New group" button and a dialog that calls the application controller's `createGroup` method (lines around 512-532 and 809-837), and `docs/PLAN.md` explicitly states that creating groups is wired. Renaming and deleting genuinely have no reachable control — a repository-wide search finds `renameGroup` only in the controller, tests and documentation, never in any user-interface file, and `deleteGroup` is not even exposed to the interface layer.

**Why it was wrong.** The gap list was written before the group-creation control was added and only partially updated afterwards; the plan document was corrected and the README was not.

**What was done.** Rewrote the sentence to "no UI for renaming or deleting a group (creating one is wired)", which now matches both the code and `docs/PLAN.md`.

**Why it matters.** The project's front page understated its own capabilities and contradicted its own plan document — the sort of inconsistency that makes a reader distrust every other claim on the page.


#### BD18 — Plan document cited a stale line number for a known test limitation

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `docs/PLAN.md`

**What was wrong.** The known-gaps list said two live tests hard-code a development checkout layout, citing `tst_livessh:183` and `tst_liveagent:355`. Line 183 of `src/ssh/tests/tst_livessh.cpp` is an unrelated `device.closeChannel();`; the line that actually builds a path from the checkout root is 194. The other citation, line 355 of `src/agent/tests/tst_liveagent.cpp`, is correct.

**Why it was wrong.** Code was inserted above the cited line after the note was written; a line number in prose has nothing keeping it honest.

**What was done.** Corrected the reference to `tst_livessh:194`, verified by reading that line (it composes the remote command from `<checkout>/remote/src/codeharbord.ts`).

**Why it matters.** Anyone following the note to fix the limitation landed on the wrong statement and could reasonably conclude the note was obsolete.


#### BD19 — Section titled "Current state" reported a test count from the project's first week

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `docs/PLAN.md`

**What was wrong.** Under the heading `Current state (bootstrap — DONE, verified)`, a bullet read "**Verified:** `npm test` → 11/11 pass". The remote suite now runs 106 tests. The heading says both "Current state" and "bootstrap", so the figure reads as a present-tense claim.

**Why it was wrong.** A historical record placed under a present-tense heading and never marked as historical.

**What was done.** Relabelled the bullet "**Verified at bootstrap:**" and noted that the suite has grown well beyond that, pointing at `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` for the current count (which BD2 corrected to 106). The historical number is preserved rather than rewritten, since it documents what was true at that milestone.

**Why it matters.** A reader comparing 11 against 106 has no way to tell whether the plan is stale or their checkout is wrong.


#### BD20 — Release table omitted a directory that the remote artifact actually contains

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`

**What was wrong.** The release-pipeline table described the remote service artifact as "`codeharbor-remote.tar.gz` (`dist/` + `package.json`)". The workflow step that builds it runs `tar -czf ../artifact/codeharbor-remote.tar.gz dist package.json sql`, so the archive also contains the `sql/` directory holding the database schema. The README's install instructions already say the archive gives you `dist/`, `sql/` and `package.json`, so the two documents disagreed.

**Why it was wrong.** The `sql/` directory was added to the archive without updating this table.

**What was done.** Changed the cell to "(`dist/` + `sql/` + `package.json`)", matching the workflow and the README.

**Why it matters.** Someone auditing what ships to the remote server would conclude the database schema is absent — and the schema is exactly the file you go looking for when a fresh install misbehaves.


#### BD21 — Release drill implied the bump script pushes the tag, which it does not

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`

**What was wrong.** Step 2 of the release drill read: "**Then push the tag.** `bash .omp/skills/bump-version/bump.sh --set X.Y.Z`." That command does not push anything. It rewrites version files, commits them and creates an annotated tag, then prints the push command; pushing is what actually starts the release build.

**Why it was wrong.** An instruction summarised by its intent rather than its effect.

**What was done.** Expanded step 2 to state exactly what the script does and does not do, and to name both ways to complete it (`--push`, or running the `git push origin HEAD vX.Y.Z` the script prints), ending with "Only the push starts the release build."

**Why it matters.** A maintainer following the drill would run the script, see a success message, and believe the release was underway. Nothing would build, and the discrepancy is invisible until someone notices the missing release.


#### BD22 — Editor configuration prescribed an indentation width the project does not use for build files, and omitted one file type

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.editorconfig`

**What was wrong.** `.editorconfig` declared `indent_size = 2` for `CMakeLists.txt` and `*.cmake`. Every build file in the tree is indented with four spaces (the top-level `CMakeLists.txt`, each per-module one, and the package overlay script). Separately, the two-space rule listed `ts,js,json,qml,yml,yaml` but not `mjs`, even though the bundle build scripts `src/web/*/build.mjs` are two-space JavaScript.

**Why it was wrong.** The build-file rule encoded a preference that was never followed, and the extension list predates the `.mjs` scripts.

**What was done.** Deleted the two build-file overrides, so those files inherit the file's default of four spaces — which is what they actually contain — and added `mjs` to the two-space extension list. No source file was reformatted.

**Why it matters.** An editor honouring the configuration would silently re-indent every build file it touched from four spaces to two, producing enormous unrelated diffs, and would indent the bundle build scripts at four spaces against their existing style.


#### BD23 — Redundant ignore rule

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `.gitignore`

**What was wrong.** `.gitignore` contained both `/dist/` (top level only) and, further down, `dist/` (any directory). The second fully subsumes the first.

**Why it was wrong.** The general rule was added later, for the JavaScript workspace outputs, without removing the narrow one.

**What was done.** Removed the redundant `/dist/` line. The remaining `dist/` rule, whose comment already explains it covers `remote/dist` and `src/web/*/dist`, still ignores a top-level `dist/` as well.

**Why it matters.** None functionally; two rules for one thing invite the next reader to assume they differ and to "fix" the wrong one.


#### BD24 — Continuous integration never type-checks the two browser bundles

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

**What was wrong.** The `client` job builds the browser bundles with `npm install && npm run build`. That build uses esbuild, which strips types without checking them, so a TypeScript type error anywhere in `src/web/terminal` or `src/web/editor` reaches a released bundle unreported. Both packages already declare a `typecheck` script (`tsc --noEmit`), and the root package declares a `typecheck` script that runs it across all workspaces. The remote service job does type-check. Nothing type-checks the browser code.

**Why it was wrong.** The bundling step was added to make configuration succeed, not as a quality gate, and no one revisited whether the browser code is checked anywhere.

**What was done.** Not applied. The fix is a single step in the `client` job — `run: npm run typecheck` after the bundle build. I could not apply it safely: verifying that it passes today requires running the TypeScript compiler, which this review round explicitly forbids, and a second agent is concurrently editing the very TypeScript configuration files (`src/web/*/tsconfig.json`) it would use. Adding a gate I cannot verify risks turning the main branch red for a reason unrelated to this change. Recommended sequence for the lead: run `npm run typecheck` at the repository root once the tree is settled, then add the step.

**Why it matters.** A type error in the terminal or editor page is invisible to every automated check and only surfaces as a broken pane at runtime — precisely the class of silent breakage the rest of this project's build wiring works hard to prevent.


#### BD25 — Linux packaging downloads its deployment tools from a floating "continuous" build

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/release.yml`

**What was wrong.** The Linux packaging step downloads `linuxdeploy` and its Qt plugin from the upstream projects' `continuous` release, which is overwritten on every upstream commit, with no version pin and no checksum verification.

**Why it was wrong.** Upstream publishes these tools only as a rolling `continuous` release; there are no versioned releases to pin to.

**What was done.** Not applied. Pinning would require pointing at a specific release asset that upstream does not publish. The alternatives are to vendor a copy of the tool (a multi-megabyte binary in the repository) or to record and verify a checksum, which would need updating by hand every time upstream changes anything and would fail the release rather than warn. Both trade one problem for a worse one, so this is recorded rather than changed. Note that this is the same class of risk as BD11, which was fixable because that action does publish version tags.

**Why it matters.** An upstream regression, or a compromise of that download, changes or poisons the Linux release artifact with no change to this repository. The blast radius is limited to the packaging step (the binary itself is already built at that point), but it is real.


#### BD26 — The release workflow publishes binaries that no test has ever run against

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `.github/workflows/release.yml`

**What was wrong.** The release workflow configures and builds with the `release` preset, which sets the build-tests option to OFF, and never invokes the test runner. The three platform binaries and the remote-service archive are published on the strength of compiling. The continuous-integration workflow does run the full test suite, but only for pushes to the main branch and for pull requests — a version tag can be pushed at any commit, including one that never passed.

**Why it was wrong.** The release workflow was written to optimise for build time, on the assumption that tagging always follows a green main branch. The documented release drill reinforces that assumption but does not enforce it.

**What was done.** Not applied, because every available fix is a substantial change to release behaviour rather than a bug fix: running the tests requires a second tests-enabled configuration and build on each of three operating systems (roughly doubling a run that already takes about fourteen minutes), and the honest alternative — refusing to publish unless the tagged commit has a green continuous-integration run — needs a design decision about which check to require. Recommended as a follow-up: gate the `publish` job on the tagged commit's continuous-integration status.

**Why it matters.** A tag pushed from an untested commit produces a public release with no test evidence behind it. Given that this project's own history (recorded in `docs/PLAN.md`) is a series of green suites coexisting with broken products, publishing with no suite at all is worth naming.


#### BD27 — The dependency lock file records stale workspace versions, and continuous integration does not install from it strictly

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `package.json`

**What was wrong.** `package-lock.json` records version 0.1.0 for the top-level package and for the workspace entries, while the manifests now say 0.1.7 (this predates my change: the top-level manifest was already at 0.1.7 with the lock file at 0.1.0). Both workflows use `npm install`, which quietly reconciles the difference and may also resolve dependencies differently from the recorded tree, rather than `npm ci`, which installs exactly what is locked.

**Why it was wrong.** The version-bump script rewrites the manifests but never refreshes the lock file, and nothing installs strictly enough for the drift to be noticed.

**What was done.** Not applied. I am explicitly not permitted to hand-edit `package-lock.json`, and doing so by hand is error-prone in exactly the way that makes lock files worth having. Switching the workflows to `npm ci` would be the real hardening, but it must not be done while the lock file is stale, or every job fails. Recommended sequence for the lead: run `npm install` at the repository root once to refresh the lock file, commit it alongside the version bump, and only then consider `npm ci` in the workflows.

**Why it matters.** Builds are not fully reproducible from the committed lock file, and the recorded versions are misleading. No active breakage: `npm install` tolerates it, which is why it has gone unnoticed since version 0.1.1.


#### BD28 — Conditional zlib dependency in the generated package configuration can never be true

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Uncertain
- **Files:** `packaging/vcpkg-ports/libssh/portfile.cmake`

**What was wrong.** The package overlay writes a CMake configuration file containing `if("${WITH_ZLIB}")`. That expansion happens while the port is being built, not when the generated file is later read, and `WITH_ZLIB` is not a variable at that point (the feature check only populates a separate options list). The written file therefore always contains `if("")`, which is false, so the compression library dependency would never be resolved for a consumer even if the feature were enabled.

**Why it was wrong.** The overlay deliberately mirrors the upstream registry package byte-for-byte except for the version and archive checksum — and this quirk is inherited from upstream.

**What was done.** Not applied, and I am flagging it as uncertain rather than fixing it. Two reasons. First, it has no effect today: the compression feature is not among the package's default features, so nothing in this project requests it. Second, the overlay's stated purpose (documented in its own header comment) is to differ from upstream in exactly two fields, so that the built tree keeps upstream's layout, features and CMake configuration; "improving" a line upstream also has would make the overlay harder to re-derive when the pin is next bumped. Recommended if it ever matters: report it upstream rather than diverging here.

**Why it matters.** None in this project's configuration. It would matter only if someone enabled the compression feature and then relied on the generated configuration to pull in the compression library, at which point their build would fail at link time with a confusing missing-symbol error.


*Reviewer's closing note for this area:* Scope: I read every file in my slice end to end and verified claims against the tree rather than trusting prose. Twenty-three findings fixed, four explained-not-fixed with the reasoning and a recommended follow-up, one flagged uncertain and deliberately left alone.

Things I checked that turned out to be CORRECT, recorded so nobody re-checks them:
- Version consistency, after BD6: top-level CMakeLists.txt, all four package.json files, and the newest git tag all read 0.1.7. The desktop entry carries no version (correct — it should not). The release workflow derives the release name from the tag at run time and hard-codes no version. The only version literal in the workflow is the pinned SSH library version, which it reads out of the package overlay file rather than duplicating.
- Specification section numbering: sections 1 to 19 with no duplicates and no gaps, and every numbered subsection sequential. I extracted every `SPEC <n>[.<m>]` citation from the entire tree (source, remote service, documents, workflows, scripts) — 49 distinct citations — and every single one resolves to a real heading. No citation points at the wrong section. Two sections (13 and 16) use unnumbered subheadings while the rest number theirs; nothing cites those subheadings, so I left the style alone rather than renumber a document other people cite by number.
- Every relative link in README.md, docs/DEVELOPMENT.md and docs/PLAN.md resolves to an existing path (checked individually).
- The absence of a `release` test preset in CMakePresets.json is correct, not an omission: the release configuration sets the build-tests option to OFF, so a test preset for it would be meaningless. I searched the whole tree for anything invoking the test runner with that preset — nothing does. Every documented and scripted test invocation uses the `dev` preset.
- The documented live-test environment contract is accurate: all eight documented variables exist in the code, and the code introduces no undocumented ones (the two extra matches are internal string prefixes, not environment variables).
- The documented claim that exactly one target carries the `desktop` label is true.
- The plan document's honest-gaps list is accurate where I could verify it: the group rename and delete operations really have no user-interface control, and the crash-recovery signal really has no consumer. Only its group-creation claim was stale, in the README rather than the plan (BD17).
- Release artifact paths line up with the README's asset table on all three platforms, and both upload steps set `if-no-files-found: error`, so a missing artifact fails rather than publishing an empty release.
- Shell scripts: all three in my slice already set the strict-mode flags they should (`set -euo pipefail` in the bump script, `set -eu` in the two POSIX scripts), quote their variables, use no unsafe file deletion, and use `mktemp` safely. All three pass a syntax check. The apparent absence of `set -e` in workflow steps is not a defect: GitHub runs each shell step with error-exit and pipeline-failure already enabled.

Verification performed, in full: no build, test, or continuous-integration command was run, per instruction. What I did run was (a) syntax-only checks — `bash -n` on the bump script, `sh -n` on both POSIX scripts, a JSON parse of all six JSON files I touched, and a YAML parse of both workflows; and (b) the anti-scaffolding script itself, which is a three-line grep, both against the real tree (passes) and against a throwaway temporary tree containing a deliberate marker plus a marker inside a dependency directory, to prove the gate still fails on real source and now correctly ignores dependencies. That temporary tree was deleted. One transparency note: the YAML parse used the system `python3` purely as a parser; this repository contains no Python and no virtual environment exists, and the operation was read-only with no side effects.

Two changes I want the lead to be aware of because they alter build inputs rather than text: removing the Qt SQL component (BD3) and making the Qt Test component conditional (BD4). Both are backed by exhaustive searches showing nothing links those modules outside the guarded test targets, and both are exercised by the existing continuous-integration job, which configures the tests-on and tests-off presets in the same run. Raising the CMake floor to 3.25 (BD5) cannot break a working setup, since a CMake older than 3.25 could never read this project's presets file in the first place.

---

## 5. Pass two — cross-area items, routed and fixed

Each of these was found by a reviewer who was not allowed to edit the affected file, then
handed to whoever owns that file.

### Desktop client — application layer — 4 item(s)

#### X1 — Blind trust-on-first-use of an unknown SSH host key in SessionBootstrap

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/SessionBootstrap.h`, `src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp`, `src/app/tests/tst_sessionbootstrap.cpp`, `src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp`, `src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp`

**What was wrong.** ch::SessionBootstrap::attemptWire() (in src/app/SessionBootstrap.cpp) checked whether the SSH connection pool already had a host-key decision callback installed and, if not, installed one that returned Accept for any key. Any code path that reached connectAndWire() without a real callback would therefore silently trust whatever host key the server presented, write it into the known_hosts file and connect, with nobody asked. docs/SPEC.md 12.1 requires that an unknown host key be shown to the user for approval.

**Why it was wrong.** attemptWire() treated 'no decision policy installed' as 'accept everything', because the headless CH_LIVE_* harness path and several tests have no user interface to raise a prompt in and would otherwise be unable to connect. The convenience default was applied to every caller instead of only to those callers.

**What was done.** Added an explicit opt-in to SessionBootstrap: setTrustUnknownHostKeys(bool) / trustUnknownHostKeys(), backed by the new member m_trustUnknownHostKeys which defaults to false. attemptWire() now installs the accept-everything callback only when that flag is set; with the flag off and no callback installed it emits error() with a message naming the host and port and returns false BEFORE any handshake, so nothing is connected to and nothing is written to known_hosts. connectAndWireFromEnvironment() sets the flag for itself, but only after confirming CH_LIVE_SSH is set, so the ordinary desktop launch never turns it on. Call paths audited: (a) AppController::startConnect always calls installPoolCallbacks() first, so the prompting callback is present and production asks the user; (b) main.cpp calls connectAndWireFromEnvironment(), which returns false immediately with no CH_LIVE_SSH, and never turns the flag on in that case; (c) the unit harness in src/app/tests/tst_sessionbootstrap.cpp and the direct connectAndWire() call in src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp now opt in explicitly; (d) the live tests in src/app/tests/tst_livereconnect.cpp, src/app/tests/tst_sessionlayouts.cpp and src/editor/tests/tst_liveeditor*.cpp go through connectAndWireFromEnvironment() and keep working untouched. New test tst_sessionbootstrap::unknownHostKeyIsNeverTrustedWithoutADecisionPolicy() pins the behaviour: with the opt-in off and no callback on the pool the wire attempt fails, the fake connectPool() seam is never called (0 calls), no callback is left installed on the pool, the state is Failed and exactly one error mentioning 'host key' is emitted; installing any decision callback then lets the same wire succeed. Also refreshed the now-stale diagnostic string in tst_coldstart step 3, which described the old overwrite bug.

**Why it matters.** A release build with the normal object graph can no longer trust an unknown host key without the user approving it; the worst case for a mis-wired caller is a clean, explained connect failure instead of a silent trust decision.


#### X2 — Displayed host-key fingerprint lacked the SHA256: prefix that ssh-keygen prints

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/AppController.cpp`, `src/app/AppController.h`, `src/app/tests/tst_appcontroller.cpp`, `src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp`

**What was wrong.** AppController showed the fingerprint to approve as bare base64 of the SHA-256 of the key bytes with '=' padding removed. OpenSSH prints the identical base64 but prefixed with 'SHA256:', which is what an administrator compares against (for example `ssh-keyscan host | ssh-keygen -lf -`). The two strings did not look alike at a glance, discouraging the comparison the dialog asks for.

**Why it was wrong.** The value was emitted straight out of the internal comparison form, which has no prefix because it is matched against a fingerprint re-derived from the key blob inside the host-key callback.

**What was done.** Added a file-local helper displayFingerprint() in src/app/AppController.cpp that prepends 'SHA256:', and applied it at the single point the value leaves for the user interface: the emit of hostKeyPrompt() in AppController::startConnect. The internal values (m_pendingFingerprint, m_approvedFingerprint, the value passed back into startConnect by resolveHostKey and submitCredential, and the value the pool callback compares) all stay unprefixed, so no comparison changes. Verified no consumer round-trips the displayed string: src/qml/Main.qml only stores it into ConnectSheet.pendingHostKey for display and answers with app.resolveHostKey(accept), which takes a bool. Documented the display form on the hostKeyPrompt signal in AppController.h. Tests updated: tst_appcontroller gained displayedHostKeyFingerprint() and asserts the prompt carries the prefixed form; tst_coldstart's cross-check of the persisted known_hosts line against the prompted string now adds the same prefix. Existing QML tests already used 'SHA256:...' mock strings, so they were already consistent.

**Why it matters.** The string the approval dialog shows can now be compared character for character with ssh-keygen output; no internal comparison or SSH pool expectation changed.


#### X3 — Live test tore the RPC transport down in the reverse of the production order

- **Kind:** Inconsistency
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The FreshView destructor in src/app/tests/tst_liveshell.cpp did client.setTransport(nullptr) and then device.closeChannel(). SessionBootstrap::unwire() deliberately does the opposite: close the channel first, then detach, because closing is what makes CodeharbordClient fail any in-flight call with a transport error, whereas detaching first used to orphan them.

**Why it was wrong.** The test's teardown was written independently of unwire() and never aligned with it.

**What was done.** Swapped the two statements in ~FreshView() so the channel is closed before the client is detached, with a comment naming SessionBootstrap::unwire() as the ordering being mirrored. (The reported location was described as a stop() helper around line 170; it is in fact the FreshView destructor at that line.)

**Why it matters.** The live test now exercises the shipped teardown sequence rather than one production avoids.


#### X4 — Two objects published into the QML root context that no QML reads

- **Kind:** Simplification
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/app/main.cpp`, `src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp`

**What was wrong.** src/app/main.cpp installed seven objects as QML root context properties. Two of them, 'layouts' (ch::SessionLayouts) and 'notifier' (ch::Notifier), were read by no QML anywhere: QML reaches the layouts through app.layouts, and the notifier is only connected in C++ to AgentStatusMonitor::notify.

**Why it was wrong.** Leftover context properties from before AppController exposed the layouts, kept alive as an apparent part of the user-interface contract.

**What was done.** Verified the claim: a search of every .qml file in the repository for a bare 'layouts' or 'notifier' identifier finds only a prose comment in src/qml/Main.qml, and the only setContextProperty calls for those two names were in src/app/main.cpp and the graph builder in src/app/tests/tst_coldstart.cpp (which mirrors main.cpp line for line). Removed both calls from main.cpp and from the tst_coldstart mirror, leaving a comment stating that QML reaches the layouts via app.layouts and that the Notifier is a C++-only sink. Both objects are still constructed and still wired in C++ (appController.setConnection() and the AgentStatusMonitor::notify connection), and both still outlive the engine, so the documented destruction order in main.cpp is unchanged.

**Why it matters.** The QML context now advertises only what QML actually binds to, so nobody is invited to bind to a second, unsupported path into the same objects.


*Closing note:* No build or test command was run, per instructions. Only files under src/app/ were modified: SessionBootstrap.h/.cpp, AppController.h/.cpp, main.cpp, tests/tst_sessionbootstrap.cpp, tests/tst_liveshell.cpp, tests/tst_appcontroller.cpp, tests/tst_coldstart.cpp. Compile-safety checks done by reading declarations and call sites: the new SessionBootstrap accessor is declared in the header and defined next to setConnectTimeoutMs; ch::KnownHosts used in the new test lambda is available transitively via SshConnectionPool.h, which includes KnownHosts.h; the tst_appcontroller helper hostKeyFingerprint() is still used (by the new displayedHostKeyFingerprint()), so no unused-function warning. Note for the lead: docs/PLAN.md lines 309-313 describe the wave-7 version of X1 ('attemptWire unconditionally installed an accept-all host-key callback'); the mechanism has now changed again (no callback is installed at all unless a caller opts in), so DocsFixups may want to refresh that paragraph. I did not touch docs/.

### Desktop client — SSH transport — 2 item(s)

#### X5 — Real libssh floor is 0.8.0 (set by ssh_get_server_publickey); the documented "0.10" floor is unevidenced, and the 0.12.0 hybrid-KEX workaround itself needs 0.11.0

- **Kind:** Documentation error
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp`

**What was wrong.** I listed every libssh function, type, enum and constant that src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp and src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp use (58 distinct names; `ssh_buffer_pack` appears only inside an explanatory comment, not as a call) and checked each against the public headers of the upstream releases. Every one of them is already declared in include/libssh/libssh.h or include/libssh/callbacks.h at tag libssh-0.7.0 EXCEPT one: ssh_get_server_publickey(), called in SshConnectionPool::verifyHostKey(). It is absent from libssh-0.7.0/libssh.h (which only has the older ssh_get_publickey()) and present in libssh-0.8.0/libssh.h, where ssh_get_publickey() is marked SSH_DEPRECATED. So the compile/link floor implied by the code is libssh 0.8.0 (released 2018-08-10), not 0.10. Two extra facts matter for the documented floor: (1) the mitigation in connectToHost() passes the string "-mlkem768x25519-sha256" to ssh_options_set(SSH_OPTIONS_KEY_EXCHANGE, ...); the leading '-' (subtract-from-default) syntax is the "+,-,^ modifiers in front of algorithm lists" feature listed under "version 0.11.0 (released 2024-07-31)" in the upstream CHANGELOG, so that mitigation only functions on libssh 0.11.0 and newer. It is not an active bug, because the mitigation is gated on ssh_version() reporting exactly 0.12.0, which is newer than 0.11.0 — an older libssh never executes that call. (2) Two upstream advisories land exactly on calls these files make: CVE-2023-6004 (command injection through a ProxyCommand built from ssh_config, fixed in 0.10.6 per the CHANGELOG; connectToHost() calls ssh_options_parse_config() on the user's ~/.ssh/config) and CVE-2025-5351 (double free in pki_key_to_blob(), which backs the public-key export functions; the advisory states it affects "libssh >= 0.10.0, built with OpenSSL >= 3.0" and is fixed in 0.11.2; verifyHostKey() calls ssh_pki_export_pubkey_base64() on every connection). I also confirmed ssh_options_get() supports SSH_OPTIONS_IDENTITY in 0.10.0's src/options.c, so identityFileCandidates() works there. Sources relied on: upstream CHANGELOG at https://gitlab.com/libssh/libssh-mirror/-/raw/master/CHANGELOG (release-dated sections for 0.8.0, 0.10.0-0.10.6, 0.11.0-0.11.4, 0.12.0); release headers include/libssh/libssh.h and callbacks.h at tags libssh-0.7.0, libssh-0.8.0, libssh-0.9.6, libssh-0.10.0, libssh-0.11.0 from the same mirror; advisories https://www.libssh.org/security/advisories/CVE-2025-5351.txt and CVE-2025-4877.txt. (CVE-2025-4877 turned out NOT to apply: it is confined to ssh_get_fingerprint_hash(), which this code never calls.) The locally installed libssh, per pkg-config --modversion, is 0.11.3.

**Why it was wrong.** The "minimum libssh 0.10" number in the docs/DEVELOPMENT.md verified-toolchain table was never derived from the API the client actually calls. No symbol used by src/ssh requires 0.10; conversely the version-specific key-exchange workaround added later requires 0.11.0 features, and the security posture of the paths these files exercise requires 0.11.2, so both the low bound and the reasoning behind it were wrong.

**What was done.** Documentation change only, for the DocsFixups agent (I did not touch docs/DEVELOPMENT.md). Recommended wording for the table: minimum libssh 0.11.2, excluding 0.12.0; hard API floor 0.8.0. Justification to record: the only symbol that rules out anything older is ssh_get_server_publickey() (0.8.0); 0.11.0 is required for the '+,-,^' algorithm-list modifier used by the 0.12.0 hybrid-KEX mitigation; 0.11.2 is the first release without CVE-2025-5351, which affects the ssh_pki_export_pubkey_base64() call made on every host-key verification (0.10.x is also end-of-life upstream and is missing the CVE-2023-6004 ProxyCommand fix before 0.10.6). In my own slice I added a comment block at the top of src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp recording this audit: the floor, the single symbol that sets it, the 0.11.0 requirement of the algorithm-list modifier, and the two CVEs that bear on these calls.

**Why it matters.** No behavioural change. Before: the build documentation promised support for a libssh version (0.10.x) that nothing verified, that upstream no longer maintains, that carries a known double-free in the key-export path this client calls on every connect, and on which the shipped 0.12.0 key-exchange workaround could not work at all. After: the floor is stated with per-symbol evidence, so packagers and CI can pick a version that is actually exercised.


#### X6 — Device-outliving-session teardown re-audited on all five paths: sound as written; added a live regression test for it

- **Kind:** Missing test
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.h`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp`, `src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.h`, `src/ssh/tests/tst_livessh.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Wave one's claim holds. Walking the current code: every libssh channel that SshConnectionPool::openChannel() hands out is recorded in m_channels, and the ONLY place ssh_free()/ssh_channel_free() runs for a tracked channel is SshConnectionPool::closeSession() and SshConnectionPool::releaseChannel(). closeSession() first moves m_session into a local and nulls the member (so a nested disconnect started from a slot returns immediately and openChannel() cannot hand out a channel on a dying session), THEN emits sessionClosing(), and only afterwards sweeps the channel list — which it re-reads after the emission, so channels that devices gave back during the emission are not touched twice. SshChannelDevice's constructor connects sessionClosing() to closeChannel(), which stops the poll timer, hands the channel back, nulls its own pointer, clears the read buffer, closes the QIODevice and emits readChannelFinished() once. Path by path: (a) normal disconnect - disconnectFromHost() -> closeSession(), covered; (b) teardown at the start of a reconnect - connectToHost() calls disconnectFromHost() before allocating a new session, same path; (c) pool destruction - ~SshConnectionPool() calls disconnectFromHost() from the destructor body, where the object is still a SshConnectionPool and its members are still alive, so devices are notified and release normally; even in the hypothetical where the device's QPointer to the pool already read null, closeChannel() still nulls the device's channel pointer and stops the timer, and the pool's own sweep frees the channel, so there is neither a leak nor a stale pointer; (d) device destruction while the session is alive - ~SshChannelDevice() stops the timer and calls releaseChannel(); (e) a PTY/exec request failing on a freshly opened channel - SshChannelDevice::abortStart() calls releaseChannel() and nulls the pointer, and openChannel()'s own ssh_channel_request_pty() failure frees the channel before it is ever appended to m_channels or returned. Double release is impossible for two independent reasons: releaseChannel() returns early unless QList::removeOne() actually removed the handle, and no code path leaves a device holding a non-null handle to an already-freed channel (the device always nulls its pointer in the same statement block in which it releases). pump() also re-checks m_channel before every libssh call and re-arms the timer before emitting, so a handler that closes the channel cancels the next pass. I changed no production code.

**Why it was wrong.** n/a - the reported hazard was genuinely fixed in wave one. What was missing is a test: nothing in the suite exercised a channel that is still live at the moment its session is torn down, so a regression in the sessionClosing() notification or in closeSession()'s ordering would have gone unnoticed until it crashed a user.

**What was done.** Added one Qt Test slot, TstLiveSsh::deviceOutlivingItsSessionStopsCleanly(), at the end of src/ssh/tests/tst_livessh.cpp (declared last because it deliberately takes the shared session down; every other slot calls ensureConnected() and the existing cleanupTestCase() disconnect becomes a no-op). It starts a remote `cat` on an Exec channel - `cat` reads the channel's own stdin, so the remote end never sends EOF and the channel is unambiguously live - then calls SshConnectionPool::disconnectFromHost() and asserts, synchronously, that the device reported readChannelFinished(), is no longer open, advertises zero readable bytes, refuses resizePty(), and that after waiting 100 ms (ten times the device's 10 ms idle poll) the device is still inert and the pool reports Disconnected. Under the pre-wave-one behaviour this test crashes or faults inside libssh instead of passing.

**Why it matters.** No production behaviour change. The teardown contract that prevents a use-after-free inside libssh is now covered by the live SSH gate (ctest label `live`, environment variable CH_LIVE_SSH), so re-ordering closeSession() or dropping the sessionClosing() connection fails a test instead of shipping.


*Closing note:* Files written: src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp (comment block only, inserted above the libssh include, recording the audited version floor) and src/ssh/tests/tst_livessh.cpp (one added test slot + its declaration). No other file touched; docs/DEVELOPMENT.md left alone as instructed. No build or test command was run.

For the lead / DocsFixups: the concrete documentation edit X5 asks for is to replace the libssh row's "0.10+, except 0.12.0" with "0.11.2+, except 0.12.0 (hard API floor 0.8.0)", citing ssh_get_server_publickey() as the symbol that sets the API floor, the 0.11.0 '+,-,^' algorithm-list modifier as what the 0.12.0 workaround needs, and CVE-2025-5351 (fixed 0.11.2, affects the ssh_pki_export_pubkey_base64() call in verifyHostKey()) plus CVE-2023-6004 (fixed 0.10.6, affects ~/.ssh/config ProxyCommand parsing) as why the practical floor sits above the API floor. If you would rather not raise the documented floor, the strongest statement that is still defensible from the source alone is: "builds against libssh 0.8.0 and newer; 0.12.0 is unusable; releases are built and tested against 0.11.3."

One residual constraint I deliberately did not change, noted for completeness: the sessionClosing() -> closeChannel() notification is safe only while devices live in the same thread as the pool (a queued cross-thread delivery would arrive after the channels were freed). Nothing in the tree creates an SshChannelDevice off the main thread, and adding a blocking cross-thread handshake would be speculative complexity, so this stays an invariant of the design rather than a defect.

### Desktop client — JSON-RPC client — 1 item(s)

#### X7 — CodeharbordClient had no destructor, so pending request callbacks were silently dropped when the client was destroyed

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.h`, `src/remote/CodeharbordClient.cpp`, `src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp`

**What was wrong.** The class `ch::CodeharbordClient` (a JSON-RPC client over a QIODevice) documents above its `call()` declaration that "No callback is ever registered that cannot fire". Three paths honour that: a failed write in `call()`, replacing/detaching the transport in `setTransport()`, and the transport closing in `onTransportClosed()`. Each sweeps the `QHash<int, ResponseCallback> m_pending` via `failAllPending()` and invokes every waiting callback once with a synthetic error. I verified the claim by reading the whole class: no destructor was declared or defined, so destroying the client while requests were outstanding just destroyed the hash and those callbacks never ran.

**Why it was wrong.** Missing destructor. The exactly-once guarantee was enforced only on transport-lifecycle events, not on the client's own lifetime, so object destruction was an uncovered exit path out of the pending map.

**What was done.** Chose option (a) — added `~CodeharbordClient() override` and implemented it in CodeharbordClient.cpp, because every other path in this class deliberately refuses to leave a caller waiting, and weakening the header comment instead would have left real callers hanging. The destructor first sets `m_closed = true` and disconnects/clears `m_transport`; then, if `m_pending` is non-empty, builds an RpcError with code -32603 and message "client destroyed with request pending" and calls the existing `failAllPending()`. Ordering matters and is commented in place: latching closed first means a callback that reacts by re-entering `call()` takes the existing "transport closed" branch, which fails that retry synchronously and registers nothing (a new pending entry on a dying object could never fire); disconnecting stops a late readyRead()/disconnected() from reaching a half-destroyed slot; `failAllPending()` already moves the map out before iterating, so re-entry cannot see a half-cleared map; and nothing touches a member after the dispatch loop. Also extended the `call()` header comment to name destruction as one of the synthetic-error paths. Added test `TstRpcClient::destroyingClientFailsPendingCallbacks()` in src/remote/tests/tst_rpcclient.cpp: it issues one request with a callback that itself retries, plus one fire-and-forget request with a null callback, asserts pendingCount()==2, deletes the client, then asserts the first callback fired exactly once with code -32603 and the exact message, that the retry fired exactly once with an error (so it was rejected, not queued), and that the null callback was swept without tripping over the empty std::function.

**Why it matters.** Callers that allocate state released only by their callback, or that wait on the callback before completing an operation, no longer leak or hang when the client is torn down with requests in flight. Existing callers in src/persistence/WorkspaceDb.cpp and src/app/AppController.cpp already guard their callbacks with QPointer, so the newly-delivered late failure is safe for them.


*Closing note:* Only X7 was routed to me and only the three files above were written (all inside src/remote/, which I own). No build or test command was run, per instructions. Compile-safety checks done by inspection: QObject's destructor is virtual so `override` is valid; `kInternalError` and `failAllPending()` are both visible at the destructor's definition point (anonymous namespace constant at the top of the translation unit, private member declared in the header); the new test slot name was added to the `private slots:` list so moc generates its entry.

### Desktop client — data model — 1 item(s)

#### X8 — Nothing mechanically tied the C++ AgentState enumeration to the TypeScript AGENT_STATES list it must mirror

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/models/SessionState.h`, `src/models/SessionState.cpp`, `src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp`, `src/models/tests/CMakeLists.txt`

**What was wrong.** The C++ enumeration ch::AgentState in src/models/SessionState.h and the array AGENT_STATES in remote/src/events.ts have to hold the same agent-status names in the same order. The names matter because the remote service sends them as strings; the order matters because ch::AgentStatusMonitor::stateFor() returns the enumerator's integer position and its agentStateChanged signal carries that same integer into the QML user interface. Before this change the only thing linking the two files was a prose comment in each asking the reader to keep them in sync. Reordering the C++ enumeration, inserting a new entry in the middle, adding an entry to only one of the two files, or spelling a word differently would all compile cleanly and pass every test, while a terminal would then display the wrong agent status.

**Why it was wrong.** The synchronisation between the two files was documented but never checked. toString(AgentState) in src/models/SessionState.cpp is a switch, and the project does not compile with -Wall/-Werror (no add_compile_options in any CMakeLists), so even a missing switch case would not have produced an error - it would silently fall through to the trailing 'unknown' return. No test read the TypeScript file, so no test could observe a change made on the TypeScript side at all.

**What was done.** Two checks were added, one at compile time and one at run time. (1) src/models/SessionState.h now declares 'inline constexpr int kAgentStateCount = 8;' next to the enumeration plus a static_assert that AgentState::Unknown equals kAgentStateCount - 1. Inserting an enumerator anywhere before Unknown shifts Unknown's ordinal and fails to compile, with an assertion message listing every place that must be updated together. (2) A new Qt Test slot TstModels::agentStateWireWordsMatchRemoteEventsTs in src/models/tests/tst_models.cpp opens remote/src/events.ts, extracts the literal AGENT_STATES array with a regular expression, collects the quoted words in order, builds the C++ side by calling toString(static_cast<AgentState>(i)) for i in [0, kAgentStateCount), and compares the two string lists with QCOMPARE. It fails loudly (QVERIFY2 with the file name and error string) if the file cannot be opened, if the array literal is not found, or if the parsed list is empty - it never silently skips. src/models/tests/CMakeLists.txt gained a CH_REPO_ROOT compile definition set to ${CMAKE_SOURCE_DIR}, which is exactly how src/persistence/tests and src/remote/tests already reach repository files from a test. The existing toString(AgentState) output was left byte-for-byte unchanged, and the existing stateStringsArePinnedWireValues test still pins those literals; explanatory comments above the enumeration and above toString(AgentState) now point at the two checks instead of merely asking for care.

**Why it matters.** A divergence between the two languages is now caught by the build or by the test suite instead of by a user seeing a wrong status badge. The new test fails if either side is reordered, if an entry is added to one side only, or if a word is spelled differently, and it is the only check in the repository that can notice an edit made purely on the TypeScript side. No production behaviour changed.


*Closing note:* Regex sanity-checked offline against the real remote/src/events.ts (both patterns yield exactly starting, running, waiting_input, idle_unseen, idle, error, stopped, unknown). Caught and fixed one C++ hazard while writing it: the word-extraction pattern R"("([^"]*)")" would have terminated its raw string early at the ')"' sequence, so it uses the R"RX(...)RX" delimiter. The array pattern contains no double quote and is safe with the plain delimiter. Per instructions no build or test command was run; every edit was checked against the declarations it touches (kAgentStateCount is a new name, grep confirms no collision; QFile/QRegularExpression/QStringList includes were added at the top of the test file with the existing ones). Only files under src/models/ were written. Noted but not touched: the header comment in remote/src/events.ts already claims remote/test/events.test.ts parses src/agent/AgentEvent.h and fails on drift - that is the opposite direction and belongs to another agent; my test complements it by checking the C++ enumeration's order and count against the TypeScript list.

### Desktop client — terminal subsystem — 2 item(s)

#### X9 — Terminal panes are already told when the whole SSH session goes down; no factory-level pool observer needed

- **Kind:** Nit
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.h`

**What was wrong.** The routed claim was that ch::TerminalFactory never reacts to the SSH connection pool leaving its Connected state, so a pane could sit in 'Ready' or 'AttachingTmux' after the session died. I traced every teardown path and the claim does not hold: a pane always reaches the Disconnected state promptly, and the QML pane sees it.

**Why it was wrong.** There is no defect. Two independent paths already deliver the drop per pane. (1) Local teardown: SshConnectionPool::closeSession() (src/ssh/SshConnectionPool.cpp) emits sessionClosing() BEFORE freeing the session's channels; every SshChannelDevice connects that signal to its own closeChannel() in its constructor (src/ssh/SshChannelDevice.cpp line 34); closeChannel() emits readChannelFinished(); TerminalController::onTransportFinished() drains the last bytes and calls setState(Disconnected) for any pane in a live state. Because these are direct, same-thread connections, each pane is Disconnected before the pool even announces its own new state. Every path that leaves Connected (disconnectFromHost(), and each handshake failure that sets State::Error) goes through closeSession(). (2) Remote/network death with no local disconnect: the device's read pump gets SSH_ERROR or EOF from ssh_channel_read_nonblocking() and emits the same readChannelFinished(), reaching the same Disconnected transition. From there TerminalBridge re-emits the state as a string and src/qml/TerminalPaneView.qml's onConnectionStateChanged clears its 'attached' flag and shows the banner. A second, pool-level observer inside TerminalFactory could only race the first path, and being session-wide it would be less precise: it would re-report panes whose channel had already ended for their own reasons.

**What was done.** No behavioural change. Added a comment block to the class documentation of ch::TerminalFactory (src/terminal/TerminalFactory.h) recording why it deliberately does not subscribe to SshConnectionPool::stateChanged, naming the exact signal chain that already covers it, so the next reader does not add the redundant observer.

**Why it matters.** None on behaviour. The rationale is now written down where somebody would otherwise 'fix' a non-bug.


#### X10 — A remote terminal that produces no bytes left the pane stuck in the 'attaching_tmux' state forever

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/terminal/TerminalController.h`, `src/terminal/TerminalController.cpp`, `src/terminal/TerminalFactory.cpp`, `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalcontroller.cpp`, `src/terminal/tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp`, `src/terminal/tests/tst_liveterminal.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Confirmed reachable, and fixed. A terminal pane leaves the OpeningChannel/AttachingTmux states only when the remote pseudo-terminal produces its first bytes: TerminalFactory::attach() connects the channel's readyRead signal to a handler that then sets the Ready state. If the remote side produces no bytes at all AND never closes the channel, nothing moved the pane on, so it advertised 'attaching_tmux' for the rest of the application's life, with no way for the user to tell a slow attach from a dead one.

**Why it was wrong.** Most silent-failure cases do end: if the tmux command fails (missing tmux, unusable working directory) the remote command exits, the channel reaches end-of-stream, and TerminalController::onTransportFinished() moves the pane to Disconnected. The uncovered case is a channel that stays open and stays silent — a tmux server blocked on its socket, or the login shell that runs the attach command stalling inside a startup script on an unresponsive network mount. Neither bytes nor end-of-stream ever arrive, so neither of the two existing transitions can fire. The pane is not merely mislabelled: TerminalPaneView.qml computes its 'live' flag from connectionState === "ready", so it also keeps showing the not-live banner, and SessionsModel counts only Ready terminals as connected.

**What was done.** Added a bounded watchdog to the pane state machine itself, so every host that drives it is covered rather than only TerminalFactory::attach(). ch::TerminalController gains: a public constant kAttachTimeoutMs = 60000 (one minute), setAttachTimeoutMs(int)/attachTimeoutMs() where 0 means 'no bound' and negatives clamp to 0, a single-shot QTimer armed by setState() whenever the pane enters OpeningChannel or AttachingTmux and stopped on entering any other state, a new signal attachTimedOut(), and the private slot onAttachTimeout() which re-checks the state (so a queued timeout cannot hit a pane that just came up) and then moves the pane to the Error state. Both attaching states restart the window, so a slow channel open does not consume the budget tmux gets to draw. The reason has to reach the user, and TerminalFactory::error() is the only message channel the pane's chrome listens to (TerminalPaneView.qml binds factory.onError), so TerminalFactory::create() now connects attachTimedOut() once per controller and emits a sentence naming the seconds waited and the pane's tmux target. One minute was chosen deliberately: it is the same budget the existing live gates (tst_liveterminal, tst_liveterminalfactory, and src/app/tests/tst_coldstart, all kAttachTimeoutMs = 60000) already allow an attach before failing, so the watchdog cannot turn a currently-passing slow attach into a false failure; reporting a working pane as broken would be worse than the wait. Tests: tst_terminalcontroller gains silentAttachIsBoundedAndReportedAsAnError(), which pins the default window, the clamp, the stall (open, silent channel -> Error plus exactly one attachTimedOut), the absence of false positives (a pane that reaches Ready inside the window never errors), that a real drop keeps Disconnected instead of being overwritten, that changing the window mid-attach takes effect for that pane, and that 0 disables the bound; tst_terminalfactory gains attachStallIsReportedAsAPaneMessage(), which pins that the stall produces exactly one error() carrying a non-empty message against the stalled controller only, and once more per retry (proving the handler is per controller, not per attach). tst_liveterminal now calls setAttachTimeoutMs(0) in initTestCase with a comment: that gate hand-drives the states and deliberately parks the pane in AttachingTmux for minutes while typing into it, which is precisely the shape the watchdog ends.

**Why it matters.** A wedged attach now fails visibly after a bounded wait with an explanatory message and the pane's existing Retry action, instead of showing 'attaching_tmux' indefinitely. Panes that come up normally are unaffected: the window is stopped by the same first-bytes transition that already existed, which in practice fires in well under a second.


*Closing note:* No build or test command was run, as instructed. Only files under src/terminal/ were written: TerminalController.h/.cpp, TerminalFactory.h/.cpp, tests/tst_terminalcontroller.cpp, tests/tst_terminalfactory.cpp, tests/tst_liveterminal.cpp. No CMakeLists change was needed (no new files). Compile-safety checks done by hand: the new declarations match their definitions; qMax and QTimer are already available through the existing includes; the new factory lambda uses only QString/TerminalController APIs already included by TerminalFactory.h; the new tests use only QtTest/QSignalSpy facilities the neighbouring cases already use. Cross-slice note for the lead, no action taken by me: SshConnectionPool has no keepalive-based drop detection, so after a network-level session death the pool can still report Connected and TerminalFactory::connected() can still return true; an attach() then fails through the normal channel-error path, so this is a diagnostics wart rather than a fault, and it is outside src/terminal.

### Desktop client — editor subsystem — 3 item(s)

#### X11 — Editor read requests had no byte ceiling and a truncated reply was still presented as a normal, clean open

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.h`, `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Confirmed. `EditorController::open()`, `EditorController::reload()` and the crash-recovery snapshot read in `EditorController::checkRecovery()` all called the remote `file.readFile` method with only a `path` (helper `readParams()`), so the Node service took its un-ranged branch and loaded the entire file into memory before sending it; the client then held it again and the Monaco page a third time as one JavaScript string. Wave one had already made a reply with `truncated: true` mark the buffer unsaveable (`m_bufferReadOnly`), but the pane still reported the file lifecycle state as `Clean`, i.e. "the buffer is exactly what the server has", which for a prefix is false.

**Why it was wrong.** `remote/src/files.ts` `readFile()` reads the whole file when neither `offset` nor `length` is supplied, and it derives its `truncated` flag from the requested `length` — so an unbounded read can never even report that the file was too big. The editor was the only remaining caller that read unbounded; the inline viewer (`src/viewers/InternalUrlSchemeHandler.cpp`) already passed `offset: 0, length: 8 MiB`.

**What was done.** Added `EditorController::kMaxEditableReadBytes = 8 * 1024 * 1024` (public static constant in `src/editor/EditorController.h`) and a new `readFileParams()` helper in `src/editor/EditorController.cpp` that always sends `offset: 0` plus that length. All three `file.readFile` call sites (open, reload, recovery-snapshot read) now use it. 8 MiB is deliberately the same value as `InternalUrlSchemeHandler::kMaxInlineReadBytes` so no file is editable-but-unviewable or the reverse; the value is restated rather than shared because the `ch_editor` static library does not link `ch_viewers`. When the reply is marked truncated, the file now settles in `FileState::ReadOnly` instead of `FileState::Clean` (in both `open()` and `reload()`), which is a state no save can be issued from — the existing `m_readOnly` guard in `save()` already refuses the write, and the recovery-snapshot probe is already skipped for a read-only buffer. `FileState::Error` was rejected because the read genuinely succeeded and Error would leave the pane blank instead of showing the head of a large log. A truncated recovery snapshot is likewise never offered to the user. `FileState::ReadOnly` was added to the set of states remembered across a transport drop (`onTransportClosed`), so a reconnect cannot launder a truncated file back to "clean".

**Why it matters.** Opening a multi-gigabyte file no longer makes the remote service read it whole into memory, and a file above the ceiling can no longer be shown as an ordinary editable buffer whose save would delete every byte past the prefix.


#### X12 — Read-only detection cannot see the real permissions of a file opened through a symbolic link

- **Kind:** Footgun
- **Outcome:** Not fixed (explained)
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Confirmed, and DELIBERATELY KEPT. `statSaysUnwritable()` decides read-only-ness from the `mode` field of the remote `file.stat` reply, which `remote/src/files.ts` produces with `lstat`. A symbolic link's own permission bits are always 0777 on Linux, so for a file opened through a link the check can never claim read-only, no matter how unwritable the target is.

**Why it was wrong.** `mode` comes from `lstat` (the link), while `readFile`/`writeFile` follow the link (the target). The reply carries nothing else that helps: there is no uid/gid, `size` for a symlink is the length of the target path string, and `revision` is minted from the target's mtime/ctime/inode/size — none of which say anything about permission. The client also does not know the remote process's effective user, so a set write bit never proves writability either.

**What was done.** Kept the existing trade and rewrote the comment above `statSaysUnwritable()` to state the consequence explicitly and accurately: the user can type into a file they cannot write and only finds out when the save fails, surfaced as the server's EACCES through the `saveError` signal. The comment now also records why the alternative was rejected — treating a reported `kind: "symlink"` as read-only would produce a false read-only for the common case (dotfile farms, checked-out worktrees), taking a file away from a user who could have edited it, which is worse than a late failure. No behaviour was changed; the server was not touched.

**Why it matters.** No behaviour change. The accepted limitation is now documented at the exact place a future reader would otherwise mistake it for an oversight.


#### X13 — A file deleted on the server while open: baseline is correct to keep, but a deletion during a dropped session was silently ignored

- **Kind:** Latent bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `src/editor/EditorController.cpp`, `src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp`

**What was wrong.** Two separate questions. (a) The stale revision baseline is CORRECT and was left alone. When a live watch reports the file deleted, `onNotification()` re-reads it, the read fails, and the pane lands in `FileState::Error`; `m_revision` keeps the baseline of the file that was loaded. That is what makes the user's next save a guarded write the server refuses rather than an unguarded create that silently resurrects the file. No wrong outcome is reachable: the server's revision token embeds the inode (`revisionFrom()` in `remote/src/files.ts` is mtime-ctime-inode-size), so a different file that took the path can never match the baseline by accident, and the create-only retry behind the page's "overwrite" affordance is refused too if something else now holds the path. (b) A real gap did exist on the reconnect path: `reconcileAfterReconnect()` returned silently whenever its `file.stat` failed, so a file deleted while the SSH session was down — the one case that produces no watch event anywhere — left the pane advertising the pre-outage state, i.e. claiming "clean" for a file that no longer exists, and the user's next save was then refused as a revision conflict for a revision they never saw change.

**Why it was wrong.** `reconcileAfterReconnect()` treated any stat failure as "nothing to claim". But that request was answered by a transport that had just been bound, so a failure means the path itself is the problem (deleted, or its directory no longer traversable), not the connection.

**What was done.** In `reconcileAfterReconnect()` the stat-failure case is now handled instead of ignored: a save still in flight still wins; a dirty buffer is only FLAGGED as `FileState::ExternallyModified` and never re-read, so unsaved work is never thrown away; a clean buffer is re-read via `reload(FileState::ExternallyModified)`, which either proves the stat failure was transient (the buffer re-baselines and settles Clean) or fails too and lands the pane in `FileState::Error` — the same place a live "deleted" watch event leaves it. The revision baseline is explicitly left untouched on every one of those paths, and the reason is now spelled out in comments at both `reconcileAfterReconnect()` and `onNotification()`.

**Why it matters.** After a reconnect over a file that was deleted while the session was down, the pane now reports Error (clean buffer) or Externally-Modified (unsaved work) instead of falsely reporting a clean, saveable buffer. Recreating a deliberately deleted file remains an explicit user action.


*Closing note:* Tests added to src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp (four new cases, declared in the private-slots list and defined before QTEST_GUILESS_MAIN), plus two new request-inspection helpers reqOffset()/reqLength() in the file's anonymous namespace:
- readsAskForABoundedWindow — the first load AND the reload both send offset 0 and length == EditorController::kMaxEditableReadBytes.
- aTruncatedFileIsNotOpenedForEditing — a truncated reply yields fileState "read_only" and readOnly true, issues no recovery probe, and is not laundered back to "clean" by a transport drop and rebind.
- deletedWhileDisconnectedStopsClaimingClean — reconnect, stat fails with ENOENT, the controller re-reads, the read fails, fileState becomes "error", the revision baseline is still r1, and the following save goes out GUARDED with expectedRevision "r1" and is refused (conflict), never as a create-only resurrect.
- deletedWhileDisconnectedWithUnsavedWorkIsOnlyFlagged — same vanished file with a dirty buffer: fileState "externally_modified", no read issued, no content delivered, baseline unchanged.

No build or test command was run, per instruction. Compile-safety checks done by reading: kMaxEditableReadBytes is declared public in EditorController.h before the anonymous-namespace helper that uses it; FileState::ReadOnly exists in src/models/SessionState.h and toString() maps it to "read_only"; the Monaco page only renders the fileState string (src/web/editor/src/index.ts), so an unfamiliar value cannot break it; QCOMPARE arguments in the new tests are type-matched to qint64.

Existing expectations I checked for regressions: the pre-existing truncatedReadIsNotWritableBack case does not assert fileState, so the new ReadOnly state does not break it; every other unit test responds truncated=false and is unaffected by the added offset/length params (no test asserted the readFile params before). The live tests (tst_liveeditor, tst_liveeditorreconnect) read small real files, and remote/src/files.ts computes truncated as start > 0 || end < size, which is false for offset 0 with an 8 MiB length on a small file, so their "clean" expectations still hold.

Files written: src/editor/EditorController.h, src/editor/EditorController.cpp, src/editor/tests/tst_editorcontroller.cpp. src/editor/CMakeLists.txt and src/editor/tests/CMakeLists.txt needed no change (no new source files). Nothing outside src/editor/ was touched.

### Remote service — 5 item(s)

#### X14 — readFile reported truncated=false for reads that did not return the whole file

- **Kind:** Active bug
- **Outcome:** Fixed
- **Files:** `remote/src/files.ts`, `remote/src/rpc-types.ts`, `remote/test/files.test.ts`

**What was wrong.** The remote file service function readFile (remote/src/files.ts) returns a boolean flag named `truncated` alongside the file content. The flag was only ever set inside the branch that handles a byte-range read, and only when the caller supplied a `length`. So a caller that passed only an `offset` received the tail of the file with truncated=false, and a caller whose `offset` was at or past the end of a non-empty file received empty content, also with truncated=false. The claim was verified by reading the code and by the pre-existing test that asserted `truncated === false` for a read at offset 1000 of a 10-byte file.

**Why it was wrong.** The flag was computed as a side effect of one branch of the read logic (`if (wanted !== undefined) truncated = offset + wanted < size;`) instead of being derived once from the window that was actually returned. Two of the four possible return paths never assigned it, so it kept its `false` initializer.

**What was done.** Chose and wrote down a single definition: `truncated` is true whenever any byte of the file is absent from the returned content, whether the missing bytes are before the window (any offset past byte 0) or after it. It is now computed once, after the read, from the clamped window [start, end) against the file size: `start = min(offset, size)`, `end = wanted === undefined ? size : min(start + wanted, size)`, `truncated = start > 0 || end < size`. That single expression covers every return path, including the whole-file read (0 and size, so false) and an offset past the end of an empty file (nothing is missing, so false).
